outcome.

3.4.3.1
Empirical Analysis

Though the theoretical guarantee of our algorithm
Ordered-Relax
is limited in terms of approximation ratios and the spaces of instances it covers, empirically it is found to exhibit remarkable performance to give
exact optimal solutions
on all the PB datasets of real-world PB elections available online
[
84
]
. The datasets and corresponding results are included in detail at
https://github.com/Participatory-Budgeting/maxmin_2022
. Some of these election datasets are selected arbitrarily and the corresponding results are displayed in
Table

3.1
. This indicates that, although theoretically the worst cases do not allow the approximation ratio to get better, such worst cases are seldom encountered in real-life, thus explaining the excellent performance of our algorithm.

3.4.4
Bound on the Performance of Exhaustive and Strategy-Proof PB Algorithms

In many cases, real-world voters tend to be strategic and self-interested. Designing rules that are resistant to manipulations by strategic voters is thus a common objective in social choice literature. This property is formally referred to as strategy-proofness. Another property that is of interest in particularly PB settings is exhaustiveness, which ensures that the available budget is utilized without any wastage. In other words, the goal is to design PB rules that allocate the budget without under-utilization. It would be useful to know how the requirements of strategy-proofness and exhaustiveness will degrade the performance of PB algorithms with respect to
\mathsf{MPB}
objective.

We establish an upper bound on the approximation ratios of exhaustive strategy-proof PB algorithms by invoking an argument used in the mechanism design literature
[
70
,
28
]
. We construct a specific profile whose outcome can be derived using strategy-proofness and establish an upper bound on its approximation ratio.

Definition 3.4.26

(
Strategy-Proofness
)
.

An algorithm
\mathcal{G}
is said to be strategy-proof, if and only if, for any instance
\mathcal{I}
,

\displaystyle\forall i\quad\forall S\subseteq P\quad\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{i}\cap\mathcal{G}(A_{i},\mathcal{A}_{-i})\right)\geq\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{i}\cap\mathcal{G}(S,\mathcal{A}_{-i})\right)

where
\mathcal{A}_{-i}
denotes the dichotomous preference profile of all voters except
i
and
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A})
denotes the outcome of
\mathcal{G}
at profile
\mathcal{A}
.

That is, if all voters except
i
continue to approve the same sets of projects,
i
should not obtain a better utility by approving any set other than
A_{i}
. An algorithm is exhaustive if it is impossible to fund an unselected project with the remaining budget.

Definition 3.4.27

(
Exhaustiveness
)
.

An algorithm
\mathcal{G}
, is said to be exhaustive if and only if for every instance
\mathcal{I}
and every
p\notin\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{I})
, it holds that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)+\operatorname{c\!}\left(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{I})\right)>b
.

Theorem 3.4.28
.

No exhaustive strategy-proof algorithm can achieve an approximation guarantee better than
\frac{2}{3}
for
\mathsf{MPB}
even for instances with only knapsack votes and unit cost projects.

Proof 3.4.29
.

Consider an exhaustive strategy-proof algorithm
\mathcal{G}
. Consider an instance
\mathcal{I}_{1}
with a budget
b
,
2b
projects each costing
1
, two voters, and a dichotomous preference profile
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\{A_{1},A_{2}\}
such that
A_{1}\cap A_{2}=\emptyset
and
\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\right)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{2}\right)=b
. Let
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{I}_{1})=S
. Without loss of generality, assume that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\cap S\right)\leq\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{2}\cap S\right)
. Modify the dichotomous preference profile of
\mathcal{I}_{1}
to get
\mathcal{I}_{2}
as follows:
\mathcal{A}_{2}=\{A_{1},S\}
. For
\mathcal{I}_{2}
,
\mathcal{G}
should output
S
. Else, voter
2
would have the incentive to misreport
A_{j}
instead of the true vote
S
and get the preferred outcome
S
. Hence,
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{I}_{2})=S
. Therefore, the minimum utility of the algorithm for
\mathcal{I}_{2}
,
ALG(\mathcal{I}_{2})
, is
\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\cap S\right)
. Let
\emph{OPT}(\mathcal{I}_{2})
be the optimal minimum utility for
\mathcal{I}_{2}
. That is,

\displaystyle\emph{OPT}(\mathcal{I}_{2})\geq\min{(\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\cap X\right),\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\cap X\right))}\quad\forall X\in\mathcal{F}

(3.14)

Since
\mathcal{G}
is exhaustive,
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)=b
. Consider a set
X\subseteq P
such that
(A_{1}\cap S)\subseteq X
,
|X\cap(A_{1}\setminus S)|=\frac{b-\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\cap S\right)}{2}
, and
|X\cap(S\setminus A_{1})|=\frac{b-\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\cap S\right)}{2}
. Clearly,
X\in\mathcal{F}
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\cap X\right)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\cap X\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\cap S\right)+\frac{b-\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\cap S\right)}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{b+\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\cap S\right)}{2}

(3.15)

Since
\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\cap S\right)\leq\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{2}\cap S\right)
and
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)=b
,
\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\cap S\right)\leq\frac{b}{2}
. Substituting
b\geq 2\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\cap S\right)
in
Equation

3.15
, we get,

\displaystyle\emph{OPT}(\mathcal{I}_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{3\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{1}\cap S\right)}{2}

(From
Equation

3.14
)

\displaystyle\frac{ALG(\mathcal{I}_{2})}{\emph{OPT}(\mathcal{I}_{2})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{3}

Hence, the approximation guarantee of the algorithm is at most
\frac{2}{3}
. Clearly in both the instances
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
, all the votes are knapsack votes and the theorem follows.

3.5
Axiomatic Results

An axiomatic study of any voting rule provides valuable insights into the characteristics of the voting rule. We now undertake an axiomatic study of the
\mathsf{MPB}
rule by exploring several axiomatic properties available in the literature.
Given an instance
\mathcal{I}
and a PB rule
R
, we say a project
p
wins if there exists
S\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
such that
p\in S
. Let
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
represent the set of all projects which win, i.e.,
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)=\{p\in P:\exists S\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)p\in S\}
. Throughout this section, we represent the
\mathsf{MPB}
rule by
\boldsymbol{M}
.

First, we examine axioms in the PB literature for rules that output a single feasible subset of projects
[
85
]
. We extend these axioms to irresolute rules (like
\mathsf{MPB}
) that output multiple feasible subsets. We start by defining all the axioms.

The first axiom requires that, if a winning project is replaced by a set of projects whose total cost is the same, then at least one project of this set should continue to win. In other words, take any winning project
p\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
. Split
p
into a set
P^{\prime}
of smaller projects such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(P^{\prime}\right)
. In every preference
A_{i}
such that
p\in A_{i}
, replace
p
with all the projects in
P^{\prime}
. Let the new resultant instance be
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Splitting monotonicity requires that at least one project from
P^{\prime}
must belong to
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

Definition 3.5.30

(Splitting Monotonicity
[
85
]
)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to satisfy splitting monotonicity iff, for any instance
\mathcal{I}
,
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)\cap P^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
whenever
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by splitting a project
p\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
into a set of projects
P^{\prime}
with
\operatorname{c\!}\left(P^{\prime}\right)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)
and changing every
A_{i}
having
p
to
(A_{i}\setminus\{p\})\cup P^{\prime}
.

The next axiom requires that, if we replace a set of winning projects that are approved by exactly same set of voters by a single project having the same total cost, then the single project wins. In other words, take a set
P^{\prime}
such that (i) it consists of only winning projects, i.e.,
P^{\prime}\subseteq\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
and (ii) every voter either approves all the projects from
P^{\prime}
or approves none of them, i.e.,
A_{i}\cap P^{\prime}\in\{P^{\prime},\emptyset\}
for every
i
. Merge all the projects in
P^{\prime}
to get a new project
p
such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(P^{\prime}\right)
. In every
A_{i}
such that
P^{\prime}\subseteq A_{i}
, replace
P^{\prime}
with the project
p
. Let the new resultant instance be
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Merging monotonicity requires that
p\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

Definition 3.5.31

(Merging Monotonicity
[
85
]
)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to satisfy merging monotonicity iff, for any instance
\mathcal{I}
,
p\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
whenever
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}
as follows: for any
S\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
and
P^{\prime}\subseteq\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
such that
A_{i}\cap P^{\prime}\in\{P^{\prime},\emptyset\}
for every voter
i
, merge the projects in
P^{\prime}
into a single project
p
with cost
\operatorname{c\!}\left(P^{\prime}\right)
and change every
A_{i}
with
P^{\prime}
to
(A_{i}\setminus P^{\prime})\cup\{p\}
.

The next axiom requires that no winning project should be dropped if it becomes less expensive.

Definition 3.5.32

(Discount Monotonicity
[
85
]
)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to satisfy discount monotonicity iff, for any instance
\mathcal{I}
,
p\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
whenever
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by reducing the cost of
p\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
to
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)-1
.

The following axiom requires that no winning project should be dropped if the budget increases. Note that we insist no project to cost exactly
b+1
since that leads to a trivial result of selecting that project, thereby not capturing the essence of axiom.

Definition 3.5.33

(Limit Monotonicity
[
85
]
)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to satisfy limit monotonicity iff, for any instance
\mathcal{I}
such that no project in
P
costs
b+1
,
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)\subseteq\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
whenever
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by increasing the budget to
b+1
.

The next axiom requires that all sets in
\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
are maximal.

Definition 3.5.34

(Strong Exhaustiveness
[
5
]
)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to satisfy strong exhaustiveness iff, for any instance
\mathcal{I}
,

\forall S\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)\;\forall p\in P\setminus S\quad\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)+\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)>b

Next, we introduce a new axiom, weak exhaustiveness, which requires that any winning non-maximal set can be made maximal without compromising on the win. In other words, suppose
S
is a winning set of projects (
S\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
). If addition of another project
p
to
S
does not make
S
infeasible, then adding
p
must not also affect its winning status. That is,
S\cup\{p\}
must also be a part of
\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
.

Definition 3.5.35

(Weak Exhaustiveness)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to satisfy weak exhaustiveness iff, for any instance
\mathcal{I}
,

\forall S\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right),\;\forall p\in P\setminus S,\;\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)+\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)\leq b\implies S\cup\{p\}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)

Theorem 3.5.36
.

The
\mathsf{MPB}
rule satisfies: (a) splitting monotonicity (b) merging monotonicity (c) weak exhaustiveness.

Proof 3.5.37
.

(a)

Splitting Monotonicity:

Let
p\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(M,\mathcal{I}\right)
be split into a set of projects
P^{\prime}
as above to produce
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. For the sake of contradiction, let us assume that
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(M,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)\cap P^{\prime}=\emptyset
. Since
p\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(M,\mathcal{I}\right)
, there exists
S\in\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
such that
p\in S
. Consider a set of projects
K=(S\setminus\{p\})\cup P^{\prime}
. Since
\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{i}\cap K\right)
and
\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{i}\cap S\right)
are same for every voter
i
, the minimum utility from
K
is equal to that from
S
. From our assumption, for any set
T\in\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
,
T\cap P^{\prime}=\emptyset
and the minimum utility from
T
is strictly greater than that from
K
and
S
. This contradicts the fact that
S\in\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
.

(b)

Merging Monotonicity:

Let
S\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
and
P^{\prime}\subseteq S
satisfy the condition specified. That is, each voter either approves entire
P^{\prime}
or approves no project in
P^{\prime}
. Let
P^{\prime}
be merged into a single project
p
and the new instance thus produced be
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. For the sake of contradiction, let us assume that
p\notin\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
. Consider the set
K=(S\setminus P^{\prime})\cup\{p\}
. Since
\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{i}\cap K\right)
and
\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{i}\cap S\right)
are same for every voter
i
, the minimum utility from
K
is equal to that from
S
. From our assumption, for any set
T\in\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
,
p\notin T
and the minimum utility from
T
is strictly greater than that from
K
and
S
. This contradicts
S\in\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
.

(c)

Weak Exhaustiveness:

For the sake of contradiction, let us assume that
\exists S\in\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
and
p\in P\setminus S
such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)+\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)\leq b
. Consider the feasible set
K=S\cup\{p\}
. Consider any arbitrary voter
i
. If
p\in A_{i}
,
\operatorname{u_{i}\!}\left(K\right)=\operatorname{u_{i}\!}\left(S\right)+\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)
. Else if
p\notin A_{i}
,
\operatorname{u_{i}\!}\left(K\right)=\operatorname{u_{i}\!}\left(S\right)
. So, the minimum utility of any voter from
K
is at least that from
S
. Since
S\in\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
,
K\in\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
.

This completes the proof.

Theorem 3.5.38
.

The
\mathsf{MPB}
rule does not satisfy: (a) discount monotonicity (b) limit monotonicity (c) strong exhaustiveness.

Proof 3.5.39
.

(a)

Discount Monotonicity:

Consider an instance
\mathcal{I}
with
P=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}
each costing
4
, a budget
12
, and three voters with
A_{1}=\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
,
A_{2}=\{p_{3}\}
, and
A_{3}=\{p_{4}\}
. It can be seen that
p_{2}\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(M,\mathcal{I}\right)
since
\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)=\{\{p_{1},p_{3},p_{4}\},\{p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}\}
. Now consider the case where
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{2}\right)
is reduced to
3
to get new instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Then,
\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)=\{\{p_{1},p_{3},p_{4}\}\}
and hence
p_{2}\notin\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(M,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

(b)

Limit Monotonicity:

Consider an instance
\mathcal{I}
with
P=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6}\}
costing
\{3,1,3,3,3,6\}
respectively, a budget
12
, and four voters with
A_{1}=\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
,
A_{2}=\{p_{3},p_{4}\}
,
A_{3}=\{p_{5}\}
, and
A_{4}=\{p_{6}\}
. Clearly,
\{p_{1},p_{3},p_{5}\}\in\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
. Therefore,
p_{1}\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(M,\mathcal{I}\right)
. If
b
is increased to
13
to get
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,
\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)=\{\{p_{2},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6}\},\{p_{2},p_{3},p_{5},p_{6}\}\}
and
p_{1}\notin\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(M,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

(c)

Strong Exhaustiveness:

The above example also illustrates the counter example for strong exhaustiveness. Clearly,
S=\{p_{1},p_{3},p_{5}\}\in\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
but
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{2}\right)+\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)<12
.

This completes the proof.

We now examine two important axioms from multi-winner voting literature. The first axiom is an analogue of unanimity, which requires that a project approved by all voters needs to win.

Definition 3.5.40

(Narrow-Top Criterion
[
40
]
)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to satisfy narrow-top criterion iff, for any instance
\mathcal{I}
,
p\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
whenever
p\in A_{i}
for every
i\in N
.

Proposition 3.5.41
.

The
\mathsf{MPB}
rule does not satisfy narrow-top criterion.

Proof 3.5.42
.

Our example uses the fact that the utility from an unanimously approved project could be low and selecting it could make the other projects unaffordable. Consider an instance
\mathcal{I}
with
P=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
costing
\{1,3,3\}
respectively, a budget
6
, and two voters with
A_{1}=\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
and
A_{2}=\{p_{1},p_{3}\}
. Since
\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)=\{\{p_{2},p_{3}\}\}
, we have
p_{1}\notin\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(M,\mathcal{I}\right)
though
p_{1}\in A_{i}
for all
i
.

The next axiom tries to capture diversity in the voting rules
[
7
,
25
]
.

Definition 3.5.43

(Clone Independence
[
25
]
)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to satisfy clone independence iff, for any instance
\mathcal{I}
,
\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
does not change when a group of voters, all having exactly the same approval vote
A_{v}
, is replaced by a single voter with approval vote
A_{v}
.

Proposition 3.5.44
.

The
\mathsf{MPB}
rule satisfies clone independence.

The above proposition follows from the fact that redundant votes do not affect the value of maxmin objective. Clearly, this axiom represents diversity, but it is rather narrow. Faliszewski et al.
[
40
]
identified that the fairness notion of diversity does not have a clear axiomatic representation in the literature. We address this gap by introducing a new axiom, called
Maximal Coverage
, to capture diversity in PB as well as in multi-winner voting settings.

Let us define
covered voters
as the voters with at least one approved project funded and a
redundant project
as a project whose removal from an outcome does not change the set of covered voters. To achieve diversity, we need to cover as many voters as possible. Our new axiom ensures that a redundant project is funded only when no more voters can be covered by doing otherwise. For example, while allocating time for plenary talks, the organizers might want to explore as many novel ideas as possible, without eliminating any key area. They might allocate a time slot to a proposed plenary talk on a not-so-popular topic (hoping to prop up a new area), by dropping 1 out of 4 proposed talks in a popular area. This
covers
or reaches out to the audience from the new area and broadens the reach of conference.

Definition 3.5.45

(Maximal Coverage)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to satisfy maximal coverage iff, for any instance
\mathcal{I}
,
S\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
,
p\in S
such that
\{j_{1}:p\in A_{j_{1}}\}\subseteq\{j_{2}:(S\setminus\{p\})\cap A_{j_{2}}\neq\emptyset\}
, and
i\in N
,

\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)\cap A_{i}=\emptyset\implies\operatorname{c\!}\left(a\right)>b-\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\setminus\{p\}\right)\;\forall a\in A_{i}

(3.16)

We consider an example to understand this better. Suppose a budget of $2.25B is to be allocated to projects in two counties, X and Y, with populations of 10000 and 6000, resp. County X proposes projects
\{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}\}
costing
\{\$0.5B,\$1B,\$1B\}
, and County Y proposes projects
\{Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3}\}
costing
\{\$0.7B,\$0.7B,\$0.8B\}
resp. Assume each voter approves all and only the projects of her county. A rule that maximizes utilitarian welfare selects the set
\{\{X_{2},X_{3}\}\}
. Let
p
be
X_{2}
.
If
i=2
and
a=Y_{1}
, then the first part of
Equation

3.16
satisfies but
\operatorname{c\!}\left(Y_{1}\right)\leq b-\operatorname{c\!}\left(X_{3}\right)
. Hence it does not satisfy maximal coverage. Now, let us apply the
\mathsf{MPB}
rule to this example. That leads to
\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)=\{\{X_{1},X_{2},Y_{1}\},\{X_{1},X_{2},Y_{2}\},\{X_{1},X_{3},Y_{1}\},\{X_{1},X_{3},Y_{2}\}\}
and
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(M,\mathcal{I}\right)=\{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},Y_{1},Y_{2}\}
. It is notable that
Equation

3.16
holds.

Proposition 3.5.46
.

The
\mathsf{MPB}
rule satisfies maximal coverage.

Proof 3.5.47
.

Let
S,p,i,a
be as stated in
Definition

3.5.45
. Assume
\exists S
such that the first part of
Equation

3.16
holds. Since
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(M,\mathcal{I}\right)\cap A_{i}=\emptyset
,
\operatorname{u_{i}\!}\left(S\right)=0
. Hence the minimum utility from any set in
\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
is
0
. But, since
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(M,\mathcal{I}\right)\cap A_{i}=\emptyset
and
a\in A_{i}
,
\{a\}\notin\operatorname{M\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
. This is possible only if
\operatorname{c\!}\left(a\right)>b
and, thus, the second part of
Equation

3.16
holds.

3.6
Conclusion and Discussion

This chapter introduces the Maxmin Participatory Budgeting (
\mathsf{MPB}
) rule to optimize egalitarian welfare when the costs of projects are restricted to admit only one value. We conduct a comprehensive computational and axiomatic study of this rule.

On the computational front, we demonstrate that
\mathsf{MPB}
is strongly NP-hard. We look at two methods of coping up with this intractability: (i) identifying the tractable special cases and (ii) designing approximation algorithms. As a part of studying the first method, we show that
\mathsf{MPB}
is only weakly NP-hard when the number of distinct votes is small, and propose a pseudo-polynomial time algorithm for solving it. We also introduce a novel parameter called
scalable limit
and prove that when this parameter is also constant,
\mathsf{MPB}
can be solved in polynomial time. As a part of second method, we devise an LP-rounding algorithm
Ordered-Relax
that provides distinct additive approximation guarantees for several families of instances, and empirically demonstrate that our algorithm yields optimal outcomes when applied to real-world PB datasets. Furthermore, we establish an upper bound on the achievable approximation ratio for
\mathsf{MPB}
within the family of exhaustive and strategy-proof PB algorithms.

On the axiomatic front, we conduct a thorough analysis of
\mathsf{MPB}
and introduce a new fairness axiom called
maximal coverage
that aims to capture diversity in PB and multi-winner voting. We examine the compatibility of
\mathsf{MPB}
with all other desirable properties and prove that
\mathsf{MPB}
results in fair outcomes.

Going forward, an axiomatic characterization of
\mathsf{MPB}
is worth exploring. We studied two egalitarian objectives: maxmin and minimax (Appendix
.3.1
). Both these objectives suffer a common disadvantage: consider a case where the utility of the worst-off voter is
0
and it is impossible for any feasible subset of projects to give a non-zero utility to all the voters. In such a case, every feasible subset of voters is included in the outcome. To overcome this limitation of maxmin and minmax objectives, studying the egalitarian objective leximin is a promising direction. In the leximin objective, if two feasible subsets of projects give same utility to the worst-off voter, we pick the subset in which the utility of the second worst-off voter is higher, and so on until there is no tie between the sets of projects. This ensures that the ties between several sets of projects is dealt with most efficiency. From the axiomatic point of view, establishing connections between our new axiom maximal coverage and other existing axioms in the literature is an intriguing question to be pursued.

Throughout this chapter, we assumed that the cost of a project is restricted to only one value. In the next chapter, we study the model where the cost of each project is flexible and can take one of multiple possible values.

Chapter 4
Flexible Costs: Welfare Maximization when Projects have Multiple Degrees of Sophistication

In the realm of participatory budgeting, prior research on dichotomous preferences has largely focused on two scenarios: projects where costs are restricted to admit only one possible value or where costs are totally flexible permitting any amount to be allocated to each project. This chapter introduces a novel perspective by assuming that the costs are partially flexible. That is, we allow the projects to have a range of permissible costs, representing varying degrees of project sophistication. Each voter is allowed to express her preference by specifying upper and lower bounds on the cost she believes each project deserves.

Within this framework, the outcome of a PB rule becomes a selection of projects accompanied by their corresponding costs. For such a model, we propose four utility notions and study the corresponding rules maximizing utilitarian welfare. We showcase that the existing positive findings from the model featuring projects with restricted costs can be extended to our enriched framework, where projects can have multiple permissible costs. Moreover, we undertake an analysis of fixed parameter tractability of computationally hard rules in this model.

Further, we put forth a set of significant and intuitive axioms, aiming to capture essential aspects of multiple degrees of sophistication of projects. Through our analysis, we evaluate the extent to which the proposed PB rules satisfy these axioms, shedding light on the inherent properties and limitations of each rule.

4.1
Motivation

The cost of a project is the amount that needs to be allocated to the project in the event of it being selected for funding. As introduced in the
Section

1.1.2
, there are three possibilities with respect to the costs of the projects:

•

Restricted costs:
The cost of each project is restricted to a single value. For example, a dam construction project, if funded, may need a cost of exactly
\$10B
to be completed.

•

Flexible costs:
The amount allocated to each project is permitted to assume multiple values. This is further split into two possible scenarios:

–

Partially flexible:
A project can be implemented upto different degrees of sophistication and every degree corresponds to a different cost. The amount allocated to the project by the PB rule must belong to this set of permissible costs.

–

Totally flexible:
There is no restriction on the amount allocated to each project and any amount can be allocated to each project.

The existing work on participatory budgeting under dichotomous preferences assume that the costs of projects are totally restricted
[
85
,
43
,
51
,
13
,
73
,
54
,
45
,
16
,
57
,
8
,
43
,
57
,
38
,
60
,
26
]
or that they are totally flexible
[
21
,
32
,
10
]
. This chapter studies the case of costs being
partially flexible
. This is especially applicable in scenarios where there are multiple ways of executing a project. For example, a building can be built with wood or stone, depending on the amount allocated to it. Similarly, a health service project can build a primary healthcare center, a clinic, or a multi-speciality hospital. We call each such option of a project as a possible
degree of sophistication
of the project. Each of these options corresponds to a different cost.
Figure

1.5
is another illustration of such a model.

Figure
1.5
:
A toy example of an MNC renovation to demonstrate the applicability of PB model under partially flexible costs. Each project could be executed in multiple ways: a sit-out area could be built with multiple materials; a food center could be a coffee cafe, or a fast-food point, or a restaurant; and so on. Each possible option corresponds to a different cost
(figure repeated from page
1.5
).

The goal in PB under partially flexible costs is to select a subset of projects to be funded and also determine the amount to be allocated to each selected project.

4.2
Prior Relevant Work

From the perspective of flexibility of costs of the projects, several works in the literature study the case where costs are restricted to one value
[
85
,
43
,
51
,
13
,
73
,
54
,
45
,
16
,
57
,
8
,
43
,
57
,
38
,
60
,
26
]
or are totally flexible
[
21
,
32
,
10
]
. Alternately, this chapter studies the case where costs are partially flexible. Notably, a work by Patel et al.
[
65
]
on fairness for groups of projects can be viewed as our model: each degree of sophistication can be considered a separate project, all the degrees of same project are grouped, and a constraint that at most one project from each group can be funded is imposed. As a result, a particular result (
Theorem

4.6.8
) in this chapter can be derived from the results of Patel et al.
[
65
]
, while all the other computational and axiomatic results are novel contributions.

From the perspective of preferences of the voters, preference elicitation methods typically studied in any voting framework include dichotomous preferences, ordinal preferences, and cardinal preferences. These methods also continue to be the most studied preference elicitation approaches in PB
[
78
,
8
,
85
,
73
,
53
,
16
,
4
,
39
,
82
]
. However, PB is a setting with several attributes like costs associated with the projects. The preferences and utilities of the voters are thus much more complex. This propels the need to devise preference models specific to PB, as pointed out by Aziz and Shah
[
5
]
. One step in this direction is the introduction of a special case of dichotomous preferences, called knapsack votes, by Goel et al.
[
51
]
, where each voter reports her most favorite budget division. This idea however is criticised for its assumption that any project not in this division yields no utility to the voter.

We take another step in the direction of preference modeling for PB. We introduce the approach of
ranged dichotomous preferences
, which strictly generalizes dichotomous preferences. Each voter reports a lower bound and an upper bound on the cost that she thinks each project deserves. All the bounds are initially set to
0
by default. Voting proceeds in two steps. In the first step, voter starts by approving the projects she likes. For these approved projects, only the upper bounds automatically change to the highest permissible cost. In the second step, voter may
optionally
change bounds for some of these approved projects, if she wishes to have a say on the amount they deserve. Note that this is cognitively not much more demanding than the standard dichotomous preferences since we do not force voters to report the bounds.

Notably, all computational results in this chapter can also be extended to more general utility functions with minor tweaks. Nevertheless, we present the results for ranged dichotomous preferences due to their cognitive simplicity and natural relevance in the model.

It is worth mentioning that a work by Goel et al.
[
51
]
views divisible PB as a model of indivisible PB where every unit of money in the cost corresponds to one possible degree of sophistication, and proposes a greedy algorithm. Their work however imposes knapsack constraint on each vote and also assumes all lower bounds to be zero. Their model hence forms a very restricted special case of ours. The idea of having multiple permissible costs for each project was first proposed as a future direction in a survey by Aziz and Shah
[
5
]
, in which the authors called each of these costs a
degree of completion
and assumed an existence of a directed graph between the degrees. That is, a degree is assumed to be successor of a less expensive degree and more preferred than it by default. In our model, degrees of projects are independent of each other (a degree could correspond to constructing with wood, while other one corresponds to constructing with cement).

4.3
Contributions of the Chapter

This chapter systematically studies the PB model where each project has a set of permissible costs. Such a study has been conducted by Talmon and Faliszewski
[
85
]
for approval-based PB, which is a special case of our model with every project having only one permissible cost. We generalize the PB rules and all the positive results in
[
85
]
to our model. Namely, we propose polynomial-time, pseudo-polynomial time, and PTAS algorithms. Followed by this, we present results on parameter tractability using the parameter scalable limit introduced in
Chapter

3
and another novel parameter, variance coefficient, we introduce in the further sections. It needs to be highlighted that, as a part of our study, we also introduce and investigate novel utility notions that are specific to our model and cannot be extended from any existing notions for restricted costs model.

We further propose budgeting axioms for our model and examine their satisfiability with respect to our PB rules. All the axiomatic results are summarized in
Table

4.1
. Finally, we discuss the impact of our computational and axiomatic results and make key observations.

Organization of the chapter.

We start by introducing the formal model in
Section

4.4
. We define different utility notions (some of them are extended from the existing ones in the literature on restricted costs model; some are introduced by us explicitly for partially flexible costs model) and the corresponding PB rules in
Section

4.5
. In
Section

4.6
, we analyze the computational complexity of our PB rules and suggest ways to cope up with intractabilities. In
Section

4.7
, we define budgeting axioms for our model and examine their satisfiability.

4.4
Notations

Let us recall the necessary notations from
Section

2.1.1
and introduce a few more. A budget
b
is available for allocation. There are
n
voters
N=\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
m
projects in a set
P
. Each project
p_{j}\in P
has

t_{j}

possible degrees of sophistication captured by the set
\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{j}\right)=\{p_{j}^{0},p_{j}^{1},\ldots,p_{j}^{t_{j}}\}
. The cost of each degree
p_{j}^{t}

is indicated by
c_{j}^{t}

. We assume that
c_{j}^{0}

is zero for all
p_{j}\in P
and it corresponds to not funding the project
p_{j}
.

Each voter
i\in N
reports for every project
p
, a lower bound

l_{i}(j)

and an upper bound

h_{i}(j)

such that
l_{i}(j),h_{i}(j)\in\{c_{j}^{0},\ldots,c_{j}^{t_{j}}\}
and
l_{i}(j)\leq h_{i}(j)
. Let
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{H}
respectively denote the collection of all the lower bounds and upper bounds reported by all the voters.

Let
\mathcal{D}
denote the set of all the possible degrees of all projects, or in other words,
\mathcal{D}=\bigcup_{p_{j}\in P}{\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{j}\right)}
. We denote the cost of a set
S\subseteq\mathcal{D}
,
\sum_{p_{j}^{t}\in S}{c_{j}^{t}}
, by

\operatorname{c}\!\left(S\right)

. Given a subset
S\subseteq\mathcal{D}
, we use

S(j)

to denote the chosen degree(s) of project
p
in
S
. In other words,
S(j)=S\cap\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{j}\right)
. We use the shorthand notation

c^{S}(j)

to denote

\operatorname{c}\!\left(S(j)\right)

. We say a subset
S\subseteq\mathcal{D}
is
valid
if
\operatorname{c}\!\left(S\right)\leq b
and
|S(j)|=1
for every
p_{j}\in P
. Let
\mathcal{V}
denote the collection of all the valid subsets.

A PB instance with multiple degrees of sophistication is denoted by
\mathcal{I}=\langle N,\mathcal{D},\operatorname{c},b,\mathcal{L},\mathcal{H}\rangle
. Recall that in the PB model under flexible costs, PB rule outputs an allocation of costs to each project (
Definition

2.2
). That is, it outputs the corresponding allocated cost
x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{p_{j}}\cup\{0\}
for each project
p_{j}\in P
, where
\mathcal{X}_{p_{j}}
is the set of permissible costs of
p_{j}
. In our model with partially flexible costs,
\mathcal{X}_{p_{j}}=\{c_{j}^{1},\ldots,c_{j}^{t_{j}}\}
(
Section

2.1.1.1
). Thus, a PB rule is defined as follows:

Definition 4.4.1

(
PB Rule under Partially Flexible Costs
)
.

Given a PB instance
\mathcal{I}
with multiple degrees of sophistication, a PB rule
R
outputs a valid subset
S\in\mathcal{V}
, thereby indicating the amount allocated to each project.

4.5
The Utility Measure and PB Rules

The PB rules we study are rules maximizing utilitarian welfare. This implies that, given a function
u
that measures utility a voter derives from a subset of projects, the corresponding PB rule outputs a valid set of projects that maximizes the sum of utilities of all the voters, i.e., it outputs some
S\in\mathcal{V}
such that
\sum_{i\in N}{u_{i}\left(S\right)}
is maximized.
We say a subset
S
of projects is selected under a PB rule
R
if it maximizes the total utility of the voters.

Now, we only need to define different utility functions. Given the lower and upper bounds reported by the voters, we define the utility of a voter from a valid set
S\in\mathcal{V}
in four ways. For each of these utility functions, we also give a shorthand notation for the utilitarian welfare maximizing PB rule associated with it.

1.

Cardinal utility (
\boldsymbol{R_{|S|}}
rule)
: Each voter
i
derives a utility of
1
from a project
p
if the cost of the chosen degree falls within the bounds specified by the voter. Thus,
u_{i}\left(S\right)=|\{p_{j}\in P:l_{i}(j)\leq c^{S}(j)\leq h_{i}(j),c^{S}(j)\neq 0\}|
.

2.

Cost utility (
\boldsymbol{R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
rule)
: A voter
i
derives a utility of

c^{S}(j)

from a project
p
if its value falls within the bounds specified by the voter.

u_{i}\left(S\right)=\sum\limits_{p_{j}:l_{i}(j)\leq c^{S}(j)\leq h_{i}(j)}{c^{S}(j)}
.

3.

Cost capped utility (
\boldsymbol{R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}}
rule)
: Each voter
i
derives a utility of

c^{S}(j)

from a project
p
if the value falls within the bounds reported by her, a utility of

h_{i}(j)

if
c^{S}(j)>h_{i}(j)
, and a utility of
0
if
c^{S}(j)<l_{i}(j)
. That is, with slight abuse of notation,
u_{i}\left(S\right)=\sum_{p_{j}\in P}{u_{i}\left(S,j\right)}
, where

u_{i}\left(S,j\right)

is defined as:

\displaystyle u_{i}\left(S,j\right)=\begin{cases}0&c^{S}(j)<l_{i}(j)\\
c^{S}(j)&l_{i}(j)\leq c^{S}(j)\leq h_{i}(j)\\
h_{i}(j)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

4.

Distance disutility (
\boldsymbol{R_{\|\;\|}}
rule)
: From every project
p
, each voter derives a disutility of
0
if the value falls within the bounds reported by her, a disutility of
c^{S}(j)-h_{i}(j)
if
c^{S}(j)>h_{i}(j)
, and a disutility of
l_{i}(j)-c^{S}(j)
if
c^{S}(j)<l_{i}(j)
. That is, with slight abuse of notation,
d_{i}\left(S\right)=\sum_{p_{j}\in P}{d_{i}\left(S,j\right)}
, where

d_{i}\left(S,j\right)

is defined as follows:

\displaystyle d_{i}\left(S,j\right)=\begin{cases}l_{i}(j)-c^{S}(j)&c^{S}(j)<l_{i}(j)\\
0&l_{i}(j)\leq c^{S}(j)\leq h_{i}(j)\\
c^{S}(j)-h_{i}(j)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

The corresponding PB rule
\boldsymbol{R_{\|\;\|}}
minimizes the total disutility.

The first two utility notions are natural extensions of the existing notions
[
85
]
, which were also discussed in
Section

2.1.2.1
and
Chapter

3
. The former reflects that the voter is happy as long as the cost allocated to the project is acceptable to her, whereas the latter reflects that as the project gets more money, the voter gets happier if the cost is acceptable to her.

The second utility notion clearly assumes that if the project gets more money than what the voter thinks it deserves, voter derives zero utility. However, this need not always be the case in all applications. For example, suppose a voter will be happy to have an entertainment park in the neighborhood but feels that the park deserves any amount between 1000 and 5000 units. Now, if the park project is allocated 7000 units, the voter could still be happy that there is a park in the neighborhood but derive no more utility than 5000 (since it is the maximum value she thinks the park deserves). The third utility notion tackles such scenarios by modifying the second utility notion to cap the utility at

h_{i}(j)

instead of dropping it to
0
.

The first three utility notions assume that allocating any cost outside the range reported by the voter yields the same utility to her. However, in many situations, closer the cost of project is to the acceptable range of the voter, higher is the satisfaction voter derives from it. For example, suppose a voter feels that a certain project is worth at least 1000 units. An outcome that allocates 900 units to it is likely to be more preferred by the voter over something that allocates 50 units to it. The fourth rule handles such situations by considering the distance between the cost allocated and the closest acceptable cost as the
disutility
. Farther the cost is from the acceptable range, higher is the amount misspent.

4.6
Computational Results

Here, we analyze the computational complexity of our PB rules. We strengthen the existing positive results in the literature
[
85
]
and also present a few new results on fixed parameter tractability. All the exact algorithms we present are based on dynamic programming. The approximation schemes we present depend on both dynamic programming as well as a clever rounding scheme.

4.6.1
The Rule
{R_{|S|}}

Recall that
R_{|S|}
outputs a valid set that maximizes the sum of the utilities of voters, where the utility of a voter is defined as
u_{i}\left(S\right)=|\{p_{j}\in P:l_{i}(j)\leq c^{S}(j)\leq h_{i}(j),c^{S}(j)\neq 0\}|
. Talmon and Faliszewski
[
85
]
prove that for the case with restricted cost for each project, a subset maximizing the utility can be computed in polynomial time. We strengthen this result and prove that even when there are multiple permissible costs for each project, a subset maximizing the total utility can be computed in polynomial time. Note that the dynamic programming used in the next result will be referenced multiple times in the chapter.

Theorem 4.6.2
.

For any instance
\mathcal{I}
, a subset
S\in\mathcal{V}
that is selected under
R_{|S|}
can be computed in polynomial time.

Proof 4.6.3
.

We present an algorithm that uses dynamic programming. Let
\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)
denote the number of voters
i
such that
l_{i}(j)\leq c_{j}^{t}\leq h_{i}(j)
. Construct a dynamic programming table such that
A(x,y)
corresponds to the cost of cheapest valid subset of
\bigcup_{j=1}^{x}{\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{j}\right)}
such that the total score of projects in the set is exactly
y
.

Let
F(y)\subseteq\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{1}\right)
such that
\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{1}^{t}\right)=y
for every
p_{1}^{t}\in\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{1}\right)
. We compute the first row as:
A(1,y)=\min(c_{j}^{t}:p_{j}^{t}\in F(y))
. All the remaining rows are computed recursively as follows:
A(x,y)=\min\Big(A\big(x-1,y\big),\;\min_{t=1}^{t_{x}}{\big(A\big(x-1,y-\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{x}^{t}\right)\big)+c_{x}^{t}\big)}\Big)
.
In addition to computing this table, we store the sets corresponding to each entry in
A
in a separate table
B
as follows: if
A(x,y)
achieves the minimum value at
A(x-1,y)
, we copy
B(x-1,y)
to
B(x,y)
and append
p_{j}^{0}
. If
A(x,y)
achieves the minimum value at
A(x-1,y-\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{x}^{t}\right))+c_{x}^{t}
for some
t\in[1,t_{x}]
, we set
B(x,y)=B(x-1,y)\cup\{p_{x}^{t}\}
. Finally, we output the set at
B(x,y)
such that
A(x,y)\leq b
and
y
is maximized.

Correctness.

Recurrence ensures that for any entry in
B
, we select at most one project from

\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{j}\right)

for every
p_{j}
. While selecting the outcome from

B
, we ensured that its cost is within the budget. Hence, the output of the algorithm will be a valid subset. Optimality follows from the definition of
A
.

Running time.

Each row in
A
corresponds to making a decision about one project from
P
, and hence the number of rows is
m
. Each column corresponds to a possible total score. Since we select only one project from each

\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{j}\right)

and maximum score of any degree of a project is
n
by definition, maximum total score is
mn
. Thus, we have
mn
columns. Computing each entry in row
x
takes
O(t_{x})
time. Thus, running time is
\boldsymbol{O(m^{2}nt^{*})}
, where
t^{*}=\max_{p_{j}\in P}{t_{j}}
.

4.6.2
The Rule
{R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}}

Recall that
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
outputs a valid set that maximizes the sum of the utilities of voters, where the utility of a voter is:

u_{i}\left(S\right)=\sum\limits_{p_{j}:l_{i}(j)\leq c^{S}(j)\leq h_{i}(j)}{c^{S}(j)}.

Talmon and Faliszewski
[
85
]
prove that for the case where cost of each project is restricted to one permissible value, it is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard to determine if there exists a feasible subset that guarantees a total utility of at least a given value. Since their model can be modeled as a special case of ours, the hardness directly follows, as displayed in the next proof.

Proposition 4.6.4
.

For an instance
\mathcal{I}
and a value
s
, it is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard to check if
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
outputs a set with at least a total utility
s
.

Proof 4.6.5
.

We reduce from a known
\mathsf{NP}
-hard rule for PB under dichotomous preferences and restricted costs
[
85
]
. Given such a PB instance (where each project
p_{j}
has some cost
c(p_{j})
and every voter reports a subset
A_{i}
of projects she likes), we construct an instance for
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
as follows: For every project
p_{j}\in P
, create exactly two degrees such that
c_{j}^{0}=0
and
c_{j}^{1}=c(a)
. For each voter
i
, set
l_{i}(j)=0
for every project
p
and set
h_{i}(j)=c_{j}^{1}
if and only if
p\in A_{i}
. Clearly, both the instances are equivalent and we skip the proof of correctness.

To cope up with the intractability, Talmon and Faliszewski
[
85
]
prove that if cost of each project is restricted to one permissible value, the problem has a pseudo-polynomial time algorithm and FPTAS. We extend both these results to our model with multiple permissible costs.

Proposition 4.6.6
.

For any instance
\mathcal{I}
, a subset
S\in\mathcal{V}
selected under
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
can be computed in pseudo-polynomial time.

Proof 4.6.7
.

We multiply every
\operatorname{s}(P_{j}^{t})
in the proof of
Theorem

4.6.2
with
c_{j}^{t}

. DP tables are also constructed as explained in
Theorem

4.6.2
. The maximum score achievable by each
p_{j}^{t}

is
nc_{j}^{t}
. The total score is upper bounded by
n\operatorname{c}\!\left(S\right)
since at most a single project from each
\operatorname{D}
is chosen into each
B(x,y)
. This is bounded by
nb
since
S\in\mathcal{V}
. Thus, the table size is
O(mnb)
and the time is
\boldsymbol{O(mnt^{*}b)}
, where
t^{*}=\max_{p_{j}\in P}{t_{j}}
.

Theorem 4.6.8
.

There is an FPTAS to compute the outcome of
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
for any instance
\mathcal{I}
.

Proof 4.6.9
.

The idea is inspired from one of the existing FPTAS algorithm techniques of the knapsack problem
[
52
,
58
,
65
]
. We first round the scores of all the projects and then use the DP table explained in
Theorem

4.6.2
on the modified instance with rounded scores.

Given an instance
\mathcal{I}
, let
M
be the maximum score of a degree of project, i.e.,
M=\max_{p_{j}\in P,t\in[1,t_{j}]}{\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)}
. We can easily ensure that
M\leq OPT
by eliminating all the project degrees that cannot be part of any set with cost within
b
. Take any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Now, create new scores of all the projects as follows:
\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)=\left\lfloor\frac{s(P^{t}_{j})m}{\epsilon M}\right\rfloor
. We construct the DP tables similar to those in
Theorem

4.6.2
, but while considering new scores.
By the definition of

\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)

, for every
p_{j}\in P
and
t\in[1,t_{j}]
:

\displaystyle\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)m}{\epsilon M}

(4.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon M(\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)+1)}{m}

(4.2)

Let
S
be the outcome of this DP algorithm. Suppose
O
denotes the optimal solution for
\mathcal{I}
under
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
. Please note that the DP ensures that
S
is a valid set. Since
S
is the optimal solution for the modified scores,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{O(j)}\right)}\leq\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{S(j)}\right)}

(4.3)

Now, we prove that the approximation factor of
(1-\epsilon)
holds.

\displaystyle OPT=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{j}^{O(j)}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{\frac{\epsilon M(\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{O(j)}\right)+1)}{m}}

(From
Equation

4.2
)

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{\frac{\epsilon M\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{S(j)}\right)}{m}}+\epsilon M

(From
Equation

4.3
)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon M}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{\frac{\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{j}^{S(j)}\right)m}{\epsilon M}}+\epsilon M

(From
Equation

4.1
)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{j}^{S(j)}\right)}

\displaystyle\geq(1-\epsilon)OPT

(
\because M\leq OPT
)

Running Time.

The table has
m
rows. The modified score of each degree of project is upper bounded by
\frac{s(P^{t}_{j})m}{\epsilon M}
, which is further bounded by
\frac{m}{\epsilon}
due to the definition of
M
. Since the output is a valid set, it has at most
m
projects and the maximum possible total score is upper bounded by
\frac{m^{2}}{\epsilon}
, which is the number of columns. Computing each entry in row
x
takes
O(t_{x})
time. Thus, the running time is
\boldsymbol{O(\frac{m^{3}t^{*}}{\epsilon})}
, where
t^{*}=\max_{p_{j}\in P}{t_{j}}
.

4.6.2.1
Fixed Parameter Tractability

We introduced a new parameter called scalable limit in
Section

3.4.2.3
for PB under restricted costs and observed that this value is often small in PB elections in real life (e.g., datasets at https://pbstanford.org/ where budget and all the costs are multiples of some very high value). Motivated by this, we prove that
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
is in FPT with respect to scalable limit. Before that, we define the scalable limit in flexible costs model as follows:

Definition 4.6.10

(
Scalable Limit (
\boldsymbol{\delta}
)
)
.

For any instance
\mathcal{I}
, we refer to
\frac{\max_{p_{j}^{t}\in\mathcal{D}}{c_{j}^{t}}}{\tiny{GCD}(c_{1}^{1},\ldots,c_{m}^{t_{m}},b)}
as the
scalable limit
(
\delta
) of the instance.

Intuitively, we scale down all the costs and budget as much as possible, while ensuring that all these values continue to be integers. The scalable limit then refers to the cost of the costliest degree in
\mathcal{D}
in this scaled down instance.

Proposition 4.6.11
.

For any instance
\mathcal{I}
, computing a subset
S\in\mathcal{V}
that is selected under
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
is fixed parameter tractable parameterized by the scalable limit
\delta
.

Proof 4.6.12
.

We again prove this by constructing a DP table. Let
k=\frac{1}{\tiny{GCD}(c_{1}^{1},\ldots,c_{m}^{t_{m}},b)}
. First, we get a new instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
by scaling down all the costs and budget as follows:
c_{j}^{{}^{\prime}t}=kc_{j}^{t}
and
b^{\prime}=kb
. Then, we may construct the DP table similar to that in the proof of
Theorem

4.6.2
.

Running Time.

The table has
m
rows. The modified cost of each degree of project is upper bounded by
\delta
and hence the maximum score possible from each degree of a project is upperbounded by
n\delta
. Since the output is a valid set, there can be at most
m
projects in it and hence the maximum possible total score is upper bounded by
mn\delta
, which is the number of columns. Computing each entry in row
x
takes
O(t_{x})
time and the running time is
\boldsymbol{O(m^{2}n\delta t^{*})}
.

Proposition 4.6.13
.

For any instance
\mathcal{I}
, computing a subset
S\in\mathcal{V}
that is selected under
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
is fixed parameter tractable parameterized by the number of projects
m
.

The above proposition follows from the fact the total number of valid subsets is exponential in
m
(upper bounded by
(t^{*})^{m}
, where
t^{*}=\max_{p_{j}\in P}{t_{j}}
) and computing the total utility of any set under
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
can be done in polynomial time.

4.6.3
The Rule
{R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}}

This rule is similar to the previous rule
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
, with the only difference being the way a voter is assumed to view the projects allocated higher cost than her approved limit. Recall that the utility is defined as
u_{i}\left(S\right)=\sum_{p_{j}\in P}{u_{i}\left(S,j\right)}
, where

u_{i}\left(S,j\right)

is as follows:

\displaystyle u_{i}\left(S,j\right)=\begin{cases}0&c^{S}(j)<l_{i}(j)\\
c^{S}(j)&l_{i}(j)\leq c^{S}(j)\leq h_{i}(j)\\
h_{i}(j)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Proposition 4.6.14
.

Given an instance
\mathcal{I}
, the following statements hold:

1.

For any value
s
, it is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard to determine if
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
outputs a set that has a total utility of at least
s
.

2.

A subset
S\in\mathcal{V}
that is selected under
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
can be computed in pseudo-polynomial time.

3.

There is an FPTAS for
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
.

4.

Computing a subset
S\in\mathcal{V}
that is selected under
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
is fixed parameter tractable parameterized by the scalable
limit
\delta
.

5.

Computing a subset
S\in\mathcal{V}
that is selected under
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
is fixed parameter tractable parameterized by the number of projects
m
.

Proof 4.6.15
.

All the above statements follow from the proofs in
Section

4.6.2
with some minor changes. Statements (a) and (e) follow without any changes in the proof (
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
is the same as
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
when every project has only permissible cost).

For (b), we modify the definition of

\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)

as
\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)=\sum\limits_{i\in N}{u_{i}\left(S,j\right)}
, where
u_{i}\left(S,j\right)
is as defined in the
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
rule. The maximum possible total score of
S
is again
n\operatorname{c}\!\left(S\right)
(since
u_{i}\left(S,j\right)\leq c^{S}(j)
always) and the rest follows. For (c), we replace
\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)
in the proof of
Theorem

4.6.8
with the above value. Rest of the proof remains the same. The proof for (d) follows from
Proposition

4.6.11
since
u_{i}\left(S,j\right)
is upper-bounded by
c_{j}^{t}

for every
i\in N
and
p_{j}\in P
.

4.6.4
The Rule
{R_{\|\;\|}}

Recall that
R_{\|\;\|}
outputs the feasible set that minimizes the sum of disutilities of all the voters, where the disutility of voter
i
is defined as
d_{i}\left(S\right)=\sum_{p_{j}\in P}{d_{i}\left(S,j\right)}
, such that

d_{i}\left(S,j\right)

is as follows:

\displaystyle d_{i}\left(S,j\right)=\begin{cases}l_{i}(j)-c^{S}(j)&c^{S}(j)<l_{i}(j)\\
0&l_{i}(j)\leq c^{S}(j)\leq h_{i}(j)\\
c^{S}(j)-h_{i}(j)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

It is worth bearing in mind that the results from
Section

4.6.2
and
Section

4.6.3
cannot be transferred since we have a minimization problem and also because we cannot upper bound the disutilities by
b
. We start the section by proving that this rule too is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, using a slightly different but simple reduction from the same problem (used in
Proposition

4.6.4
) solved by Talmon and Faliszewski
[
85
]
. Then, we give a parameterized approximation algorithm, more specifically a parameterized FPTAS, which guarantees the approximation ratio when the parameter is small
[
42
]
.

Proposition 4.6.16
.

For an instance
\mathcal{I}
and a value
s
, it is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard to determine if
R_{\|\;\|}
outputs a set that has a total disutility of at most
s
.

Proof 4.6.17
.

For a PB instance under restricted costs (each project
p
has some cost
c(p)
and every voter reports a subset
A_{i}
of projects that she likes) and a positive value
x
, we construct an instance for
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
as follows: For every project
p_{j}\in P
, create exactly two degrees such that
c_{j}^{0}=0
and
c_{j}^{1}=c(a)
. For each voter
i
, set
h_{i}(j)=c(a)
for every project
p
and set
l_{i}(j)=c_{j}^{1}
if and only if
p\in A_{i}
. Let
Z=\sum_{i\in N}{\operatorname{c}\!\left(A_{i}\right)}
. Set
s=Z-x
.

Correctness.

To prove the forward direction, suppose the PB instance under restricted costs is a
Yes
instance. That implies, there exists a set
S
of projects such that
\sum_{i\in N}{\operatorname{c}\!\left(S\cap A_{i}\right)}\geq x
. Now calculate the disutility of
S
under
R_{\|\;\|}
. Then,
d_{i}\left(S,j\right)=c(a)
for every
j\in A_{i}\setminus S
and
0
otherwise. Therefore,
d_{i}\left(S\right)=-c(A_{i})-c(A_{i}\cap S)
. Then, total utility from
S
=
Z-\sum_{i\in N}{c(A_{i}\cap S)}
. Since the former is at least
x
, this total disutility is at most
Z-x
. To prove the backward direction, assume that the resultant instance of our problem is a
Yes
instance. We can construct a set
S
of projects such that a project
p_{j}
is included in
S
if and only if
p^{1}_{j}
is in the solution. Set
S
is a solution for PB under restricted costs, making it a
Yes
instance.

4.6.4.1
Parameterized Approximation Algorithm (FPTAS)

The very high disutilities that are hard to be bound motivate us to propose a
parameterized FPTAS
, which has been of great interest recently
[
42
]
. We consider a parameter that we call
variance coefficient
,
\gamma
, which, on being given an instance
\mathcal{I}
, intuitively shows how divergent the disutilities of different degrees of projects in the instance are. It is captured by measuring the highest disutility a single project can have, relative to sum of the least possible disutilities from all the projects. We explain this formally below.

Given an instance
\mathcal{I}
, for each degree of a project
p_{j}^{t}\in\mathcal{D}
, we use
q_{j}^{t}
to denote the disutility project
p_{j}^{t}

will contribute to any set that contains it. We call this
disutility contribution
of
p_{j}^{t}

. Suppose for example,
p_{j}^{t}\in S
. That implies,
c^{S}(j)=c_{j}^{t}
(
\because S
is valid). Therefore,

q_{j}^{t}=\sum_{i:c_{j}^{t}<l_{i}(j)}{(l_{i}(j)-c_{j}^{t})}+\sum_{i:h_{i}(j)<c_{j}^{t}}{(c_{j}^{t}-h_{i}(j))}.

It can be observed that,
\sum_{i\in N}d_{i}\left(S\right)=\sum_{p_{j}^{t}\in S}{q_{j}^{t}}
.

Let
q_{m}=\max_{p_{j}^{t}\in\mathcal{D}}{q_{j}^{t}}
. That is,
q_{m}
is the maximum of disutility contributions of all the degrees of all the projects. We use
q_{\sigma}
to denote the sum of minimum disutility contributions from each

\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{j}\right)

, i.e.,
q_{\sigma}=\sum_{p_{j}\in P}{\min_{t\in\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{j}\right)}{q_{j}^{t}}}
. Our parameter
\gamma
is the ratio
q_{m}/q_{\sigma}
. Intuitively, this parameter means that we want the disutility contribution from a single project not to be much higher than the sum of the least disutility contributions from all the
m
projects.

Theorem 4.6.18
.

For any instance
\mathcal{I}
and
\epsilon\in[0,1]
, a subset
S\in\mathcal{V}
such that
\sum_{i\in N}{d_{i}\left(S\right)}\leq(1+\epsilon)OPT
can be computed in
O(\frac{m^{3}\gamma t^{*}}{\epsilon})
time, where
OPT
is the optimal possible total disutility under
R_{\|\;\|}
,
\gamma
is the variance coefficient, and
t^{*}=\max_{p_{j}\in P}{t_{j}}
.

Proof 4.6.19
.

The idea is similar to that in
Theorem

4.6.8
. We define new disutilities for each project by rounding their disutility contributions. We then construct a DP table using which we select a set. For each project
p_{j}^{t}

, we define

\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)

as follows:

\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)=\left\lfloor{q_{j}^{t}m}/{\epsilon q_{\sigma}}\right\rfloor.

We construct the DP tables
A
and
B
similar to those in
Theorem

4.6.2
, with a slight change the each column now represents the total disutility, and

\operatorname{s}\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)

is replaced by

\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right)

defined in the above paragraph. We output the set at
B(x,y)
such that
A(x,y)\leq b
and
y
is
minimized
. Let
S
be the resultant outcome.
Suppose
O
denotes the optimal solution for
\mathcal{I}
under
R_{\|\;\|}
. Please note that the DP ensures that
S
is a valid set. Since
S
is the optimal solution with respect to new disutilities (i.e., w.r.t.
\alpha
’s),

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{S(j)}\right)}\leq\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{O(j)}\right)}

(4.4)

By the definition of
\alpha
, we have

\displaystyle q_{j}^{t}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\epsilon q_{\sigma}\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right){}}{m}

(4.5)

\displaystyle\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{t}\right){}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{q_{j}^{t}m}{\epsilon q_{\sigma}}-1

(4.6)

Now, we prove that the approximation factor of
(1+\epsilon)
holds.

\displaystyle OPT=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{q_{j}^{O(j)}}

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{\frac{\epsilon q_{\sigma}\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{O(j)}\right)}{m}}

(From
Equation

4.5
)

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{\frac{\epsilon q_{\sigma}\alpha\!\left(p_{j}^{S(j)}\right)}{m}}

(From
Equation

4.4
)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{\epsilon q_{\sigma}}{m}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{\frac{mq_{j}^{S(j)}}{\epsilon q_{\sigma}}}\right)-\epsilon q_{\sigma}

(From
Equation

4.6
)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=1}^{m}{q_{j}^{S(j)}}

\displaystyle\leq OPT+\epsilon q_{\sigma}

(4.7)

Note that
q_{\sigma}
is the sum of minimum possible disutility contribution from each

\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{j}\right)

. Any valid set should have one degree (degree
0
corresponds to not funding the project) from each

\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{j}\right)

. Since the optimal solution has to be a valid set, the optimal disutility must be at least
q_{\sigma}
, i.e.,
OPT\geq q_{\sigma}
. Applying this in
Equation

4.7
, results in
\sum_{j=1}^{m}{q_{j}^{S(j)}}\leq(1+\epsilon)OPT
.

Running Time

Now, we need to check the running time of constructing the DP table. The table has
m
rows. The disutilities calculated for each degree of project is upper bounded by
\frac{q_{j}^{t}m}{\epsilon q_{\sigma}}
, which is further bounded by
\frac{m\gamma}{\epsilon}
(by the definition of
\gamma
,
\because q_{j}^{t}\leq q_{m}
). Since the output is a valid set, there can be at most
m
projects in the output and hence the maximum possible total disutility is upper bounded by
\frac{m^{2}\gamma}{\epsilon}
, which is the number of columns. Computing each entry in row
x
takes
O(t_{x})
time. Thus, the running time is
\boldsymbol{O(\frac{m^{3}\gamma t^{*}}{\epsilon})}
, where
t^{*}=\max_{p_{j}\in P}{t_{j}}
.

Fixed Parameter Tractability

Here, we prove that
R_{\|\;\|}
is also in FPT with respect to the parameters
\delta
or
m
.

Proposition 4.6.20
.

For any instance
\mathcal{I}
, computing a subset
S\in\mathcal{V}
that is selected under
R_{\|\;\|}
is fixed parameter tractable parameterized by the scalable limit
\delta
.

Proof 4.6.21
.

The proof of the above proposition is same as the proof of
Proposition

4.6.11
. Let
k=\frac{1}{\tiny{GCD}(c_{1}^{1},\ldots,c_{m}^{t_{m}},b)}
. First, we get a new instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
by scaling down all the costs and budget as follows:
c_{j}^{{}^{\prime}t}=kc_{j}^{t}
and
b^{\prime}=kb
. Then, we may construct the DP table similar to that in the proof of
Theorem

4.6.2
. Running time is again
{O(m^{2}n\delta t^{*})}
.

Proposition 4.6.22
.

For any instance
\mathcal{I}
, computing a subset
S\in\mathcal{V}
that is selected under
R_{\|\;\|}
is fixed parameter tractable parameterized by the number of projects
m
.

The above result follows from the fact that when
m
is constant, we can exhaustively search all the valid sets for the one achieving optimal disutility.

4.7
Axiomatic Results

An enormous amount of work has been done on the axiomatic study of PB with approval votes
[
8
,
3
,
85
,
73
,
13
,
82
]
. However, our PB model in which each project has a set of permissible costs is unique technically as well as realistically. This uniqueness demands the development of novel axioms applicable explicitly in such a setting. We introduce different axioms and investigate which of the PB rules introduced in
Section

4.5
satisfy our axioms and which of them do not. We call
[l_{i}(j),h_{i}(j)]
to be the interval approved by voter
i
.

4.7.1
Monotonicity Properties

We start by introducing monotonicity properties, which capture how a change in one parameter related to the problem affects the outcome. The first axiom, shrink-resistance, implies that if the voters are narrowing down their interval towards a degree that was winning, then the degree still continues to win.

Definition 4.7.23

(Shrink-resistance)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to be
shrink-resistant
if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
, a voter
i
, a project
p_{j}\in P
, it holds that a set
S
selected under
R
continues to be selected even if

l_{i}(j)

and

h_{i}(j)

are shifted closer to

c^{S}(j)

.

Proposition 4.7.24
.

All the four rules
R_{|S|}
,
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
,
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
, and
R_{\|\;\|}
are shrink-resistant.

Proof 4.7.25
.

First, we look at
R_{|S|}
. Consider a set
S
selected under
R_{|S|}
. If initially
l_{i}(j)\leq c^{S}(j)\leq h_{i}(j)
, then the condition continues to satisfy even if

l_{i}(j)

and

h_{i}(j)

are shifted closer to

c^{S}(j)

. Thus,

u_{i}\left(S\right)

does not change. If initially
c^{S}(j)<l_{i}(j)
or
h_{i}(j)<c^{S}(j)
, then shifting

l_{i}(j)

and

h_{i}(j)

closer to

c^{S}(j)

may cause

u_{i}\left(S\right)

to remain the same or increase by
1
. Utilities of sets without

S(j)

remain unchanged thus proving the claim. This logic can also be extended to
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
and
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
. For both these rules, shifting will make

u_{i}\left(S\right)

remain the same or increased by

c^{S}(j)

. Finally, look at
R_{\|\;\|}
. By the definition of

d_{i}\left(S\right)

, shifting

l_{i}(j)

and

h_{i}(j)

closer to

c^{S}(j)

will cause a strict decrease in

d_{i}\left(S\right)

if it was a non-zero value. Else,

d_{i}\left(S\right)

remains the same.

Next, we extend discount montonicity
[
85
]
to ensure that a winning degree of project should not be omitted if it becomes less expensive.

Definition 4.7.26

(Discount-proofness)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to be
discount-proof
if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
, a project
p_{j}\in P
, and a set
S
that is selected under
R
,
S
continues to be selected if

c^{S}(j)

is decreased by
1
.

Proposition 4.7.27
.

The rule
R_{|S|}
is discount-proof, whereas
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
,
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
, and
R_{\|\;\|}
are not.

Proof 4.7.28
.

Let the rule
R
be
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
,
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
, or
R_{\|\;\|}
. Consider an instance such that: (i) there are two projects with
\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{1}\right)=\{p_{1}^{0},p_{1}^{1}\}
and
\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{2}\right)=\{p_{2}^{0},p_{2}^{1}\}
(ii)
c_{1}^{1}=2
and
c_{2}^{1}=2
,
b=2
(iii) for every voter
i\in N
,
l_{i}(1)=h_{i}(1)=c_{1}^{1}
and
l_{i}(2)=h_{i}(2)=c_{2}^{1}
. Clearly,
S=\{p_{1}^{1}\}
is a set that is selected under
R
. If
c_{1}^{1}

is changed to
1
,
S
is not selected since only
\{p_{2}^{1}\}
is the unique set to be selected.

Now, consider
R_{|S|}
. This is straight-forward since the utilities are unaffected by costs as long as they are in the acceptable range. Please note that by their definitions, both

l_{i}(j)

and

h_{i}(j)

belong to the set of permissible costs and hence if the cost that is reduced is equal to the lower bound reported by some voter, the lower bound is also automatically considered to be reduced by
1
. Thus, the number of voters finding the chosen degree to be acceptable remains unaffected.

4.7.2
Unanimity Properties

Now, we introduce properties that are based on unanimous approval of all the voters. Given an instance
\mathcal{I}
, for each project
p\in P
, we define
\tau_{p}=\{c_{j}^{t}:p_{j}^{t}\in\mathcal{D},\;\forall i\in N\;\;l_{i}(j)\leq c_{j}^{t}\leq h_{i}(j)\}
. Let
\overline{\tau_{p}}=\max{(\tau_{p})}
and
\underline{\tau_{p}}=\min{(\tau_{p})}
. The first axiom, range-abidingness, requires that if some range of costs is found to be acceptable unanimously by all voters, then the cost allocated to the project must not go beyond this range.

Definition 4.7.29

(Range-abidingness)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to be
range-abiding
if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
, a project
p_{j}\in P
, and a set
S
selected under
R
, it holds that

\tau_{p}\neq\emptyset\implies c^{S}(j)\leq\overline{\tau_{p}}.

Proposition 4.7.30
.

The rules
R_{|S|}
and
R_{\|\;\|}
are range-abiding, whereas
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
and
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
are not.

Proof 4.7.31
.

First, we prove that
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
and
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
are not range-abiding. Recall that
b
denotes the budget. Suppose we have a single project
p
with exactly two permissible costs, respectively equal to
\left\lfloor\frac{b-1}{n}\right\rfloor
and
b
. Suppose all the voters set
l_{i}(j)=c_{j}^{1}
. One voter
i
sets
h_{i}(j)=c_{j}^{2}
, whereas all the remaining voters report
c_{j}^{1}
as the upper bound. Clearly, in a set
S
containing
p_{j}^{1}

, the utility from project
p
is at most
b-1
. Whereas, if
S
selects
p_{j}^{2}

, the utility from
p
is equal to
b
(entirely due to the utility of a single voter). Thus
p_{j}^{2}
is selected. Note that, in this example,
\tau_{p}=\left\lfloor\frac{b-1}{n}\right\rfloor
and clearly
c_{j}^{2}>\tau_{p}
.

Moving on, the rules
R_{|S|}
and
R_{\|\;\|}
are range-abiding. For the sake of contradiction, assume that they are not. That is, in a set
S
selected under the rule,
c^{S}(j)>\overline{\tau_{p}}
. Consider the set
S^{\prime}=S\setminus\{S(j)\}\cup\{p_{j}^{t}:\overline{\tau_{p}}=c_{j}^{t}\}
. Clearly,
S^{\prime}
is valid since
c^{S}(j)>\overline{\tau_{p}}
and
S
is valid. Also,
u_{i}\left(S^{\prime},p\right)>u_{i}\left(S,p\right)
since the degree chosen in
S^{\prime}
is within the bounds reported by every voter (it gives the optimal utility of
n
for
R_{|S|}
and the optimal disutility of
0
for
R_{\|\;\|}
). Thus,
S^{\prime}
is a strictly better set and
S
must not be selected. This forms a contradiction.

The next axiom, range-convergingness, requires that increasing the budget should result in the winning degree of some project moving closer to its unanimously approved range of costs. That is, increasing the budget must increase the chance of maximally satisfying
all
the voters, as opposed to dissatisfying some voters.

Definition 4.7.32

(Range-convergingness)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to be
range-converging
if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
, a set
S
selected for
\mathcal{I}
under
R
, and a set
S^{\prime}\neq S
selected under
R
on increasing the budget, it holds that whenever there is at least one project
k
with
\tau_{k}\neq\emptyset
, there also exists some project
p_{j}\in P
such that:

c^{S}(j)\notin\tau_{p}\implies|c^{S}(j)-\underline{\tau_{p}}|>|c^{S^{\prime}}(j)-\underline{\tau_{p}}|.

Proposition 4.7.33
.

All the four rules
R_{|S|}
,
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
,
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
, and
R_{\|\;\|}
are range-converging.

Proof 4.7.34
.

First, consider the first three rules, i.e., let
R
be
R_{|S|}
,
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
, or
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
. Note that for these rules, the value
\sum_{i\in N}{u_{i}\left(S,p\right)}
decreases as
c^{S}(j)
moves farther from all the costs in
\tau_{p}
(the farther

c^{S}(j)

moves, more is the number of voters for whom

c^{S}(j)

falls out of their acceptable range). We prove the claim by contradiction. Assume that for any project
p_{j}\in P
such that
c^{S}(j)\notin\tau_{p}
and
c^{S}(j)\neq c^{S^{\prime}}(j)
,
c^{S}(j)
is closer to
\tau_{p}
than

c^{S^{\prime}}(j)

is. This implies that
\sum_{i\in N}{u_{i}\left(S^{\prime},p\right)}<\sum_{i\in N}{u_{i}\left(S,p\right)}
. By adding these inequalities for all such
p
, we have
\sum_{i\in N}{u_{i}\left(S^{\prime}\right)}<\sum_{i\in N}{u_{i}\left(S\right)}
. Thus,
S^{\prime}
cannot be selected by
R
since
S
continues to be feasible under the increased budget. The proof for rule
R_{\|\;\|}
follows similar idea. This is because,
\sum_{i\in N}{d_{i}\left(S,p\right)}
increases as
c^{S}(j)
moves farther from all the costs in
\tau_{p}
(as

c^{S}(j)

falls out of the acceptable range for more voters).

The last monotonicity axiom, range-unanimity, requires that if some range of costs is found to be acceptable unanimously by all the voters, the project must be allocated the maximum amount in this range, or in other words, the highest unanimously approved amount.

Definition 4.7.35

(Range-unanimity)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to be
range-unanimous
if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
, whenever
\sum_{p_{j}\in P}{\overline{\tau_{p}}}\leq b
, the set
\{p_{j}^{t}:\;
p
\in P,\;c_{j}^{t}=\overline{\tau_{p}}\}
is selected under
R
.

Note that the above holds by default if
\overline{\tau_{p}}
is not defined for some
p
. Range-unanimity and range-abidingness do not imply each other, though they seem closely related. For example, take the rule that picks for each project
p
, degree with minimum cost in
\tau_{p}
(whenever this set costs lesser than
b
). This is range-abiding but not range-unanimous. In an instance where the maximum costs in
\tau_{p}
together cost more than the budget, range-unanimity is satisfied by default by any rule. However, a rule that selects a degree for project
p
whose cost is greater than maximum cost in
\tau_{p}
, does not satisfy range-abidingness. Thus, range-unanimity does not imply range-abidingness.

Proposition 4.7.36
.

The rules
R_{|S|}
and
R_{\|\;\|}
are
range-unanimous
, whereas
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
and
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
are not.

Proof 4.7.37
.

The proof for
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
and
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
follow from the example in the proof of
Proposition

4.7.30
. Now, consider the rules
R_{|S|}
and
R_{\|\;\|}
. Note that the set
\{p_{j}^{t}:p\in P,\;c_{j}^{t}=\overline{\tau_{p}}\}
achieves the optimal total utility
mn
and optimal total disutility of
0
respectively for both the rules. Hence, if
S^{\prime}
not being selected implies that
\operatorname{c}\!\left(S^{\prime}\right)>b
. This contradicts
\sum_{p_{j}\in P}{\overline{\tau_{p}}}\leq b
and completes the argument.

4.7.3
Efficiency Properties

We finally introduce efficiency properties, which capture how a slight betterment of the outcome in one dimension is not possible (similar to the idea behind classic pareto-efficiency property). The first axiom, degree-efficiency, essentially implies that if two valid sets differ only on the degree of one project, then the set with higher degree needs to be preferred.

Definition 4.7.38

(Degree-efficiency)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to be
degree-efficient
if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
, any project
p_{j}\in P
, any set
S
selected under
R
, and the degree
x\in S(j)
, it holds that

k>x\implies\operatorname{c}\!\left(S\right)-c^{S}(j)+c_{j}^{k}>b.

Proposition 4.7.39
.

The
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
is degree-efficient, whereas
R_{|S|}
,
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
, and
R_{\|\;\|}
are not.

Proof 4.7.40
.

Let
R
be
R_{|S|}
,
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
, or
R_{\|\;\|}
. Consider the example with single project
p
such that for every
i
: (i)
c_{j}^{t_{j}}<b
and
h_{i}(j)<c_{j}^{t_{j}}
(ii) there exists
t<t_{j}
such that
l_{i}(j)\leq c_{j}^{t}\leq h_{i}(j)
. The set
\{p_{j}^{t}:c_{j}^{t}=\overline{\tau_{p}}\}
is selected under
R
since we know that
\tau_{p}\neq\emptyset
and
\overline{\tau_{p}}<b
. Assume that the axiom is satisfied. Then, by the definiton of

t_{j}

,
c_{j}^{t_{j}}>b
contradicting (i).

Now, we look at the rule
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
. Let
S
be a selected set and
k<t_{j}
be such that
k>S(j)
. Consider the set
S^{\prime}=S\setminus\{S(j)\}\cup\{p_{j}^{k}\}
. Take any voter
i
. If
c^{S}(j)<l_{i}(j)
,
u_{i}\left(S^{\prime},j\right)
remains zero like
u_{i}\left(S,j\right)
or increases by
c_{j}^{k}

. If
l_{i}(j)\leq c^{S}(j)\leq h_{i}(j)
,
u_{i}\left(S^{\prime},j\right)
is exactly
\min{(c_{j}^{k},h_{i}(j))}-c^{S}(j)
more than
u_{i}\left(S,j\right)
. This value is positive since
k>S(j)
. Finally, if
c^{S}(j)>h_{i}(j)
,
u_{i}\left(S^{\prime},j\right)
is exactly
c_{j}^{k}
more than
u_{i}\left(S,j\right)
. Thus,

u_{i}\left(S^{\prime}\right)

is clearly greater than

u_{i}\left(S\right)

. Since
S
is selected under
R_{\|\;\|}
,
S^{\prime}
must be infeasible. Thus, the given condition holds.

The next two axioms insist that the valid set closer to the bounds reported by all the voters must be preferred over a valid set farther from them.

Definition 4.7.41

(Lower bound-sensitivity)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to be
lower bound-sensitive
if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
, any project
p_{j}\in P
, and any two valid set
S,S^{\prime}
such that for every voter
i
we have
c^{S}(j)<c^{S^{\prime}}(j)<l_{i}(j)
, it holds that
S
is not selected under
R
.

Proposition 4.7.42
.

The
R_{\|\;\|}
is lower bound-sensitive, whereas
R_{|S|}
,
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
, and
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
are not.

Proof 4.7.43
.

Let
R
be
R_{|S|}
,
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
, or
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
. For lower-bound sensitivity, consider a counter example as follows: (i) there are two projects with
\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{1}\right)=\{p_{1}^{0},p_{1}^{1},p_{1}^{2},p_{1}^{3}\}
and
\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{2}\right)=\{p_{2}^{0},p_{2}^{1}\}
(ii)
c_{1}^{1}=1
,
c_{1}^{2}=2
,
c_{1}^{3}=b-3
, and
c_{2}^{1}=b-2
(iii) for every voter
i\in N
,
l_{i}(1)=h_{i}(1)=c_{1}^{3}
and
l_{i}(2)=h_{i}(2)=c_{2}^{1}
. Clearly,
S=\{p_{1}^{1},p_{2}^{1}\}
is a set that is selected under
R
. Set
S^{\prime}=\{p_{1}^{2},p_{2}^{1}\}
and
j=1
.

Now, let us consider the rule
R_{\|\;\|}
. The proof is straight-forward. Since the disutility of
i
from
S
depends on
l_{i}(j)-c^{S}(j)
and
c^{S}(j)-h_{i}(j)
, clearly,
d_{i}\left(S\right)<d_{i}\left(S^{\prime}\right)
for every voter
i
. Hence,
S
does not get selected by
R_{\|\;\|}
.

Definition 4.7.44

(Upper bound-sensitivity)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to be
upper bound-sensitive
if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
, any project
p_{j}\in P
, and any two valid set
S,S^{\prime}
such that for every voter
i
we have
c^{S}(j)>c^{S^{\prime}}(j)>h_{i}(j)
, it holds that
S
is not selected under
R
.

Proposition 4.7.45
.

The rules
R_{|S|}
,
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
, and
R_{\|\;\|}
are upper bound-sensitive, whereas
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
is not.

Proof 4.7.46
.

First, let the rule
R
be
R_{|S|}
,
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
, or
R_{\|\;\|}
. Since
c^{S}(j)>h_{i}(j)
,
u_{i}\left(S,j\right)=0
and
d_{i}\left(S\right)=c^{S}(j)-h_{i}(j)
. Clearly, utility of
S
and
S^{\prime}
will be same for
R_{|S|}
and
R_{\operatorname{c}(S)}
. However, none of them chooses
S
or
S^{\prime}
. For
R_{\|\;\|}
, utility from
S
will be lesser than that from
S^{\prime}
. Thus,
S
cannot be selected. Finally, consider
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
. Consider an instance such that: (i) there are two projects with
\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{1}\right)=\{p_{1}^{0},p_{1}^{1},p_{1}^{2},p_{1}^{3}\}
and
\operatorname{D}\!\left(p_{2}\right)=\{p_{2}^{0},p_{2}^{1}\}
(ii)
c_{1}^{1}=1
,
c_{1}^{2}=2
,
c_{1}^{3}=3
, and
c_{2}^{1}=b-3
(iii) for every voter
i\in N
,
l_{i}(1)=h_{i}(1)=c_{1}^{1}
and
l_{i}(2)=h_{i}(2)=c_{2}^{1}
. Clearly,
S=\{p_{1}^{3},p_{2}^{1}\}
is a set that is selected under
R_{\widehat{\operatorname{c}(S)}}
. Set
S^{\prime}=\{p_{1}^{2},p_{2}^{1}\}
and
j=1
. The condition is not met.
