4.8
Conclusion and Discussion

Many times, there are multiple ways of executing a public project and hence several, but limited number of, choices for the amount to be allocated to this project. Unfortunately, the existing preference elicitation methods and aggregation rules for participatory budgeting do not take this factor into account and we bridge this gap. We generalized two utility notions defined for PB under restricted costs to our model. We also proposed two other utility notions unique to our model. We analyzed all the corresponding utilitarian welfare maximizing rules computationally and axiomatically.

Our computational part strengthens all the existing positive results, and also introduces several new parameterized tractability results (FPT, parameterized FPTAS) taking into account the parameters recently introduced in the PB literature. It is worth highlighting that all our computational results in
Section

4.6
can be generalized by replacing the utilities with cardinal utility for every degree of each project. However, we present all the results for ranged approval votes due to their practical relevance, simplicity, and deep association with axiomatic analysis.

Followed by this, we introduce several axioms for our model with ranged approval votes and investigate which of these are satisfied by our PB rules. Note that, though none of the proposed PB rules satisfies all the axioms, every rule satisfies some axioms. Axiomatic analysis reflects the properties of each rule, using which the PB organizer can pick a rule based on the context. Also, it is worth bearing in mind that the novel disutility notion and PB rule
R_{\|\;\|}
we proposed for our model satisfies as many axioms as any simple approval-based PB rule satisfies. One of the key takeaways of this chapter is hence a conclusion that
R_{\|\;\|}
is a very good choice when each voter approves a range of costs.

In this chapter, we studied welfare maximization for PB under partially flexible costs. An intriguing direction to explore further would be the concept of fairness within the context of partially flexible costs.

Thus far, our emphasis has been on participatory budgeting under dichotomous preferences. In the subsequent part of this thesis, we will delve into the study of participatory budgeting under ordinal preferences, considering both restricted and flexible costs as the cases of interest.

Part II
Ordinal Preferences

Chapter 5
Restricted Costs: Welfare Maximization and Fairness under Incomplete Weakly Ordinal Preferences

We study the participatory budgeting model where the cost of each project is restricted to one value and the voters report incomplete weakly ordinal preferences. The chapter is structured into two distinct parts: one emphasizing the maximization of welfare and the other dedicated to exploring the concept of fairness.

In the first part, we extend the existing welfare maximizing rules in the literature on dichotomous and strictly ordinal preferences to propose a class of rules, called
dichotomous translation rules
and another rule named
PB-CC rule
. We prove that our extensions mostly preserve and also enhance the computational and axiomatic properties of the rules. As a part of this, we also introduce a new axiom,
pro-affordability
, explicitly relevant to PB under weakly ordinal preferences. In the latter part of the chapter, we introduce fresh mechanisms to address fairness, called average rank-share guarantee (ARSG) rules. This novel class of rules consists of two distinct families: average rank guarantee rules and share guarantee rules. By employing ARSG rules, we are able to overcome the limitations associated with existing fairness notions in the literature on participatory budgeting under ordinal preferences.

5.1
Motivation

Though dichotomous preferences are well studied both for the cases of restricted costs
[
21
,
32
,
10
]
as well as flexible costs
[
85
,
51
,
53
,
73
,
82
,
39
]
, the dichotomous preferences inherently have limited expressibility since all the approved projects are assumed to be equally preferred by the voter. This motivates eliciting ordinal preferences (rankings) of the projects from the voters. Ordinal preferences are proven to be cognitively easy as well as the most liked ballot by the voters since they get to express more information
[
15
]
. Various works study strictly ordinal preferences when the costs are totally flexible
[
6
,
9
,
1
]
and also when the costs are restricted to one single value
[
78
]
. Undeniably, the inherent ranking of projects of a voter is usually weak since ties among the projects are very natural, especially when the number of projects is high. This highlights the need for studying weakly ordinal preferences in participatory budgeting.

Though weakly ordinal preferences are well studied when the costs are flexible
[
6
,
9
,
34
]
, their study when the costs are restricted has been very limited
[
4
]
. This chapter skillfully bridges this gap. It is worth emphasizing that we also allow the weakly ordinal preferences to be incomplete, thereby making the elicitation method no more cognitively harder than standard dichotomous preferences (voters can
optionally
rank the approved projects further).

5.1.1
Contributions and Organization of the Chapter

The two most desired objectives in social choice literature are
welfare maximization
and
fairness
. While the welfare maximization in participatory budgeting (PB) is studied under dichotomous as well as strictly ordinal preferences (the latter is studied only for multi-winner voting, a special case of PB under restricted costs), it remained to be studied under weakly ordinal preferences. We propose PB rules which extend the existing rules to the incomplete weakly ordinal preferences setting, such that the computational and axiomatic properties of the rules are mostly preserved and even enhanced. We justify the significance of each of our extended rules through an exhaustive axiomatic analysis.
Section

5.3
of this chapter studies the PB rules that maximize the utilitarian welfare.

Unlike welfare, fairness for PB under restricted costs and weakly ordinal preferences has been studied in the literature
[
4
]
. However, these existing fairness notions can be unreasonable in several scenarios, as illustrated in the upcoming sections. We resolve this drawback by proposing a different perspective on fairness and introducing a novel interesting class of fair rules. Also, unlike the work by Aziz et al.
[
4
]
, we allow for the ordinal preferences to be incomplete (i.e., voters can rank only a few projects and ignore the rest, as explained in
Section

1.1.1
).
Section

5.4
of this chapter studies the fair PB rules. We conclude by making important and compelling observations in
Section

5.5
.

5.2
Notations and Preliminaries

Let us recall the necessary notations from
Section

2.1.1
and introduce a few more. Recall that
N=\{1,\ldots,n\}
is the set of
n
voters and
P=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\}
is the set of
m
projects. Each voter
i
gives a weakly ordinal preference
\succeq_{i}
over a subset
A_{i}
of projects.
That is,
\succeq_{i}
partitions a set
A_{i}
into equivalence classes such that
E^{i}_{1}\succ_{i}E^{i}_{2}\succ_{i}\ldots
(note that every project need not be included in some equivalence class). The rank of a project
p
is said to be
r
if exactly
r-1
projects are strictly preferred over
p
.
We use
r_{i}(p)
to denote the rank of
p
and
\operatorname{\succeq_{i}\!}\left(t\right)
to denote all the projects ranked exactly
t
in
\succeq_{i}
. We denote by
E^{i}_{[j]}
the set of projects in the first
j
equivalence classes of
\succeq_{i}
, i.e.,
E^{i}_{[j]}=\bigcup_{k\leq j}E^{i}_{k}
. A preference profile
\mathcal{P}
is a vector of ordinal preferences of all the voters, i.e.,
\mathcal{P}={(\succeq_{i})}_{i\in N}
. A cost function
c:P\to\mathbb{N}
gives the cost of each project. Cost of a subset
S\subseteq P
,
\sum_{p\in S}{\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)}
, is denoted by
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
.
b
denotes the total budget available. A set
S\subseteq P
is said to be
feasible
if
c(S)\leq b
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be the set of all feasible subsets of
P
.

Example 5.2.1
.

Suppose
P=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}
is the set of projects such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{1}\right)=3,\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{2}\right)=4,\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{3}\right)=2
, and
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{4}\right)=6
. Let the ranking of a certain voter, suppose
i
, be
\{p_{2},p_{3}\}\succ_{i}\{p_{1}\}
. Now,
A_{i}=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
,
r_{i}(p_{1})=3
,
\operatorname{\succeq_{i}\!}\left(2\right)=\emptyset,\operatorname{\succeq_{i}\!}\left(3\right)=\{p_{1}\}
,
E^{i}_{[2]}=E^{i}_{1}\cup E^{i}_{2}=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
.

A PB instance under incomplete weakly ordinal preferences
\langle N,P,c,b,\mathcal{P}\rangle
is represented by
\mathcal{I}
. The rules we present are
irresolute
, i.e., they output multiple feasible subsets. For an instance
\mathcal{I}
, a PB rule
R
outputs a set of feasible subsets of projects
\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
.

5.3
Welfare Maximizing Rules

In this section, we study PB rules that maximize utilitarian welfare. For this, we first need to define the utility notion. When the preferences are weakly ordinal, utility of a voter from a set of projects must ideally depend on the following factors: (i) the number/costs of the selected projects and (ii) ranks of those projects in her preference. Including both factors (i) and (ii) in the definition of utility may lead to a multi-objective optimization problem. On the other hand, we propose two kinds of PB rules, each considering one of the above two factors. PB rules discussed in
Section

5.3.1
consider the factor (i) while defining the utility, whereas the one in
Section

5.3.2
considers the factor (ii). Each PB rule outputs all the feasible subsets of projects that maximize the utilitarian welfare (i.e., the sum of utilities of all voters).

We define the rules by carefully extending the existing rules in the literature to our model of PB under weakly ordinal preferences, without compromising much on their axiomatic properties. We justify the significance of our extended rules by conducting an exhaustive axiomatic analysis towards the end of this section. In fact, taking a close look at our axiomatic results reveals an interesting observation as will be explained in
Section

5.5
.

5.3.1
Dichotomous Translation Rules

We start by extending the rules defined for PB under dichotomous preferences
[
85
]
. In the model with dichotomous preferences, every voter
i
reports a subset
A_{i}
of projects that she likes and no ranking is involved. Thus, the utility of a voter here depends on factor (i), i.e., on the number of approved projects that got selected or their costs. Talmon and Faliszewski
[
85
]
define three such utility notions and study the corresponding utilitarian rules. The utility of
i
from a set
S
of projects is defined as
u_{i}(S)=f(A_{i}\cap S)
, where
f(S)
could be
|S|,\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right),
or
\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
(takes
1
if
S
is non-empty and
0
otherwise). These utility notions are also explained in
Section

2.1.2.1
.

Dichotomous translation rules progress in two phases. In the first phase, a
translation scheme
is used to convert weakly ordinal preferences into dichotomous preferences satisfying certain property. In the second phase, the existing utilitarian rules described in the previous paragraph are applied on the resultant instance of dichotomous preferences. The translation scheme in the first phase must be chosen carefully such that most properties are preserved in the outcome after the second phase. Additionally, our translation also resolves the drawbacks suffered by PB under dichotomous preferences. We discuss two such translation schemes in this section.

Definition 5.3.2
.

A
dichotomous translation rule

\langle\mathcal{T},f\rangle
is a two-phase protocol with the following phases: (i) translation scheme
\mathcal{T}
converts the weak ranking profile
\mathcal{P}
into an approval vote profile
(A_{i})_{i\in N}
(ii) Every
S
that maximizes
\sum_{i\in N}{f(A_{i}\cap S)}
is selected in the outcome.

Figure 5.1
:
Block diagram to show the working of a dichotomous translation rule
\langle\mathcal{T},f\rangle

5.3.1.1
Multi-knapsack translation scheme

In the PB under dichotomous preferences, all the approved projects are interpreted to be equally desirable. Thus, the PB rules fail to capture the feasible subsets of projects most desired by the voter. This motivated Goel et al.
[
51
]
to introduce knapsack voting, where for every voter
i
,
\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{i}\right)\leq b
. That is, every voter approves her most desired feasible subset of projects. However, this may not perform well when there are ties among the projects and there are multiple equally desirable feasible subsets for the voter. This is illustrated below.

Example 5.3.3
.

Let
A=\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{8}\}
and
b=5
, with costs
\{3,3,2,\ldots,2\}
, respectively. Let there be two voters whose inherent preferences for the projects are as follows:
p_{1}\succ_{1}\{p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}\succ_{1}p_{5}
and
\{p_{1},p_{3}\}\succ_{2}\{p_{4},p_{8}\}
.

If the voters are asked for standard dichotomous preferences, voter
1
approves
\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},p_{5}\}
and voter
2
approves
\{p_{1},p_{3},p_{4},p_{8}\}
. Here, the information that voter
1
prefers an outcome
\{p_{1},p_{4}\}
over
\{p_{1},p_{5}\}
is lost. Now, suppose the voters are asked to report knapsack votes instead (i.e., each
A_{i}
must cost within the budget). Then, voter
1
is forced to approve
p_{1}
and exactly one project from
\{p_{3},p_{4}\}
, thereby implying incorrectly that the remaining project yields no utility to her (note that both
\{p_{1},p_{3}\}
and
\{p_{1},p_{4}\}
are equally desirable feasible outcomes).

In our model, we allow the voter
i
to also report a weakly ordinal preference over projects in
A_{i}
. Our translation scheme uses this weakly ordinal preference to further shrink down
A_{i}
such that it captures all and only those projects present in
at least one
of the most desired feasible subsets of
i
. The
multi-knapsack translation scheme (
\boldsymbol{MT}
)
greedily adds the projects following the rank until the budget constraint is respected. At the point where the constraint gets violated,
MT
includes only those projects whose inclusion will not make the subset infeasible. This translation scheme is demonstrated in Algorithm
1
. The corresponding dichotomous translation rule is further elucidated below in
Example

5.3.4
.

Algorithm 1

Multi-knapsack translation scheme

Input:

An ordinal PB instance
\langle N,P,c,b,\mathcal{P}\rangle

Output:

A dichotomous preference profile
\mathcal{A}

1

for

each voter
i

do

2

A_{i}\leftarrow\emptyset
;

3

j\leftarrow 1
;

4

while

\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{i}\right)+\operatorname{c\!}\left(E^{i}_{j}\right)\leq b

do

5

A_{i}\leftarrow A_{i}\cup E^{i}_{j}
;

6

j\leftarrow j+1
;

7

O_{i}\leftarrow\emptyset
;

8

for

each
p\in E^{i}_{j}

do

9

if

\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)<b-\operatorname{c\!}\left(A_{i}\right)

then

10

O_{i}\leftarrow O_{i}\cup\{p\}
;

11

A_{i}\leftarrow A_{i}\cup O_{i}
;

12

return

(A_{i})_{i\in N}

Example 5.3.4
.

Consider
Example

5.3.3
. Let us look at the first phase. For the first voter, the multi-knapsack translation scheme starts by adding
E^{1}_{1}
(i.e.,
p_{1}
) to
A_{1}
. The left over after this is
5-\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{1}\right)=2
. Cost of
E^{1}_{2}
is
7
, which is greater than left over of
2
and hence
E^{1}_{2}
does not fit entirely into
A_{1}
. Thus, we mark only those projects in
E^{1}_{2}
whose costs are within the left over limit, i.e.,
\{p_{3},p_{4}\}
and add these marked projects into
A_{1}
(In Algorithm
1
, we used
O_{i}
to denote the marked projects for
i
). Finally,
A_{1}=\{p_{1},p_{3},p_{4}\}
. Similarly, for the second voter, set the left over limit to the budget
5
. Since
E^{2}_{1}
costs
5
, add it to
A_{2}
. The left over limit is now
0
. Thus,
A_{2}=\{p_{1},p_{3}\}
.

In the second phase, if
f(S)
is defined as
|S|
or
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
, then the rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
outputs
\{\{p_{1},p_{3}\}\}
. If
f(S)
is defined as
\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
, then the
\langle MT,f\rangle
rule outputs all the sets containing at least one of
p_{1}
and
p_{3}
.

It needs to be mentioned that adding the projects greedily with respect to rank may not always result in an optimal knapsack vote. However, solving the knapsack problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard and cognitively hard for the voters
[
16
]
. Thus, especially when costs of the projects are close to each other, the greedy approach provides a close-to-ideal outcome with much less cognitive load. Consequently, the greedy approach is commonly used for knapsack and PB in real-world. It is worth highlighting that
\langle MT,f\rangle
generalizes the existing multi-winner voting rules
[
41
]
. An ordinal multi-winner voting instance can be represented as a PB instance with
b=k
and unit cost projects. If
f(S)
is
|S|
or
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
,
\langle\mathcal{M},f\rangle
is equivalent to multi-winner bloc rule. If
f(S)=\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
, it is equivalent to
\alpha_{k}
-CC rule.

We now analyze the computational complexity of the decision problem of the dichotomous translation rule with multi-knapsack translation scheme. That is, the problem is to decide if the sets selected by the rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
guarantee at least a given utility
s
.

Theorem 5.3.5
.

Deciding
\langle MT,f\rangle
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard if any of the following conditions holds:

1.

f(S)=c(S)

2.

f(S)=\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
.

Proof 5.3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(A_{i})_{i\in N}
be the dichotomous preference profile obtained from the incomplete weak rankings profile
\mathcal{P}
using the translation scheme
MT
.

1.

We reduce
Subset Sum
(
Definition

3.4.9
) to our problem of deciding if there exists
S\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\sum\limits_{i\in N}{c(A_{i}\cap S)}\geq s
for any score
s
. Given an integer
H
and a set of integers
X=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
, the
Subset Sum
problem is to decide if there exists a subset
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
such that
\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}{x}=H
. This is known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard
[
48
]
. W.l.o.g., we can assume that
X
is sorted in non-decreasing order.

Construct a PB instance as follows: set
b=s=H
. Create
n
projects
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{n}
such that
c(p_{i})=x_{i}
and another project
p_{n+1}
with
c(p_{n+1})=H
. Create
n
voters such that the preference of voter
i
is
p_{i}\succ_{i}p_{n+1}\succ_{i}others
(‘others’ could be ordered arbitrarily). We claim that
Subset Sum
is equivalent to our problem. Let us prove the correctness. The scheme
MT
approves only the top-ranked project for each voter
i
. Hence, for each
i
,
A_{i}
is
\{p_{i}\}
. If the given instance is a
Yes
instance of
Subset Sum
and
X^{\prime}
is the required subset, then
\sum_{i\in N}{f\big({A_{i}\;\cap\;S}\big)}=\sum_{x_{i}\in X^{\prime}}{x_{i}}=H
when
S=\{p_{i}:x_{i}\in X^{\prime}\}
. Hence, this is a
Yes
instance of the given problem. Now, let us assume that the given instance is a
No
instance of
Subset Sum
. Therefore, there is no subset
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
such that
\sum_{x_{i}\in X^{\prime}}{x_{i}}=H
. Thus, for every
S\in\mathcal{F}
,
\sum_{i\in N}{f\big({A_{i}\;\cap\;S}\big)}<s
(since
b=H
), making this a
No
instance of the given problem.

2.

We reduce
Vertex Cover
to our problem of deciding if there exists
S\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\sum\limits_{i\in N}{\mathds{1}(|A_{i}\cap S|>0)}\geq s
for any score
s
.

Definition 5.3.7

(
Vertex Cover
)
.

Given an undirected graph
G=(V,E)
and an integer
k
, the
Vertex Cover
problem is to decide if there exists a
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
such that
|V^{\prime}|\leq k
and
E=\{(v_{1},v_{2}):(v_{1}\in V^{\prime})\lor(v_{2}\in V^{\prime})\}
.

The
Vertex Cover
problem is known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard
[
48
]
. We can assume without loss of generality that
k>2
(for constant values of
k
,
Vertex Cover
becomes tractable).

Construct a PB instance as follows: set
b=k
. For each vertex
v
, add a project
p_{v}
with cost
1
. Add another dummy project
p_{d}
with cost
k-1
. For each edge
e_{i}=(v^{1}_{i},v^{2}_{i})
, add a voter
i
with preference
p_{v^{1}_{i}}\succ_{i}p_{v^{2}_{i}}\succ_{i}p_{d}\succ_{i}others
(‘others’ could be ordered arbitrarily). Set
s=|E|
. We claim that
Vertex Cover
is equivalent to our problem. Let us prove the correctness. For each voter
i
, the scheme
MT
gives an output such that
A_{i}=\{p_{v^{1}_{i}},p_{v^{2}_{i}}\}
. Suppose the given instance
(G,k)
is a
Yes
instance of
Vertex Cover
. Let
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
cover entire
E
such that
|V^{\prime}|\leq k
. Consider the feasible subset of projects
S=\{p_{v}:v\in V^{\prime}\}
. Since
V^{\prime}
is a vertex cover, for any voter
i
,
|A_{i}\cap S|>0
. Therefore,
\sum_{i\in N}{f\big({A_{i}\;\cap\;S}\big)}=|E|
making it a
Yes
instance. Now, let us assume that
(G,k)
is a
No
instance. Any feasible subset of projects gives a total score less than
|E|
, making it a
No
instance.

This completes the proof.

To find the computational complexity when
f(S)=|S|
, we rely on a result presented by Talmon and Faliszewski
[
85
]
.

Proposition 5.3.8

(Talmon and Faliszewski
[
85
]
)
.

Given any PB instance
\mathcal{I}
with dichotomous preference profile, a set
S
maximizing
\sum\limits_{i\in N}{|A_{i}\cap S|}
can be computed in polynomial time.

The next result is a direct consequence of
Proposition

5.3.8
.

Corollary 5.3.9
.

If
f(S)=|S|
, then
\langle MT,f\rangle
can be decided in polynomial time.

Next, we discuss another good translation scheme, called cost-worthy translation scheme (CT), which results in an outcome satisfying many good properties.

5.3.1.2
Cost-worthy translation scheme

The CT scheme represents the desirability of economical projects. A major disadvantage of existing PB rules under dichotomous preferences is that some of them treat all the approved projects same, while others prefer an expensive project assuming that the cost reflects its prestige and quality
[
8
,
85
,
51
]
. While this could be reasonable in some contexts, often there are real-world scenarios in which inexpensive projects are to be preferred and an expensive project is to be funded only if it is
preferred over other projects
by a high enough number of voters. The CT scheme captures the above requirement using the weakly ordinal preferences of the voter and a parameter
\alpha:[m]\to\mathbb{N}
. The function
\alpha
is such that
\alpha(m)=0
and it is monotonically non-increasing function, i.e.,
\alpha(i)\geq\alpha(j)
whenever
i<j
. This is called a
worth function
and is used to capture the relation between ranks and the costs of the projects. For any
j\in[m]
,
\alpha(j)
denotes the maximum amount a project ranked at
j
deserves. The scheme approves, for each voter, only those projects that are affordable according to their ranks and the worth function, as shown in Algorithm
2
. We illustrate this with an example.

Algorithm 2

Cost-worthy translation scheme

Input:

Incomplete weakly ordinal preference profile
\mathcal{P}
, a worth function
\alpha

Output:

Dichotomous preference profile
\mathcal{A}

1

for

each voter
i

do

2

A_{i}\leftarrow\emptyset

3

for

j=1;j\leq m;j++

do

4

if

\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{j}\right)\leq\alpha(r_{i}(p_{j}))

then

5

A_{i}\leftarrow A_{i}

\cup\{p_{j}\}

6

return

(A_{i})_{i\in N}

Example 5.3.10
.

Organizers of a 2-hour seminar must select a subset of candidate talks (each of different duration) based on the preferences of audience. They may decide that only one of the talks, the plenary talk, be given 60 min., while the others be allocated at most 50 or 30 or 20 min. Based on this, they could set the worth function such that
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)=60,\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(2\right)=50,\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(3\right)=30,\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(4\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(5\right)=20,
and
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(x\right)=0
for
x>5
.

\boldsymbol{\alpha}

\mathbf{60}\>\;\;\;

\mathbf{50}\>\;\;\;

\mathbf{30}\>\;\;\;

\mathbf{20}\>\;\;\;

\mathbf{20}\>\;\;\;

40\%

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}A}\>\;\;\succ

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}B}\>\;\;\succ

\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}C},{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}D}\}\>\;\;\succ

\;\>\;\;\;\>\;\;\;\>\;\;\>\>\;\succ

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}E}\>\;\;

of voters

30\>\;\;\;

50\>\;\;\;

30,20\>\;\;\;

20\>\;\;\;

60\%

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}A}\>\;\;\succ

{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}C}\>\;\;\succ

\>\;\;\;\>{B}\>\;\;\>\;\;\>\succ

\{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}D},{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}E}\}\>\;\;

of voters

30\>\;\;\;

30\>\;\;\;

50\>\;\;\>\;\;

\;20,20\>\;\;\;

All projects ranked adequately high are marked above (in the first ranking, rank of
E
is
5
since four projects are preferred over it).
60\%
of the voters do not rank
B
high enough to justify its cost. If
f(S)
is
|S|
or
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
, the dichotomous translation rule
\langle CT,f\rangle
outputs
\{A,C,D,E\}
. If
f(S)
is
\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
, the rule outputs all the sets having at least one of
\{A,C,D,E\}
.

One may wonder why we use a separate worth function instead of asking the voters to simply approve only the projects they consider worth the cost. However, asking so would assume that a voter deeming a project to be cost-worthy is same as the organizer deeming it to be so. But, a voter decides the worth of a project based on the value derived from it with respect to cost, whereas the organizer may decide the worth also based on how it fares in
comparison
to other projects. A voter may feel that a park worth 900 does bring the proportional value and hence approve or rank it decently high. But if there are several projects she prefers over the park, the organizer may decide spending 900 on it is not worth it. The cost-worthy translation scheme offers the organizer the flexibility to decide whether the degree of preference for an expensive project (i.e., its rank) is good enough to warrant a huge cost.

We describe a few situations where CT scheme provides a desirable choice, by specifying the corresponding worth functions. If a funding agency wants no project costing higher than
x
to be selected unless it is of the highest quality, setting
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)=b
and all the others to be
x
captures the requirement. If the funding agency believes that the projects in the top
p
positions have stiff competition among themselves and there is a consensus regarding the remaining projects, a function whose top
p
entries are almost similar while the rest of the entries have larger gaps can be the parameter. If the projects ranked less than a certain threshold should never be approved, we can achieve this by setting to zero, all the entries in the range of worth function to be zero after the threshold. Such a vector can also be used to avoid an inexpensive project being approved by all the voters trivially.

Note that while deciding the worth function parameter can be done intuitively and heuristically in the real-world in several scenarios (organizing a seminar, funding research projects etc.), it may be hard in some other scenarios. This is the primary and only drawback of this rule. Thus, there is an interesting optimization problem underlying their choice. Learning optimal parameters of desirable rules such as median and min-max rules and threshold approval rules
[
16
]
are considered important open directions in the voting literature. Similarly, the question of determining optimal parameters for dichotomous translation rules with cost-worthy translation scheme is an important problem which we preserve for our future work.

The proofs of the next two results is similar to that of
Theorem

5.3.5
and
Corollary

5.3.9
. A similar approach as that of
Corollary

5.3.9
can be used to prove
Theorem

5.3.14
.

Theorem 5.3.11
.

Deciding
\langle CT,f\rangle
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard if any of the following conditions holds:

1.

f(S)=c(S)
and
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)=b

2.

f(S)=\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
and
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)\neq\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)
.

Proof 5.3.12
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(A_{i})_{i\in N}
be the dichotomous preference profile obtained from the incomplete weak rankings profile
\mathcal{P}
using the translation scheme
CT
.

1.

We reduce
Subset Sum
(
Definition

3.4.9
) to our problem of deciding if there exists
S\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\sum\limits_{i\in N}{c(A_{i}\cap S)}\geq s
for any score
s
. Given an integer
H
and a set of integers
X=\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}
, the
Subset Sum
problem is to decide if there exists a subset
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
such that
\sum_{x\in X^{\prime}}{x}=H
. This is known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard
[
48
]
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
X
is sorted in non-decreasing order.

Construct a PB instance as follows: set
b=s=H
. For each integer
x_{i}
, create a project
p_{i}
costing
x_{i}
. Create a single voter who ranks all these
n
projects in the first place. We claim that
Subset Sum
is equivalent to our problem. Let us prove the correctness. The scheme
CT
approves all the projects and hence is equivalent to
Subset Sum
by its definition.

2.

We reduce
Vertex Cover
(
Definition

5.3.7
) to our problem of deciding if there exists
S\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\sum\limits_{i\in N}{\mathds{1}(|A_{i}\cap S|>0)}\geq s
for any score
s
. Given an undirected graph
G=(V,E)
and an integer
k
, the
Vertex Cover
problem is to decide if there exists a
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
such that
|V^{\prime}|\leq k
and
E=\{(v_{1},v_{2}):(v_{1}\in V^{\prime})\lor(v_{2}\in V^{\prime})\}
. The
Vertex Cover
problem is known to be
\mathsf{NP}
-hard
[
48
]
. We can assume without loss of generality that
k>2
(for constant values of
k
,
Vertex Cover
becomes tractable).

Construct a PB instance as follows: set
b=k(\alpha(m)+1)
. For each vertex
v
, add a project
p_{v}
with cost
\alpha(m)+1
. Add
m-2
dummy projects
d_{1},\ldots,d_{m-2}
such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(d_{i}\right)=\alpha(i+1)+1
. For each edge
e_{i}=(v^{1}_{i},v^{2}_{i})
, add a voter
i
with preference
\{p_{v^{1}_{i}},p_{v^{2}_{i}}\}\succ_{i}d_{1}\succ_{i}\ldots\succ_{i}d_{m-2}\succ_{i}others
. Set
s=|E|
. We claim that
Vertex Cover
is equivalent to our problem. Let us prove the correctness. Suppose the given instance
(G,k)
is a
Yes
instance of
Vertex Cover
. Let
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
cover entire
E
such that
|V^{\prime}|\leq k
. Consider the feasible subset of projects
S=\{p_{v}:v\in V^{\prime}\}
. Since
V^{\prime}
is a vertex cover, for any voter
i
,
|A_{i}\cap S|>0
. Therefore,
\sum_{i\in N}{f\big({A_{i}\;\cap\;S}\big)}=|E|
making it a
Yes
instance. Now, let us assume that
(G,k)
is a
No
instance. Any feasible subset of projects gives a total score less than
|E|
, making it a
No
instance.

This completes the proof.

Corollary 5.3.13
.

If
f(S)=|S|
, then
\langle CT,f\rangle
can be decided in polynomial time.

Similar to
Corollary

5.3.9
, the aforementioned corollary is also an immediate consequence of
Proposition

5.3.8
.

Theorem 5.3.14
.

If
f(S)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
, then
\langle CT,f\rangle
can be decided in the time polynomial in
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)
.

Proof 5.3.15
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(A_{i})_{i\in N}
be the approval-vote profile obtained from the incomplete weakly ordinal preference profile
\mathcal{P}
using Algorithm
2
. We present a dynamic programming based algorithm. Let us define score of a project
p
as
score(p)=|\{i\in N:p\in A_{i}\}|*\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)
. Construct a table
T
with
m
rows and
mn\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)
columns. The entry
T(i,j)
corresponds to the cost of cheapest subset of
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{i}\}
for which the total score of projects is exactly
j
. We fill the first row as follows:

T(1,j)=\begin{cases}c(p_{1})&j=score(p_{1})\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\quad\forall j\in[mn\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)].

Now the remaining rows can be filled recursively as follows:

T(i,j)=\min\Big\{T\big(i-1,j\big)\;,\;T\big(i-1,j-score(p_{i})\big)+c(p_{i})\Big\}

(5.1)

The maximum score possible is
\max\{j\in[mn\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)]:T(m,j)\leq b\}
.

Running time.

Note that computing each entry of
T
takes constant time and there are
m^{2}n\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)
entries. The running time is
O(m^{2}n\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right))
. Correctness follows from the definition of
T
.

The next theorem follows trivially since the translation scheme approves all and only those projects whose cost is at most
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)=Q
. Hence, any such project, when it exists will be an optimal solution.

Theorem 5.3.16
.

If
f(S)=\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
, then
\langle CT,f\rangle
can be decided in polynomial time if
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)
.

5.3.2
The PB-CC Rule

In this section, we generalize the Chamberlin-Courant (CC) rule defined for multi-winner voting under strictly ordinal preferences to our model of PB under weakly ordinal preferences. As explained previously, multi-winner voting is a special case of indivisible PB in which there are no costs involved and exactly
k
projects are chosen. Clearly, the utilities in this model are determined from the ranks alone. In their groundbreaking work, Chamberlin and Courant introduced one of the most popular multi-winner voting rules, called CC-rule
[
29
]
.

We generalize the CC-rule to our model where the preferences could be weak, the projects are allowed to have different costs, and a budget constraint is enforced on the outcome. The utility function of our rule however is same as that of standard CC-rule: every voter derives a utility equal to
m-r
from a subset of projects, where
r
is the rank of the voter’s most favorite project in the subset. This is illustrated in the below example.

Example 5.3.17
.

Suppose the preference of a voter is
\{p_{1},p_{3}\}\succ\{p_{2},p_{4}\}\succ p_{5}
. The utility of this voter from a set
S=\{p_{2},p_{5}\}
is
2
since
p_{2}
is the most preferred project of the voter in
S
and the rank of
p_{2}
is
3
.

Definition 5.3.18
.

Given a PB instance
\langle N,P,c,b,\mathcal{P}\rangle
, the PB-CC rule selects every feasible subset
S\in\mathcal{F}
which maximizes the value
\sum\limits_{i\in N}(m-\min\limits_{p\in S}{r_{i}(p)})
.

The following result is a consequence of the fact that deciding CC-rule is
\mathsf{W[2]}
-hard even for multi-winner voting under strictly ordinal preferences
[
17
]
, which is a special case of our model where every project costs
1
and the total budget
b=k
.

Proposition 5.3.19
.

Deciding PB-CC rule is
\mathsf{W[2]}
-hard with respect to the budget
b
.

5.3.3
Axiomatic Justification

Here, we further justify the PB rules proposed in Sections
5.3.1
and
5.3.2
using an axiomatic analysis. We prove that by extending the existing rules in the literature, we do not compromise much on their properties and that, in fact, our rules additionally satisfy a few other interesting properties. As a part of this exercise, we generalize the axioms defined for dichotomous preferences as well as strictly ordinal preferences, both of which are special cases of weakly ordinal preferences. We also introduce a novel axiom, pro-affordability, explicitly for PB under weakly ordinal preferences. The key observations from the axiomatic results are explained in detail in
Table

5.1
and
Section

5.5
.

Throughout the section, for any given PB rule
R
and an instance
\mathcal{I}
, we use
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
to denote the set of all projects included in the output of
R
. Formally,
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)=\{p\in P:\exists S\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right),p\in S\}
. We say a project
p
wins if and only if
p\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
. We use
\boldsymbol{score(p)}
to denote the number of voters such that
p
is included in
A_{i}
by the translation scheme in dichotomous translation rules. We use
\boldsymbol{\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S\right)}
to denote
\min\limits_{p\in S}{r_{i}(p)}
. We defer the axiomatic analysis of dichotomous translation rules with cost-worthy translation scheme to Appendix
.4.1
. It must be noted that almost all the axioms are satisfied for this family of rules, but the proofs are deferred to the appendix due to their simplicity.

5.3.3.1
Generalization of Axioms in the Literature on Dichotomous Preferences

Talmon and Faliszewski
[
85
]
identify compelling axioms for PB under dichotomous preferences. In
Section

3.5
, we extended these axioms to the case where PB rules under dichotomous preferences are irresolute. Now, we further extend these axioms to allow weakly ordinal preferences.

The first axiom,
splitting monotonicity
, asserts that, if a winning project is replaced by a set of multiple new projects that together cost the same, then at least one of the new projects continues to win. In other words, take any winning project
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
. Split
x
into a set
X
of smaller projects such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(X\right)
. In every preference
\succeq_{i}
with
x\in E^{i}_{j}
, replace
x
in
E^{i}_{j}
with all the projects of
X
. Let the new resultant instance be
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Splitting monotonicity requires that at least one project from
X
must belong to
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

Definition 5.3.20

(Splitting Monotonicity)
.

A PB rule
R
satisfies splitting monotonicity if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
and
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
, we have
X\cap\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)\neq\emptyset
whenever
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by splitting
x
into a set
X
of new projects such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(X\right)
and replacing
x
by
X
in every
\succeq_{i}
in
\mathcal{P}
.

Theorem 5.3.21
.

All the following rules satisfy splitting monotonicity:

1.

A dichotomous translation rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
such that
f(S)
is any of the following: (i)
|S|
(ii)
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
or (iii)
\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
.

2.

The PB-CC rule

Proof 5.3.22
.

For an instance
\mathcal{I}
and PB rule
R
, let
x
be a project in
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
and let
S_{x}
be a set such that
S_{x}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
and
x\in S_{x}
. Let
X
be a new set of projects such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(X\right)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)
. Let
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
be an instance obtained by replacing
x
with
X
in every ranking
\succeq_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
. Let
S^{\prime}=(S_{x}\setminus\{x\})\cup X
. It is enough to prove that
S^{\prime}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

1.

First, we prove that for the dichotomous translation rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
, for any
x_{1}\in X
, if
x\in A_{i}
in
\mathcal{I}
, then
x_{1}\in A_{i}
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. This follows since the point where adding of projects to
A_{i}
stops remains the same in both the instances. Since
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x_{1}\right)<\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)
and
x\in A_{i}
in
\mathcal{I}
,
x_{1}\in A_{i}
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
.

To prove (i) and (ii), suppose
f(S)
is
|S|
or
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
. For any
x_{1}\in X
, we know from the above that
score(x)=score(x_{1})
. This implies, if
f(S)=|S|
, total utility of
S^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is exactly
|X|-1
times the total utility of
S_{x}
in
\mathcal{I}
. If
f(S)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
, for any
x_{1}\in X
, we know from the above that
score(x_{1})=(\operatorname{c\!}\left(x_{1}\right)/\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right))score(x)
. Therefore, total utility of
S^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is the same as the total utility of
S_{x}
in
\mathcal{I}
.

To prove (iii), suppose
f(S)=\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
. From the above, the number of voters who have some project of
S_{x}
in
A_{i}
is the same as those who have some project of
S^{\prime}
before it. In all the above scenarios, since
S_{x}\in\operatorname{\langle MT,f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
,
S^{\prime}\cap\operatorname{\langle MT,f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)\neq\emptyset
.

2.

Finally, let
R
be the PB-CC rule. For any voter
i
,
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S_{x}\right)
in
\mathcal{I}
is the same as
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S^{\prime}\right)
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Therefore, total utility of
S_{x}
in
\mathcal{I}
is the same as total utility of
S^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Since
S_{x}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
,
S^{\prime}\cap\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)\neq\emptyset
.

This completes the proof.

The next axiom, discount monotonicity, asserts that a winning project continues to win if it becomes less expensive. The condition of insisting
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)\geq 2
is to ensure that it is possible for the cost of
x
to decrease.

Definition 5.3.23

(Discount Monotonicity)
.

A PB rule
R
satisfies discount monotonicity if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
and
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)\geq 2
, we have
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
whenever
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by reducing the cost of
x
by 1.

Theorem 5.3.24
.

The PB-CC rule satisfies discount monotonicity.

Proof 5.3.25
.

For an instance
\mathcal{I}
, let
x
be a project in
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)\geq 2
, where
R
is the PB-CC rule. Let
S_{x}
be a set such that
S_{x}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
and
x\in S_{x}
. Let
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
be an instance obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by reducing
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)
by
1
. It is enough to prove that
S_{x}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
. For any set
S
and voter
i
, we know that
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S\right)
is the rank of the best ranked project of
S
in
\succeq_{i}
. Consider an arbitrary voter
i
. If some project of
S_{x}
is ranked before
x
in
\succeq_{i}
,
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S_{x}\right)
is the same in
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. If
x
is the best ranked project of
S_{x}
in
\succeq_{i}
in
\mathcal{I}
, then
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S_{x}\right)
remains the same in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
since
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is at least
1
. Therefore, total utility of
S_{x}
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is same as that in
\mathcal{I}
. For any arbitrary voter
i
and a set
S
without
x
in it,
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S\right)
remains the same in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Hence, the total utility of any set without
x
remains the same in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Therefore, any set in
\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
with
x
in it continues to be in
\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
and hence
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

Theorem 5.3.26
.

No dichotomous translation rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
such that
f(S)
is (i)
|S|
, (ii)
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
, or (iii)
\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
satisfies discount monotonicity.

Proof 5.3.27
.

Let
R
be the rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
. For (i) and (ii), consider an instance
\mathcal{I}
with budget
b>3
, projects
P=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},p_{5}\}
costing
\{2,b-1,2,1,b\}
respectively, and four voters with preferences
p_{1}\succ_{1}p_{2}\succ_{1}others
,
p_{2}\succ_{2}p_{3}\succ_{2}others
,
p_{4}\succ_{3}p_{5}\succ_{3}others
, and
p_{4}\succ_{4}p_{5}\succ_{4}others
respectively. Now,
\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)=\{\{p_{1},p_{4}\},\{p_{2},p_{4}\}\}
and
p_{1}\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
. If
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{1}\right)
is reduced by
1
,
\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)=\{\{p_{2},p_{4}\}\}
and
p_{1}\notin\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

For (iii), consider an instance
\mathcal{I}
with budget
b>3
, projects
P=\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
each costing
b
, and two voters with preferences
p_{1}\succ_{1}p_{2}
and
p_{2}\succ_{2}p_{1}
respectively. Then,
\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)=\{\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2}\}\}
and
p_{1}\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
. However, if
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{1}\right)
is reduced by
1
,
\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)=\{\{p_{2}\}\}
and
p_{1}\notin\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

The next axiom, limit monotonicity, requires that a winning project will continue to win if the budget is increased.

Definition 5.3.28

(Limit Monotonicity)
.

A PB rule
R
satisfies limit monotonicity if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
with no project costing exactly
b+1
, we have
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
whenever
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by increasing the budget by 1.

Theorem 5.3.29
.

The following rules do not satisfy limit monotonicity:

1.

A dichotomous translation rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
such that
f(S)
is any of the following: (i)
|S|
(ii)
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
, or (iii)
\mathds{1}(|S|>0)

2.

The PB-CC rule

Proof 5.3.30
.

1.

For (i), consider an instance
\mathcal{I}
with budget
b>3
, projects
\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},p_{5}\}
respectively costing
\{2,b-1,3,1,b\}
, and four voters with rankings:
p_{1}\succ p_{2}\succ\ldots\succ p_{5}
,
p_{2}\succ p_{3}\succ p_{1}\succ p_{4}\succ p_{5}
,
p_{4}\succ p_{5}\succ p_{3}\succ p_{1}\succ p_{2}
, and
p_{4}\succ p_{5}\succ p_{3}\succ p_{2}\succ p_{1}
. Then,
\operatorname{\langle MT,f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)=\{\{p_{1},p_{4}\},\{p_{2},p_{4}\}\}
. Now if the budget is increased by
1
to get
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, we have,
\operatorname{\langle MT,f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)=\{\{p_{2},p_{4}\}\}
. Hence,
p_{1}\notin\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle MT,f\rangle,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
. The same example also works for (iii).

For (ii), consider an instance
\mathcal{I}
with budget
b>4
, projects
\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}\}
respectively costing
\{2,b-1,2,b\}
, and three voters with rankings:
p_{1}\succ p_{2}\succ p_{3}\succ p_{4}
,
p_{3}\succ p_{4}\succ p_{1}\succ p_{2}
, and
p_{3}\succ p_{4}\succ p_{2}\succ p_{1}
. Then,
\operatorname{\langle MT,f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)=\{\{p_{1},p_{3}\}\}
. Now if the budget is increased by
1
to get
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, since
b+3>7
, we have,
\operatorname{\langle MT,f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)=\{\{p_{2},p_{3}\}\}
. Hence,
p_{1}\notin\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle MT,f\rangle,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

2.

Let
R
be the PB-CC rule. Construct an instance with budget
b=1
, projects
P=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
costing
1
each, and two voters with preferences
p_{1}\succ p_{2}\succ p_{3}
and
p_{3}\succ p_{2}\succ p_{1}
respectively. Note that no project in
P
costs
b+1
. Clearly no non-singleton set is feasible and utility of every singleton set is
2
. Therefore,
\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)=\{\{p_{1}\},\{p_{2}\},\{p_{3}\}\}
. Now, construct an instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
by increasing
b
to
2
. Now though
\{p_{2}\}
is feasible, it has a utility of
2
whereas the feasible set
\{p_{1},p_{3}\}
has an optimal utility of
4
. Therefore,
\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)=\{\{p_{1},p_{3}\}\}
and
p_{2}\notin\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

This completes the proof.

Next, we study inclusion maximality
[
85
]
, which implies that whenever some budget is remaining, voters would wish to use it.

Definition 5.3.31

(Inclusion Maximality)
.

A PB rule
R
satisfies inclusion maximality if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
and
S,S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
such that
S\subset S^{\prime}
and
S\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
, we have
S^{\prime}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
.

Proposition 5.3.32
.

All the following rules satisfy inclusion maximality:

1.

Any dichotomous translation rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
such that
f(S)
is any of the following: (i)
|S|
, (ii)
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
, or (iii)
\mathds{1}(|S|>0)

2.

The PB-CC rule

Proof 5.3.33
.

1.

It may be observed that, for all the three utility notions studied, utility from a set
S
is at least as much as the utility from any subset of
S
. Such functions are said to be subset monotone functions. For any subset monotone functions, inclusion maximality is satisfied by default since adding additional projects may only increase the utility.

2.

The function
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S\right)
is subset-monotone in
S
. This is because,
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S\right)\leq\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S^{\prime}\right)
for any
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
. Since the goal is to minimize
\sum_{i}\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S\right)
, inclusion-maximality follows.

This completes the proof.

5.3.3.2
Generalization of Axioms in the Literature on Strictly Ordinal Preferences

We extend the axioms defined for strictly ordinal preferences
[
36
]
to allow for weakly ordinal preferences. The first axiom, candidate monotonicity, asserts that if a winning project is exchanged with a project ranked in the equivalence class just before it, it continues to win.

Definition 5.3.34

(Candidate Monotonicity)
.

A PB rule
R
satisfies candidate monotonicity if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
and a project
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
, we have
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
whenever
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by exchanging
x\in E^{i}_{j}
with a project
x^{\prime}\in E^{i}_{j-1}
in some preference
\succeq_{i}
.

Theorem 5.3.35
.

All the following rules satisfy candidate monotonicity:

1.

A dichotomous translation rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
such that
f(S)
is any of the following: (i)
|S|
, (ii)
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
, or (iii)
\mathds{1}(|S|>0)

2.

The PB-CC rule

Proof 5.3.36
.

For any instance
\mathcal{I}
and a PB rule
R
, let
x
be a project such that
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
. Consider any
\succeq_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
. Let
x\in E^{i}_{j}
and
x^{\prime}
be any project whose rank in
E^{i}_{j-1}
. Construct
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
by exchanging
x
and
x^{\prime}
in
\succeq_{i}
.

1.

For (i) and (ii), it can be observed that in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,
score(x)
will increase or remain the same, whereas,
score(x^{\prime})
will remain the same or decrease. This is because, depending on
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)
and
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x^{\prime}\right)
, the point where we stop adding projects to
A_{i}
will either stay the same in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
or change from
E^{i}_{j}
to
E^{i}_{j-1}
or from
E^{i}_{j-1}
to
E^{i}_{j}
. Now, consider any other project
p
whose score in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is greater than that in
\mathcal{I}
. This is possible only when
p
is ranked after
x
in the new instance. So, the increase in the score of
p
cannot be greater than the increase in the score of
x
. Since
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
, there exists
S_{x}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
such that
x\in S_{x}
. By definition, the utility of any set is the sum of scores of all projects in that set. Hence, some set containing
x
will continue to win. Therefore,
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

For (iii), the utility of a set
S
is the number of voters who have some project of
S\in A_{i}
. Utility of any
S_{x}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
increases by
1
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
(when
x
is the only project in
S_{x}
in
A_{i}
) or remains the same, otherwise. Similarly, the utility of any set without
x
stays the same or decreases
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Therefore,
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

2.

The total utility of set
S
can change only because of
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S\right)
since preferences of all the other voters are unperturbed. Consider any set
S
. Let
x\in S
and
x^{\prime}\notin S
. Then,
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S\right)
decreases from
\mathcal{I}
to
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
or remains the same. Hence, utility of
S
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is at least that in
\mathcal{I}
. Now, suppose
x\notin S
and
x^{\prime}\in S
. Then,
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S\right)
decreases from
\mathcal{I}
to
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
or remains the same. Hence, utility of
S
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is at most that in
\mathcal{I}
. If both
x
and
x^{\prime}
are present or absent together in
S
,
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S\right)
and the total utility of
S
are always the same in both
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Therefore, utility of any set with
x
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
will be at least that in
\mathcal{I}
and utility of remaining sets will remain the same or decrease. Since
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
, some set with
x
continues to win and
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

This completes the proof.

The next axiom, non-crossing monotonicity, asserts that if some project in a selected set
S
is exchanged with a project in the equivalence class just before it without disturbing any other project in
S
, the set
S
continues to win.

Definition 5.3.37

(Non-crossing Monotonicity)
.

A PB rule
R
satisfies non-crossing monotonicity if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
, a set
S\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
, and a project
x\in S
, we have
S\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
whenever
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by exchanging
x\in E^{i}_{j}
with a project
x^{\prime}\in E^{i}_{j-1}
in some preference
\succeq_{i}
such that
x^{\prime}\notin S
.

Theorem 5.3.38
.

A dichotomous translation rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
such that
f(S)
is (i)
|S|
or (ii)
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
satisfies non-crossing monotonicity.

Proof 5.3.39
.

Let
R
denote the rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
. Let
S_{x}
be a set such that
S_{x}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
and
x\in S_{x}
. Let
\succeq_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
be a preference such that
x\in E^{i}_{j}
for some
j
and
S_{x}\setminus E^{i}_{j-1}\neq\emptyset
. Construct
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
by exchanging
x
and
x^{\prime}
in
\succeq_{i}
. For the sake of contradiction, assume
S_{x}\notin\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
. Let
f(S)
be
|S|
or
c(S)
.

The proof is an extension to the proof of
Theorem

5.3.35
and we use the fact that for the said rules, the utility of a set can be expressed as the sum of scores of projects in the set. We give an outline of the argument. From
Theorem

5.3.35
, we know that the utility of
S_{x}
increases or stays the same. We also know that there exists
S\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
such that
x\in S
. This is possible only if
score(x)
increases since
S_{x}\notin\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
. But if
score(x)
increases, since
x^{\prime}\notin S_{x}
, utility of
S_{x}
also increases. This implies
S_{x}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

Theorem 5.3.40
.

The following rules do not satisfy non-crossing monotonicity:

1.

The dichotomous translation rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
with
f(S)=\mathds{1}(|S|>0)

2.

The PB-CC rule

Proof 5.3.41
.

1.

Consider an instance
\mathcal{I}
with budget
b

(>2)
, a set of projects
P=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6}\}
costing
1
,
b-1
,
b-1
,
b
,
1
, and
b
respectively, and three voters whose preferences are:
p_{1}\succ_{1}p_{2}\succ_{1}p_{3}\succ_{1}others
,
p_{3}\succ_{2}p_{6}\succ_{2}others
, and
p_{5}\succ_{3}p_{4}\succ_{3}others
respectively. Clearly,
\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)=\{\{p_{1},p_{3}\},\{p_{2},p_{5}\},\{p_{3},p_{5}\},\{p_{1},p_{5}\}\}
. Now let
S=\{p_{1},p_{3}\}
. See that
p_{2}\notin S
. Exchange
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
in
\succeq_{1}
to obtain a new instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. In
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,
\{p_{3},p_{5}\}
has a strictly higher utility than
S
and hence
S\notin\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

2.

The proof for PB-CC follows from the proof for CC rule by Elkind et al.
[
36
]
. Their CC rule is a special case of our PB-CC, where the budget is
k
and all the projects cost
1
each. The authors prove that the CC rule does not satisfy non-crossing monotonicity.

This completes the proof.

5.3.3.3
Axioms in the Literature on Generic Preferences

We examine the axioms in the literature applicable for any voting model
[
25
,
80
,
56
]
. The first two axioms we study ensure an impartial treatment of all the voters and projects by enforcing that the outcome should not depend on the indexing of voters or projects. Let
\Sigma_{S}
be the set of all permutations on a set
S
.

Definition 5.3.42

(Anonymity)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to be anonymous if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{N}
, we have
R(\mathcal{I})=R(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
whenever
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by replacing
\succ_{i}
with
\succ_{\sigma(i)}
for every
i\in N
.

Definition 5.3.43

(Neutrality)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to be neutral if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{P}
, we have
R(\mathcal{I})=R(\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
whenever
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by replacing
\operatorname{c\!}\left(j\right)
with
\operatorname{c\!}\left(\sigma(j)\right)
and
\operatorname{\succeq_{i}\!}\left(j\right)
with
\operatorname{\succeq_{i}\!}\left(\sigma(j)\right)
in every
\succ_{i}
for every
j\in[m]
.

Theorem 5.3.44
.

Following rules satisfy both anonymity and neutrality:

1.

Any dichotomous translation rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
such that
f(S)
is
|S|
,
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
, or
\mathds{1}(|S|>0)

2.

The PB-CC rule

Anonymity follows since all the rules are utilitarian and permuting the voters does not affect the summation. Neutrality follows since all rules depend only on the cost of projects and their ranks in preferences. The next popularly studied axiom, consistency, requires that for a voting rule, if two disjoint groups of voters
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
both choose an outcome
a
,
a
continues to be chosen if the groups participate together in an election, i.e., for
N_{1}\cup N_{2}
.

Definition 5.3.45

(Consistency)
.

A PB rule
R
is said to be consistent if for any two ordinal PB instances
\mathcal{I}_{1}=\langle N_{1},P,c,b,\mathcal{P}_{1}\rangle
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}=\langle N_{2},P,c,b,\mathcal{P}_{2}\rangle
such that
N_{1}\cap N_{2}=\emptyset
, we have,

R(\mathcal{I}_{1})\cap R(\mathcal{I}_{2})\subseteq R(\langle N_{1}\cup N_{2},P,c,b,\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}\rangle).

Theorem 5.3.46
.

All the following rules satisfy consistency:

1.

A dichotomous translation rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
such that
f(S)
is any of the following: (i)
|S|
, (ii)
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
, or (iii)
\mathds{1}(|S|>0)

2.

The PB-CC rule

Proof 5.3.47
.

Consider any
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
as given in
Definition

5.3.45
. Take any dichotomous translation rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
, a set
S\in\langle MT,f\rangle(\mathcal{I}_{1})\cap\langle MT,f\rangle(\mathcal{I}_{2})
, and the instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=\langle N_{1}\cup N_{2},P,c,b,\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}\rangle
. Since
S\in\langle MT,f\rangle(\mathcal{I}_{1})
,
\sum_{i\in N_{1}}{f\big({A_{i}\;\cap\;S}\big)}\geq\sum_{i\in N_{1}}{f\big({A_{i}\;\cap\;S^{\prime}}\big)}
for any
S^{\prime}\subseteq P
. Likewise,
\sum_{i\in N_{2}}{f\big({A_{i}\;\cap\;S}\big)}\geq\sum_{i\in N_{2}}{f\big({A_{i}\;\cap\;S^{\prime}}\big)}
. By including both these inequalities, we get
S\in\operatorname{\langle MT,f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
.

The proof for part (b) follows the same idea.

5.3.3.4
Pro-Affordability

We propose an axiom called pro-affordability explicitly for indivisible PB under weakly ordinal preferences, to assert that we always prefer a project that is less expensive and ranked higher by everyone.

Definition 5.3.48

(Pro-affordability)
.

A PB rule
R
satisfies pro-affordability if for any instance
\mathcal{I}
,
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
, and
x^{\prime}\in P
such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x^{\prime}\right)<\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)
and
x^{\prime}\succeq_{i}x
for every voter
i
, we have
x^{\prime}\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
.

Theorem 5.3.49
.

All the following rules satisfy pro-affordability:

1.

A dichotomous translation rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
such that
f(S)
is any of the following: (i)
|S|
or (ii)
\mathds{1}(|S|>0)

2.

The PB-CC rule

Proof 5.3.50
.

For any rule
R
, an instance
\mathcal{I}
, and a project
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(R,\mathcal{I}\right)
, let
x^{\prime}
be a project such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x^{\prime}\right)<\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)
and
x^{\prime}\succeq_{i}x
for every voter
i
. Let
S_{x}
be a set such that
S_{x}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
and
x\in S_{x}
. Let
S^{\prime}=(S_{x}\setminus\{x\})\cup\{x^{\prime}\}
. Note that
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
since
S_{x}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x^{\prime}\right)<\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)
. It is enough to prove that
S^{\prime}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
.

1.

Let us prove for (i). For any arbitrary voter
i
, if
x\in A_{i}
, then
x^{\prime}\in A_{i}
since
x^{\prime}\succeq_{i}x
. Utility of the set
S^{\prime}
is at least that of
S_{x}
since
score(x^{\prime})\geq score(x)
. Therefore,
S^{\prime}\in\operatorname{\langle MT,f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
. Now, we prove for (ii). For any arbitrary voter
i
, if
S_{x}\cap A_{i}\neq\emptyset
, then
S^{\prime}\cap A_{i}\neq\emptyset
since
x^{\prime}\succeq_{i}x
. Thus, the utility of
S^{\prime}
is at least as much as that of
S_{x}
and
S^{\prime}\in\operatorname{\langle MT,f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
.

2.

Let
R
be the PB-CC rule. Take any arbitrary voter
i
. If
\operatorname{r_{i}\!}\left(x^{\prime}\right)<\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S_{x}\right)\leq\operatorname{r_{i}\!}\left(x\right)
, then
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S^{\prime}\right)<\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S_{x}\right)
. Else,
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S^{\prime}\right)=\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S_{x}\right)
. Hence, utility of
S^{\prime}
is at least that of
S_{x}
. Since
S_{x}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
,
S^{\prime}\in\operatorname{R\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
.

This completes the proof.

Theorem 5.3.51
.

The dichotomous translation rule
\langle MT,f\rangle
such that
f(S)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
does not satisfy pro-affordability.

. Suppose there are three voters such that one voter has a ranking

and two voters have a ranking
p_{3}\succ p_{4}\succ p_{1}\succ p_{2}
. Clearly

though

is preferred over
p_{2}
by all the voters and
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{1}\right)<\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{2}\right)
.
