. However, as
c(S)=c(S^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{P}_{N}
and
\mathcal{P}_{N}^{\prime}
are group-wise equivalent. Combining these observations with group-wise anonymity of
f
, we have
\beta_{S}=\beta_{S^{\prime}}
.

Since for any
S\subseteq N
,
c(S)\in\Gamma
, the parameter set of
f
can be written as
\{\beta_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
. Consider a set
S\subseteq N
and a profile
\mathcal{P}_{N}
. The value
\max_{i\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\}
is same as
\max_{q\in G}\{\tau_{\gamma_{q}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})\}
. On combining this together with the fact that
\beta_{S}=\beta_{S^{\prime}}
for all
S
and
S^{\prime}
with
c(S)=c(S^{\prime})
, we have for all
\mathcal{P}_{N}\in\mathcal{D}^{n}

f(\mathcal{P}_{N})=\min_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\left[\max\{\tau_{\gamma_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\gamma_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta_{\gamma}\}\right].

(If:)
Let
f:\mathcal{D}^{n}\to P
be a GMMR. We have to show that
f
is unanimous, strategy-proof, and group-wise anonymous. Since
f
is GMMR, it is a MMR (see Definition 2.8). Hence, by Lemma 2.10,
f
is strategy-proof and unanimous. To show that
f
is group-wise anonymous, take two group-wise equivalent profiles
\mathcal{P}_{N}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{N}
, that is, there exists a group-preserving permutation
\pi_{N}
such that
\mathcal{P}_{N}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}_{\pi(N)}
. Let
\{\beta_{\gamma}\}_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
be the parameters of
f
. Since
\mathcal{P}_{N}
and
\mathcal{P}_{N}^{\prime}
are group-wise equivalent, for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
and any group
q\in G
, we have
\tau_{\gamma_{q}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})=\tau_{\gamma_{q}}(\mathcal{P}_{N^{\prime}_{q}})
. Therefore, by the definition of GMMR,
f(\mathcal{P}_{N})=f(\mathcal{P}_{N}^{\prime})
.

We express a RSCR as a convex combination of DSCRs
\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{q}
. That is,
\varphi=\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}\varphi_{w}
where
W=\{1,2,\ldots,q\}
,
\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}=1
, and for every
j\in W
,
0\leq\lambda_{j}\leq 1
and
\varphi_{j}
is a DSCR. If every
\varphi_{j}
such that
\lambda_{j}>0
is a group min-max rule, such a RSCR is called
random group min-max rule
. Next, we prove that random group min-max rules are equivalent to probabilistic fixed group ballot rules.

Theorem 6.7.35
.

Every probabilistic fixed group ballot rule on
\mathcal{D}^{n}
is also a random group min-max rule on
\mathcal{D}^{n}
.

Proof 6.7.36
.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma
, let
\mathcal{P}^{\gamma}
denote the profile where from group
q
,
\gamma_{q}
number of voters have top-ranked projects at
p_{1}
and
|N_{q}|-\gamma_{q}
number of voters have top-ranked projects at
p_{m}
. We call these profiles boundary profiles. Since the outcome of a probabilistic fixed group ballot rule at any profile is linearly dependent on the outcomes at the boundary profiles (see
[
35
]
), it is sufficient to show that any unanimous and strategy-proof RSCR on the boundary profiles can be written as a convex combination of unanimous and strategy-proof DSCRs on the boundary profiles.

Let
\varphi
be a unanimous and strategy-proof RSCR defined on these boundary profiles. We will show that there are unanimous and strategy-proof DSCRs such that
\varphi
can be written as a convex combination of those deterministic rules. More formally, we show that there exist
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}
unanimous and strategy-proof DSCRs and non-negative numbers
\lambda_{s}
where
s\leq r
with
\sum_{s=1}^{r}=1
such that for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
,

\varphi_{p_{k}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma})=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\lambda_{s}f_{sp_{k}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma}),

for all
p_{k}\in P
. Here
f_{sp_{k}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma})=1
if
f_{s}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma})=p_{k}
otherwise
f_{sp_{k}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma})=0
. Let
z=\sum_{q=1}^{g}2^{|N_{q}|}
. These system of equations can be represented in matrix form as
Z\lambda=d
where
Z
is a
zm\times r
size matrix of
0-1
,
\lambda
is column vector of length
r
with
\lambda_{s}
in row
s
, and
d
is a column vector of length
zm
with
\varphi_{p_{k}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma})
in the row corresponding to
(\gamma,k)
. By Farka’s Lemma, having a solution to this system of equations is equivalent to show that
d^{\prime}y\geq 0
for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{zm}
with
Z^{\prime}y\geq 0\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
.

We will prove this by using a network flow formulation of the problem and using the max-flow min-cut theorem. Consider an arbitrary numbering of
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{z}
with
\gamma_{1}=\underline{\gamma}
and
\gamma_{z}=\overline{\gamma}
. The set of vertices is
V=\{x,y\}\cup\{(S_{i},j)\mid i=1,\ldots,z,j=1,\ldots,m\}
where
x
is the source and
y
is the sink. The edges are defined as follows:

•

For every
s\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, let
E_{s}=\{((\gamma_{i},j),(\gamma_{i+1},k))\mid i=1,\ldots,z-1,f_{sp_{j}}(P^{\gamma_{i}})=f_{sp_{k}}(P^{\gamma_{i+1}})=1\}
.

•

There is an edge
(x,(\gamma_{1},j))
for all
j=1,\ldots,m
.

•

There is an edge
((\gamma_{z,j}),y)
for all
j=1,\ldots,m
.

Now the set of edges
E
is the union of sets
E_{s}
. We can define a path for every
s
, it is defined as
E_{s}\cup\{(x,(\gamma_{1},j)),((\gamma_{z},j^{\prime}),y)\mid f_{sj}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma}_{1})=f_{sj^{\prime}}(P^{z})=1\}
. Hence, every strategy-proof deterministic rule has a path from source
x
to sink
y
. The capacities of the vertices are defined as
c(\gamma_{i},j)=\varphi_{p_{j}}(P^{\gamma_{i}})
and
c(x)=c(y)=1
.

A cut is a set of vertices such that every deterministic rule intersects the cut at least once.

Lemma 6.7.37
.

The minimum capacity of a cut is equal to 1.

We prove the above lemma in Appendix
.5.1
. By Lemma
6.7.37
and the max-flow min-cut theorem, it follows that the maximal flow through the network is 1. Since the total capacity of the nodes corresponding to any given profile is
1
and every path will intersect one such node, it must be that the flow through each node in the network will be exactly the capacity of the node.

We are now ready to complete the proof of the theorem. Consider a
maximum flow through the network. It follows from the definition of the network that each path is determined by a deterministic strategy-proof rule. Consider such a rule
f_{s}
. Suppose that
Fl(s)
denotes the flow through the path induced by the rule.
Since it is a flow, we have
Fl(s)\geq 0
, and hence

\sum_{s=1}^{r}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}y(\gamma,r(\Gamma))Fl(s)\geq 0.

(6.1)

Consider the coefficient of an arbitrary term
y(\gamma,j)
at the left-hand side of
Equation

6.1
. Note that the total flow through an edge of the network is the sum of the flows through all paths containing the edge. Hence, the
total flow at the vertex
(\gamma,j)
in the network is the sum of the flows through the vertex through all paths containing
(\gamma,j)
. We have already shown
\varphi_{p_{j}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma})
, which yields

\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\sum_{j=1}^{m}y(\gamma,j)\varphi_{p_{j}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma})\geq 0.

From
Theorem

6.6.24
and
Theorem

6.7.35
, it can be concluded that a RSCR is unanimous, strategy-proof, and group-wise anonymous if and only if it can be expressed as a convex combination of group min-max rules.

6.7.2
EPC: Weak-GEG and Group-Wise Anonymity

It is evident from
Section

6.7.1
that the unanimous, strategy-proof, and group-wise anonymous RSCRs are characterized to be convex combinations of group min-max rules. In this section, we further impose weak-GEG and characterize the random group min-max rules that are
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-weak-GEG (
Definition

6.5.18
). The characterization intuitively requires that for any
\kappa_{q}
-sized interval of
\triangleleft
that can be the outcome of
\psi_{q}
under some conditions, the weightage allotted to DSCRs having both the parameters in the interval is at least
\eta_{q}
whenever
\gamma
corresponding to one of them dominates the other.

Theorem 6.7.38
.

A RSCR
\varphi:\mathcal{D}^{n}\to\Delta P
is unanimous, strategy-proof, group-wise anonymous, and
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-weak-GEG if and only if it is a random group min-max rule
\varphi=\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}\varphi_{w}
such that for all
q\in G
, for all
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma\gg\gamma^{\prime}
, and for all
p_{x}\in P
feasible at
(\gamma^{\prime}_{q},\gamma_{q};\psi_{q})
, we have

\sum_{\{w\;\mid\;p_{x}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{\prime}},\;\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma}\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}\}}{\lambda_{w}}\geq\eta_{q}.

Proof 6.7.39
.

(Only if:)
We prove that for a random group min-max rule to satisfy
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-weak-GEG, it is necessary that the given condition holds. Consider any
q,\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},
and
p_{x}
as given in the theorem. Since
p_{x}
is feasible at
(\gamma^{\prime}_{q},\gamma_{q};\psi_{q})
, we know that there exists a profile
\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}
such that exactly
\gamma^{\prime}_{q}
voters have their top-ranked projects before
p_{x}
, exactly
\gamma_{q}-\gamma^{\prime}_{q}
voters have their top-ranked projects in the interval
[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}]
, and
\psi_{q}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})=[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}]
. For every other group
h\in G\setminus\{q\}
, construct an arbitrary profile
\mathcal{P}_{N_{h}}
such that exactly
\gamma^{\prime}_{h}
voters have their top-ranked projects before
p_{x}
and exactly
\gamma_{h}-\gamma^{\prime}_{h}
voters have their top-ranked projects in the interval
[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}]
. Let the combined preference profile be
\mathcal{P}_{N}
.

Since the fairness requirement of group
q
is met at
\mathcal{P}_{N}
,
\sum_{\{w:\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\in[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}]\}}{\lambda_{w}}\geq\eta_{q}
. Consider any
w
such that
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\in[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}]
. Since
\varphi_{w}
is a group min-max rule, for any
\hat{\gamma}\in\Gamma
,
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\trianglelefteq\max\{\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\hat{\gamma}}\}
. This implies
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
since
\tau_{\gamma^{\prime}_{h}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{h}})\triangleleft p_{x}
for any
h\in G
. Similarly, since
\varphi_{w}
is a group min-max rule, there exists
\hat{\gamma}\in\Gamma
such that
\max\{\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\hat{\gamma}}\}=\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})
. This is not possible if
\hat{\gamma}_{h}>\gamma_{h}
for any
h\in G
since
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}
and
p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}\triangleleft\tau_{\gamma_{h}+1}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{h}})
by construction. Therefore, there exists
\hat{\gamma}
such that
\gamma\gg\hat{\gamma}
and
\max\{\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\hat{\gamma}}\}=\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})
. By the definition of group min-max rules,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\hat{\gamma}}
. This implies,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\hat{\gamma}}\trianglelefteq\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}
. Hence, we have
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
and
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma}\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}
.

(If:)
We now prove that any random group min-max rule that satisfies the given condition also satisfies
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-weak-GEG. Consider any group
q\in G
and an arbitrary preference profile
\mathcal{P}_{N}
. Let
p_{x}=\min{\{\psi_{q}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})\}}
. Set
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
such that for every
h\in G
,
\gamma^{\prime}_{h}=|i\in N_{h}:\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft p_{x}|
and
\gamma_{h}=|i\in N_{h}:\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}|
. By construction,
\gamma\gg\gamma^{\prime}
. Also, since
\psi_{q}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})=[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}]
,
p_{x}
is feasible at
(\gamma^{\prime}_{q},\gamma_{q};\psi_{q})
. Thus
q,\gamma,\gamma^{\prime},
and
p_{x}
satisfy all the given conditions.

Consider any group min-max rule
\varphi_{w}
such that
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
and
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma}\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}
. Since
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma}\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}
and also
\tau_{\gamma_{h}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{h}})\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}
for any
h\in G
,
\max\{\tau_{\gamma_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\gamma_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma}\}\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}
. This implies,
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}
. By construction,
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\tau_{\gamma^{\prime}_{h}+1}
for any
h\in G
. This implies, for any
\hat{\gamma}
such that
\hat{\gamma}_{h}>\gamma_{h}
for some
h\in G
,
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\max\{\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\hat{\gamma}}\}
. For any
\hat{\gamma}
such that
\gamma^{\prime}\gg\hat{\gamma}
, since
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
, by definition of group min-max rule,
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\hat{\gamma}}
and thus,
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\max\{\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\hat{\gamma}}\}
. Therefore, for any
\hat{\gamma}\in\Gamma
,
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\max\{\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\hat{\gamma}}\}
. This implies,
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})
. Combining this with
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}
gives
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\in[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}]
. Fairness requirement of the group
q
is met and this completes the proof.

6.7.3
EPC: Strong-GEG and Group-Wise Anonymity

Here, we characterize the random group min-max rules that are
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-strong-GEG (Def.
6.5.19
). Recall the notion of feasibility of a set of projects introduced in
Section

6.6.3
. Basically, the following characterization ensures that there cannot be
\kappa_{q}
consecutive projects which could be selected by
\psi_{q}
under some conditions and are allotted a probability less than
\eta_{q}
each.

Theorem 6.7.40
.

A RSCR
\varphi:\mathcal{D}^{n}\to\Delta P
is unanimous, strategy-proof, group-wise anonymous, and
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-strong-GEG if and only if it is a random group min-max rule
\varphi=\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}\varphi_{w}
such that for all
q\in G
, for all
\gamma^{0},\gamma^{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\kappa_{q}}\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma^{\kappa_{q}}\gg\ldots\gg\gamma^{1}\gg\gamma^{0}
, and for all
p_{x}\in P
such that
\{p_{x},p_{x+1},\ldots,p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}\}
is feasible at
(\gamma^{0},\gamma^{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\kappa_{q}};\psi_{q})
, there exists
t\in[0,{\kappa_{q}-1}]
such that

\sum_{\left\{w\;\mid\;p_{x+t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t}}\;,\;\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t+1}}\trianglelefteq p_{x+t}\right\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{q}.

Proof 6.7.41
.

(Only if:)
We first prove that a random group min-max rule satisfies
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-strong-GEG only when the given condition is satisfied. Consider any
q,\gamma^{0},\gamma^{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\kappa_{q}},
and
p_{x}
as given in the theorem. Construct a profile
\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}
such that: (i) For every group
N_{h}
, set the top-ranked projects of exactly
\gamma^{0}_{h}
voters before
p_{x}
and those of exactly
\gamma^{i+1}_{h}-\gamma^{i}_{h}
voters at
p_{x+i}
for every
i\in\{0,\ldots,\kappa_{q}-1\}
(ii)
\psi_{q}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})=[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}]
. Note that such construction is possible since
\{p_{x},p_{x+1},\ldots,p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}\}
is feasible at
(\gamma^{0},\gamma^{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\kappa_{q}};\psi_{q})
.

Since the fairness requirement of group
q
is met at
\mathcal{P}_{N}
, there exists
t\in[0,{\kappa_{q}-1}]
such that
\sum_{\{w:\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p_{x+t}\}}{\lambda_{w}}\geq\eta_{q}
. Consider any
\varphi_{w}
such that
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p_{x+t}
.

Since
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p_{x+t}
and
\varphi_{w}
is a GMMR,
p_{x+t}\trianglelefteq\max\{\tau_{\gamma^{t}_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\gamma^{t}_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t}}\}
. But we know that, for any
h\in G
,
\tau_{\gamma^{t}_{h}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{h}})\trianglelefteq p_{x+t-1}
by construction of
\mathcal{P}_{N}
. Therefore,
p_{x+t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t}}
. Since
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p_{x+t}
, there exists
\hat{\gamma}
such that
\max\{\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\hat{\gamma}_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\hat{\gamma}}\}=p_{x+t}
. This is not possible if
\hat{\gamma}_{h}>\gamma^{t+1}_{h}
for some
h\in G
since
\tau_{\gamma^{t+1}_{h}+1}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{h}})\succ p_{x+t}
by the construction of
\mathcal{P}_{N}
. Thus,
\gamma^{t+1}\gg\hat{\gamma}
. Since
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\hat{\gamma}}\trianglelefteq p_{x+t}
, by the definition of group min-max rules,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t+1}}\trianglelefteq p_{x+t}
. Thus it is proved that
p_{x+t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t}}
and
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t+1}}\trianglelefteq p_{x+t}
.

(If:)
Now, we prove the converse direction by considering a random group min-max rule that satisfies the condition in the theorem. We prove that the rule also satisfies
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-strong-GEG. Consider any group
q\in G
and an arbitrary preference profile
\mathcal{P}_{N}
. Let
p_{x}=\min{\{\psi_{q}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})\}}
. For every
h\in G
, set
\gamma^{0}_{h}=|\{i\in N_{h}:\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft p_{x}\}|
. For every
h\in G
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,\kappa_{q}\}
, set
\gamma^{j}_{h}=|\{i\in N_{h}:\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\trianglelefteq p_{x+j-1}\}|
. By the construction,
\gamma^{\kappa_{q}}\gg\ldots\gg\gamma^{1}\gg\gamma^{0}
. Also,
\{p_{x},p_{x+1},\ldots,p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}\}
is feasible at
(\gamma^{0},\gamma^{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\kappa_{q}};\psi_{q})
. Thus, they satisfy all the required conditions. Therefore, there exists
t\in[0,\kappa_{q}-1]
such that the condition in the theorem holds.

Consider any group min-max rule
\varphi_{w}
such that
p_{x+t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t}}
and
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t+1}}\trianglelefteq p_{x+t}
. It is enough to prove that
\varphi_{w}
selects
p_{x+t}
.

For any
\gamma
such that
\gamma_{h}>\gamma^{t}_{h}
for some
h\in G
,
p_{x+t}\trianglelefteq\tau_{\gamma_{h}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{h}})
by the construction of
\gamma^{t}
. Hence for such a
\gamma
,
p_{x+t}\trianglelefteq\max\{\tau_{\gamma_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\gamma_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma}\}
. Now consider any
\gamma
such that
\gamma^{t}\gg\gamma
. By definition of group min-max rule,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t}}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma}
. Since
p_{x+t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t}}
,
p_{x+t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma}
. Therefore, for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
p_{x+t}\trianglelefteq\max\{\tau_{\gamma_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\gamma_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma}\}
. To prove that
\varphi_{w}
selects
p_{x+t}
, it is now enough to prove that there exists a
\gamma\in\Gamma
such that
\max\{\tau_{\gamma_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\gamma_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma}\}=p_{x+t}
.

Case 1:

p_{x+t}
is the top-ranked project of some voter at
\mathcal{P}_{N}
.

Therefore, there exists a group
N_{d}
such that
\tau_{\gamma^{t+1}_{d}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{d}})=p_{x+t}
. By the construction of
\gamma^{t+1}
, for any
h\in G
,
\tau_{\gamma^{t+1}_{h}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{h}})\trianglelefteq p_{x+t}
. We know that
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t+1}}\trianglelefteq p_{x+t}
. Combining all these observations, we have
\max\{\tau_{\gamma^{t+1}_{1}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}}),\ldots,\tau_{\gamma^{t+1}_{g}}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{g}}),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t+1}}\}=p_{x+t}
.

Case 2:

p_{x+t}
is not a top-ranked project for any voter.

By construction of
\mathcal{P}_{N}
, exactly
\gamma^{t+1}_{h}-\gamma^{t}_{h}
voters of every group
N_{h}
have their top-ranked project at
p_{x+t}
. Therefore,
\gamma^{t+1}=\gamma^{t}
. Since
p_{x+t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t}}
and
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t+1}}\trianglelefteq p_{x+t}
,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t}}=\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{\gamma^{t+1}}=p_{x+t}
.
Thus, either way,
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p_{x+t}
.

6.8
Individual-Fairness Notions

When each group has exactly one voter (i.e.,
G=N
), clearly, the concept of group-wise anonymity is not relevant as any RSCR satisfies it trivially. In this case, the most natural and reasonable compliant representation scenario is that in which every representative function
\psi_{i}
selects top
\kappa_{i}
projects of voter
i
. We now define two fairness notions that are special cases of our
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-weak-GEG and
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-strong-GEG notions where
G=N
and the representation scenario is as described above.
\boldsymbol{(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})}
-
weak-IEG
(weak individual entitlement guarantee) ensures that the top
\kappa_{i}
projects of every voter
i
together receive a probability of at least
\eta_{i}
while
\boldsymbol{(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})}
-
strong-IEG
ensures that at least one project in the top
\kappa_{i}
projects of each voter
i
receives a probability of
\eta_{i}
.

Definition 6.8.42
.

A RSCR
\varphi
satisfies
\boldsymbol{(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})}
-
weak-IEG
if for any voter
i\in N
and any
\mathcal{P}_{N}\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
,

\varphi_{U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\geq\eta_{i}.

Definition 6.8.43
.

A RSCR
\varphi
satisfies
\boldsymbol{(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})}
-
strong-IEG
if for any voter
i\in N
and any
\mathcal{P}_{N}\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
, there exists
p\in U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})
such that

\varphi_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\geq\eta_{i}.

6.9
Direct Characterizations (DC) of Individual-Fair Rules

We may recall from
Section

6.4.3.1
that all the unanimous and strategy-proof RSCRs are characterized to be probabilistic fixed ballot rules whose definition is presented below. Let
S(t;\mathcal{P}_{N})
denote
\{i\in N:\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\trianglelefteq p_{t}\}
.

Definition 6.9.44
.

A RSCR
\varphi
on
\mathcal{D}^{n}
is said to be a
probabilistic fixed ballot rule (PFBR)
if there is a collection
\{\delta_{S}\}_{S\subseteq N}
of probability distributions satisfying the following two properties:

1.

Ballot Unanimity:

\delta_{\emptyset}(p_{m})=1
and
\delta_{N}(p_{1})=1
, and

2.

Monotonicity
: for all
p_{t}\in P
,
S\subset T\subseteq N\implies\delta_{S}([p_{1},p_{t}])\leq\delta_{T}([p_{1},p_{t}])

such that for all
\mathcal{P}_{N}\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
and
p_{t}\in P
, we have

\varphi_{p_{t}}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=\delta_{S(t;\mathcal{P}_{N})}([p_{1},p_{t}])-\delta_{S(t-1;\mathcal{P}_{N})}([p_{1},p_{t-1}]);

where
\delta_{S(0;\mathcal{P}_{N})}([p_{1},p_{0}])=0
.

As interpreted in
Observation

6.1
, when the groups are singleton, PFBRs and PFGBRs become equivalent since each element in
\Gamma
becomes a
n
-dimensional binary vector that can be directly interpreted as a subset of voters. In the subsequent sections, we present direct characterizations of the probabilistic fixed ballot rules that satisfy
\boldsymbol{(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})}
-
weak-IEG
and
\boldsymbol{(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})}
-
strong-IEG
. For this, we crucially use the characterization results for PFGBRs that satisfy
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-weak-GEG (
Theorem

6.6.26
) and
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-strong-GEG (
Theorem

6.6.28
).

6.9.1
DC: Weak-IEG

We characterize the probabilistic fixed ballot rules that satisfy our weak-IEG and strong-IEG notions. The notion of
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-weak-IEG ensures that the top
\kappa_{i}
projects of a voter
i
collectively receive a probability of at least
\eta_{i}
. The characterization of rules satisfying this notion follows as a corollary from
Theorem

6.6.26
, where every voter is considered to be a separate group.

Corollary 6.9.45
.

A RSCR
\varphi:\mathcal{D}^{n}\to\Delta P
is unanimous, strategy-proof, and satisfies
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-weak-IEG if and only if it is a probabilistic fixed ballot rule such that for all
S_{2}\subseteq S_{1}\subseteq N
with
|S_{1}|\geq 1
, for all
i\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2}
, and for all
x\in[1,{m-\kappa_{i}+1}]
, we have

\delta_{S_{1}}([p_{1},p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}])-\delta_{S_{2}}([p_{1},p_{x-1}])\geq\eta_{i}.

The proof follows from
Theorem

6.6.26
where
\gamma
is a binary vector corresponding to the subset
S_{1}
,
\gamma^{\prime}
is a binary vector corresponding to the subset
S_{2}
, and
\psi_{N}
is fixed to be the representation scenario that chooses top
\kappa_{i}
projects for every voter
i
.

6.9.2
DC: Strong-IEG

The notion of
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-strong-IEG ensures that some project in the top
\kappa_{i}
projects receives a probability of at least
\eta_{i}
. Therefore, we ensure that there cannot be
\kappa_{i}
consecutive projects which are allotted a probability less than
\eta_{i}
each.
The characterization of rules satisfying this notion follows as a corollary from
Theorem

6.6.28
, where every voter is considered to be a separate group.

Corollary 6.9.46
.

A RSCR
\varphi:\mathcal{D}^{n}\to\Delta P
is unanimous, strategy-proof, and satisfies
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-strong-IEG if and only if it is a probabilistic fixed ballot rule such that for all
S_{2}\subseteq S_{1}\subseteq N
with
|S_{1}|\geq 1
, for all
i\in S_{1}\setminus S_{2}
, and for all
p_{t},p_{u}
such that
p_{t}\triangleleft p_{u}
, it holds that

\delta_{S_{1}}([p_{1},p_{j}])-\delta_{S_{2}}([p_{1},p_{j-1}])\!<\eta_{i}\;\forall p_{j}\in[p_{t},p_{u}]\;\implies\;u-t\leq\kappa_{i}.

The proof follows from
Theorem

6.6.28
where
\gamma
is a binary vector corresponding to the subset
S_{1}
,
\gamma^{\prime}
is a binary vector corresponding to the subset
S_{2}
, and
\psi_{N}
is fixed to be the representation scenario that chooses top
\kappa_{i}
projects for every voter
i
.

6.10
Extreme Point Characterizations (EPC) of Individual-Fair Rules

We may recall from
Section

6.4.3.2
that all the unanimous and strategy-proof RSCRs are characterized to be random min-max rules, which are the convex combinations of min-max rules defined below.

Definition 6.10.47
.

A DSCR
f
on
\mathcal{D}^{n}
is a
min-max
rule if for all
S\subseteq N
, there exists
\beta_{S}\in P
satisfying

\beta_{\emptyset}=p_{m},\beta_{N}=p_{1},\mbox{ and }\beta_{T}\trianglelefteq\beta_{S}\mbox{ for all }S\subseteq T

such that

f(\mathcal{P}_{N})=\min_{S\subseteq N}\left[\max_{i\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right),\beta_{S}\}\right].

In the subsequent sections, we first present extreme point characterizations of the random min-max rules that satisfy
\boldsymbol{(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})}
-
weak-IEG
and
\boldsymbol{(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})}
-
strong-IEG
. Followed by it, we also present characterizations of the rules that additionally satisfy another property called
anonymity
.

6.10.1
EPC: Weak-IEG

As we saw in
Section

6.8
, the notion of
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-weak-IEG ensures that the top
\kappa_{i}
projects of a voter
i
collectively receive a probability of at least
\eta_{i}
. Below, we characterize the random min-max rules that satisfy
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-weak-IEG.

Theorem 6.10.48
.

A RSCR
\varphi:\mathcal{D}^{n}\to\Delta P
is unanimous, strategy-proof, and satisfies
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-weak-IEG if and only if it is a random min-max rule
\varphi=\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}\varphi_{w}
such that for all
S_{1},S_{2}\subseteq N
with
S_{1}\cap S_{2}=\emptyset
and
|S_{2}|\geq 1
, for all
i\in S_{2}
, and for all
x\in[1,{m-\kappa_{i}+1}]
,

\sum_{\{w\;\mid\;p_{x}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}},\;\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}\}}{\lambda_{w}}\geq\eta_{i}

Proof 6.10.49
.

(Only if:)
We first prove that for a random min-max rule to satisfy
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-weak-IEG, the above condition must be satisfied. Consider any
S_{1},S_{2},i,
and
x
as given in the theorem. Construct a profile
\mathcal{P}_{N}
as follows: (i)
U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})=[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}]
, (ii)
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft p_{x}
for all
j\in S_{1}
, (iii)
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\in[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}]
for all
j\in S_{2}
, and (iv)
p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}\triangleleft\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)
for all
j\notin S_{1}\cup S_{2}
.

Since the fairness requirement of voter
i
is met at
\mathcal{P}_{N}
,
\sum_{\{w:\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\in[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}]\}}{\lambda_{w}}\geq\eta_{i}
. Consider any
w
such that
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\in[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}]
. Since
\varphi_{w}
is a min-max rule, for any
S\subseteq N
,
\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}\succeq\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\succeq p_{x}
. This implies
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}}\succeq p_{x}
since
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft p_{x}
for any
j\in S_{1}
. Similarly, since
\varphi_{w}
is a min-max rule, there exists
S\subseteq N
such that
\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}=\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})
. For any
S\nsubseteq(S_{1}\cup S_{2})
, this is not possible since
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\succ p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}
for any
j\notin S_{1}\cup S_{2}
and
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}
. Therefore, there exists
S\subseteq(S_{1}\cup S_{2})
such that
\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}=\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})
. By the definition of min-max rules,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}
. This implies,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\trianglelefteq\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}
. Hence, we have
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}}
and
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}
.

(If:)

Consider any arbitrary voter
i
. Set
S_{1}=\{j\in N:\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft\min U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})\}
and
S_{2}=\{j\in N:\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\in U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})\}
. Observe that
S_{1}\cap S_{2}=\emptyset
by construction, and
|S_{2}|\geq 1
since
i\in S_{2}
. Set
x
such that
p_{x}=\min U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})
. Since
|U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})|=\kappa_{i}
,
x\in[1,m-\kappa_{i}+1]
. Hence,
S_{1},S_{2},i,
and
x
satisfy all the required conditions.

Consider any min-max rule
\varphi_{w}
such that
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}}
and
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}
. Since
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}
and also
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}
for any
j\in(S_{1}\cup S_{2})
by construction,
\max\limits_{j\in S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\}\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}
. This implies,
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}
. By construction,
p_{x}\triangleleft\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)
for any
j\notin S_{1}
. This implies, for any
S\nsubseteq S_{1}
,
p_{x}\triangleleft\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}
. Since
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}}
, by definition of min-max rule,
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}
for any
S\subseteq S_{1}
. This implies,
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}
for any
S\subseteq S_{1}
. Therefore, for any
S\subseteq N
,
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}
. This implies,
p_{x}\trianglelefteq\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})
. Combining this with
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}
gives
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\in U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})
. Fairness requirement of
i
is met and this completes the proof.

6.10.2
EPC: Strong-IEG

As we saw in
Section

6.8
, the notion of
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-strong-IEG ensures that some project in top
\kappa_{i}
projects of a voter
i
receives a probability of at least
\eta_{i}
. Below, we characterize the random min-max rules that satisfy
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-strong-IEG.

Theorem 6.10.50
.

A RSCR
\varphi:\mathcal{D}^{n}\to\Delta P
is unanimous, strategy-proof, and satisfies
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-strong-IEG if and only if it is a random min-max rule
\varphi=\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}\varphi_{w}
such that for all
S_{1},S_{2}\subseteq N
with
S_{1}\cap S_{2}=\emptyset
and
|S_{2}|\geq 1
, for all
i\in S_{2}
, for all
x\in[1,{m-\kappa_{i}+1}]
, and for all functions
f:S_{2}\to[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}]
, at least one of the following conditions holds:

(C1)

there exists
b_{t}\in range(f)
such that

\sum_{\left\{w\;\mid\;b_{t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\triangleleft b_{t}\}}\;,\;\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\trianglelefteq b_{t}\}}\trianglelefteq b_{t}\right\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{i}

(C2)

there exists
c\in[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}]\setminus range(f)
such that

\sum_{\left\{w\;\mid\;\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\trianglelefteq u\}}=c\right\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{i},

where
u=p_{x-1}
if
c\triangleleft\min(range(f))
,
u=p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}
if
c\succ\max(range(f))
, and else
u=p_{l}
such that
p_{l}\triangleleft c\triangleleft p_{l+1}
and
[p_{l},p_{l+1}]\cap range(f)=\{p_{l},p_{l+1}\}
.

Proof 6.10.51
.

(Only if:)
Consider any
S_{1},S_{2},i,x,
and
f
as given in the theorem. Construct a profile
\mathcal{P}_{N}
as follows: (i)
U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})=[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}]
, (ii)
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft p_{x}
for all
j\in S_{1}
, (iii)
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)=f(j)
for all
j\in S_{2}
, and (iv)
p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}\triangleleft\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)
for all
j\notin S_{1}\cup S_{2}
. Since the fairness requirement of
i
is met at
\mathcal{P}_{N}
, there exists
p\in[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}]
such that
\sum_{\{w:\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p\}}{\lambda_{w}}\geq\eta_{i}
. Consider any
\varphi_{w}
such that
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p
.

Case 1:

p\in range(f)
.

Let
S=S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\triangleleft p\}
. Since by construction
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft p_{x}
for any
j\in S_{1}
,
\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\}\triangleleft p
. Since
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p
and
\varphi_{w}
is a min-max rule,
p\trianglelefteq\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}
. This implies
S\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}
. Since
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p
, there exists some
S^{\prime}\subseteq N
such that
\max_{j\in S^{\prime}}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S^{\prime}}\}=p
. This is not possible if
S^{\prime}\nsubseteq S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\trianglelefteq p\}
since
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\succ p
for any
j\notin(S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\trianglelefteq p\})
. So, there exists
S^{\prime}\subseteq S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\trianglelefteq p\}
such that
\max_{j\in S^{\prime}}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S^{\prime}}\}=p
. This implies,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S^{\prime}}\trianglelefteq p
. By the definition of min-max rule,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\trianglelefteq p\}}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S^{\prime}}\trianglelefteq p
. Hence, (C1) holds for
b_{t}=p
.

Case 2:

p\notin range(f)
.

Case 2.1:
p\triangleleft\min(range(f))
.

Since
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p
and
\varphi_{w}
is a min-max rule,
p\trianglelefteq\max_{j\in S_{1}}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}}\}
. This implies
p\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}}
since
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft p_{x}
for any
j\in S_{1}
. Since
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p
, there exists
S\subseteq N
such that
\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}=p
. Since
p\triangleleft\min(range(f))
,
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\succ p
for any
j\notin S_{1}
. Therefore,
S\subseteq S_{1}
. Since
\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}=p
and
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft p
for any
j\in S
,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\trianglelefteq p
. By the definition of min-max rule,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\trianglelefteq p
. Combining this with
p\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}}
implies (C2) is satisfied with
c=p
and
u=p_{x-1}
.

Case 2.2:
\max(range(f))\triangleleft p
.

Since
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p
and
\varphi_{w}
is a min-max rule,
p\trianglelefteq\max_{j\in S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\}
. This implies
p\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}
since
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft p_{x}
for any
j\in S_{1}
and
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft\max(range(f))\triangleleft p
for any
j\in S_{2}
. Since
\varphi_{w}
is a min-max rule, there exists
S\subseteq N
such that
\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}=p
. Since
p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}\triangleleft\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)
for any
j\notin(S_{1}\cup S_{2})
, there exists
S\subseteq(S_{1}\cup S_{2})
such that
\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}=p
. Since
\max(range(f))\triangleleft p
,
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft p
for any
j\in S
. This implies,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}=p
. So, by the definition of min-max rule,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\trianglelefteq p
. Combining this with
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\succeq p
implies (C2) is satisfied with
c=p
and
u=p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}
.

Case 2.3:
p
lies in between two projects in
range(f)
.

Select
b_{l},b_{l+1}\in range(f)
such that
b_{l}\triangleleft p\triangleleft b_{l+1}
and
[b_{l},b_{l+1}]\cap range(f)=\{b_{l},b_{l+1}\}
. Let
S=S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\trianglelefteq b_{l}\}
. Since
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p
and
\varphi_{w}
is a min-max rule,
p\trianglelefteq\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}
. This implies
p\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}
since
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\trianglelefteq b_{l}\triangleleft p
for any
j\in S
. Since
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p
, there exists
S^{\prime}\subseteq N
such that
\max_{j\in S^{\prime}}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S^{\prime}}\}=p
. This is not possible if
S^{\prime}\nsubseteq S
since
p\triangleleft b_{l+1}\trianglelefteq\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)
for all
j\notin S
. This implies, there exists
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
such that
\max_{j\in S^{\prime}}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S^{\prime}}\}=p
. Since
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\trianglelefteq b_{l}\triangleleft p
for any
j\in S
,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S^{\prime}}=p
. By the definition of median rule,
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S^{\prime}}=p
. Combining this with
p\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}
implies (C2) is satisfied with
c=p
and
u=b_{l}
.

(If:)
Consider any arbitrary voter
i
. Set
S_{1}=\{j\in N:\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft\min U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})\}
and
S_{2}=\{j\in N:\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\in U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})\}
. Observe that
S_{1}\cap S_{2}=\emptyset
by construction, and
|S_{2}|\geq 1
since
i\in S_{2}
. Set
x
such that
p_{x}=\min U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})
. Since
|U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})|=\kappa_{i}
,
x\in[1,m-\kappa_{i}+1]
. Define a function
f
such that
f(j)=\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)
for every
j\in S_{2}
. Hence,
S_{1},S_{2},i,x,
and
f
satisfy all the required conditions.

suppose (C1) holds for some
b_{t}
as given. Take a
\varphi_{w}
such that
b_{t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\triangleleft b_{t}\}}
and
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\trianglelefteq b_{t}\}}\trianglelefteq b_{t}
. For any
j\notin(S_{1}\cup S_{2})
,
b_{t}\trianglelefteq p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}\triangleleft\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)
. For any
j\in S_{2}
such that
b_{t}\trianglelefteq f(j)
,
b_{t}\trianglelefteq\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)
by construction. From
b_{t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\triangleleft b_{t}\}}
, any set
S\subseteq(S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\triangleleft b_{t}\})
will have
b_{t}\trianglelefteq\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}
. Combining all the above together, we have, for all
S\subseteq N
,
b_{t}\trianglelefteq\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}
. Since
b_{t}\in range(f)
, there exists
j\in S_{2}
such that
f(j)=b_{t}
. Since
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\trianglelefteq b_{t}\}}\trianglelefteq b_{t}
, this implies,
\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}=b_{t}
for
S=(S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\triangleleft b_{t}\})
. Hence,
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=b_{t}
. Fairness requirement of
i
is met.

suppose (C2) holds for some
c
and
u
as given in the theorem. If
u=p_{x-1}
,
S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\trianglelefteq u\}=S_{1}
. Consider any
\varphi_{w}
such that
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}}=c
. Then,
\max_{j\in S_{1}}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}}\}=c
since
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft p_{x}
for all
j\in S_{1}
by construction. Since
c\triangleleft\min(range(f))
,
c\triangleleft\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)
for any
j\notin S_{1}
. This implies,
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=c
. Else if
u=p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}
,
S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\trianglelefteq u\}=S_{1}\cup S_{2}
. Consider any
\varphi_{w}
such that
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}=c
. Then,
\max_{j\in S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S_{1}\cup S_{2}}\}=c
. For any
j\notin S_{1}\cup S_{2}
,
c\triangleleft\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)
by construction. This implies,
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=c
. Finally, suppose
u=b_{l}
. Let
S=S_{1}\cup\{j\in S_{2}:f(j)\trianglelefteq u\}
. For any
j\in S
,
\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\trianglelefteq b_{l}\triangleleft c
. Since
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}=c
,
\max_{j\in S}\{\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{S}\}=c
. For any
j\notin S
,
c\triangleleft b_{l+1}\trianglelefteq\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)
by construction. This implies,
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=c
. Hence,
c
is allocated at least
\eta_{i}
. Fairness requirement of
i
is met and this completes the proof.

6.10.3
Total Anonymity of the Voters

Up until now, we discussed the rules which are not necessarily anonymous across the groups. We now look at a more restricted special case where, in addition to groups having exactly one voter, anonymity across all the voters is required. This requirement is referred to as
anonymity
of RSCR in the literature (i.e., permutation of the preferences of the voters do not change the outcome). This property is formally defined below. Let
\Sigma
be the set of all permutations on
N
. For
\sigma\in\Sigma
and
\mathcal{P}_{N}\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
, we define
\mathcal{P}^{\sigma}_{N}
as
(\succ_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,\succ_{\sigma(n)})
.

Definition 6.10.52
.

A RSCR
\varphi:\mathcal{D}^{n}\to\Delta P
is said to be
anonymous
if for all
\mathcal{P}_{N}\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
and all
\sigma\in\Sigma
, we have

\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})=\varphi(\mathcal{P}^{\sigma}_{N}).

6.10.3.1
EPC: Total Anonymity

All the unanimous, strategy-proof, and anonymous RSCRs are characterized to be random median rules
[
71
]
. In fact, as remarked in
Observation

6.1
, when a single group has all the voters, our probabilistic fixed group ballot rules become equivalent to random median rules. Random median rules are convex combinations of DSCRs called median rules
[
63
]
. A median rule operates by selecting the median of all top-ranked projects of the voters and additional
n+1
dummy projects fixed apriori as parameters by the PB organizer.

Definition 6.10.53
.

A DSCR
f
on
\mathcal{D}^{n}
is a
median
rule if there are
n+1
(dummy) projects
p_{1}=\beta^{f}_{0}\!\trianglelefteq\!\beta^{f}_{1}\!\trianglelefteq\!\cdots\!\trianglelefteq\!\beta^{f}_{n-1}\!\trianglelefteq\!\beta^{f}_{n}=p_{m}
such that for any profile
\mathcal{P}_{N}
,
f
selects

\mathsf{med}\!\left(\!\succ_{1}(1),\ldots,\succ_{n}(1),\beta^{f}_{0},\beta^{f}_{1},\ldots,\beta^{f}_{n}\!\right).

Example 6.10.54
.

Consider the scenario in
Example

6.4.8
. As there are four voters, a median rule
\varphi
must have three parameters as
\beta^{\varphi}_{1}=p_{1}
,
\beta^{\varphi}_{2}=p_{2}
, and
\beta^{\varphi}_{3}=p_{2}
(
\beta^{\varphi}_{0}
and
\beta^{\varphi}_{4}
are always fixed at
p_{1}
and
p_{3}
respectively). The median rule
\varphi
selects the median of the set
\{p_{1},p_{3},p_{2},p_{3},p_{1},p_{1},p_{2},p_{2},p_{3}\}
, which is
p_{2}
.

A
random median rule
is a convex combination of median rules. Using this, we now present an extreme point characterization of the unanimous, strategy-proof, and anonymous RSCRs that satisfy our
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-weak-IEG and
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-weak-IEG notions.

6.10.3.2
EPC: Weak-IEG and Total Anonymity

The notion of
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-weak-IEG ensures that the top
\kappa_{i}
projects of a voter
i
collectively receive a probability of at least
\eta_{i}
. Our characterization states that for any
\kappa_{i}
-sized interval
I
of
P
and any
r\in[0,n-1]
, the total probability given to the median rules having some element of
I
in between their
r
-th and
(r+1)
-th parameters must be at least
\eta_{i}
. Let
\mathcal{I}_{\kappa}
denote all the intervals of size
\kappa
. Before we present the characterization, we prove a necessary lemma.

Lemma 6.10.55
.

For any median rule
\varphi
, a single-peaked profile
\mathcal{P}_{N}
, and a voter
i
such that
|\{j\in N\mid\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\trianglelefteq\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\}|=r
,

\big[\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r},\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r+1}\big]\cap U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})\neq\emptyset\implies\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})\in U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i}).

Proof 6.10.56
.

If
\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\in\big[\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r},\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r+1}\big]
, then by the definition of a median rule,
\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})=\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)
. Thus, the claim holds. Now suppose
\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r}
. By the definition of a median rule this means,
\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r}
and
\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})
. Combining these two observations, we have
\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r}
. Additionally, as
\big[\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r},\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r+1}\big]\cap U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})\neq\emptyset
and
\succ_{i}
is single-peaked with
\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r}
, it follows that
[\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right),\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r}]\subseteq U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})
. This, together with
\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r}
, implies
\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})\in U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})
.

Finally, suppose
\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r+1}\triangleleft\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)
. By the definition of a median rule this means,
\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r+1}\trianglelefteq\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})\triangleleft\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)
. Moreover, as
\big[\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r},\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r+1}\big]\cap U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})\neq\emptyset
and
\succ_{i}
is single-peaked with
\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r+1}\triangleleft\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)
, we have
[\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r+1},\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)]\subseteq U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})
. All the above observations imply that
\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})\in U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})
.

We are now ready to characterize all the fair rules.

Theorem 6.10.57
.

A RSCR
\varphi:\mathcal{D}^{n}\to\Delta P
is unanimous, strategy-proof, anonymous, and satisfies
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-weak-IEG if and only if it is a random median rule
\varphi=\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}\varphi_{w}
such that for all
r\in[0,n-1]
, all
i\in N
, and all
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\kappa_{i}}
,

\sum_{\big\{w\;\mid\;\big[\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{r},\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{r+1}\big]\cap I\neq\emptyset\big\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{i}.

Proof 6.10.58
.

(Only if:)
We first show that any random median rule
\varphi=\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}\varphi_{w}
satisfying
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-weak-IEG also satisfies the condition in the theorem. Take
r\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
,
i\in N
, and
x\in\{1,\ldots,m-\kappa_{i}+1\}
. Consider a profile
\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{N}
such that (a) the top-ranked project of exactly
n-r-1
voters is
p_{1}
, (b)
U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})=[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}]
, and (c)
p_{m}
is the top-ranked project of the remaining voters. By the definition of a median rule, for all
w\in W
,

\varphi_{w}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{N})\in[\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{r},\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{r+1}].

(6.2)

Moreover, since
\varphi
satisfies
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-weak-IEG, we have

\sum_{\big\{w\in W\mid\varphi_{w}(\hat{\mathcal{P}}_{N})\in[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}]\big\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{i}.

(6.3)

Combining
Equation

6.2
and
Equation

6.3
, we have

\sum_{\big\{w\in W\mid\big[\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{r},\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{r+1}\big]\cap\big[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{i}-1}\big]\neq\emptyset\big\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{i}.

(If:)
Suppose a random median
\varphi=\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}\varphi_{w}
satisfies the condition in the theorem. We show that
\varphi
is
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-weak-IEG. Take
\mathcal{P}_{N}\in\mathcal{D}^{n}
and a voter
i\in N
. Set
r=|\{j\in N\mid\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\trianglelefteq\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\}|
. Since
\succ_{i}
is single-peaked,
U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})\in\mathcal{I}_{\kappa_{i}}
. Let
U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})=[p_{t},p_{t+\kappa_{i}-1}]
for some
p_{t}\in P
. This implies
t\in[1,m-\kappa_{i}+1]
. Moreover, as
r\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
n-r\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
. Therefore, by the condition of the theorem, we have

\sum_{\big\{w\mid\big[\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-r},\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-r+1}\big]\cap\big[p_{t},p_{t+\kappa_{i}-1}\big]\neq\emptyset\big\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{i}.

By Lemma
6.10.55
,
\big[\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-r},\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-r+1}\big]\cap U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})\neq\emptyset
implies
\varphi^{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\in U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})
. Combining these observations together, we have
\varphi_{U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\geq\eta_{i}
. Thus, the fairness requirement of voter
i
is met.

6.10.3.3
EPC: Strong-IEG and Total Anonymity

Our
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-strong-IEG ensures that some project in the top
\kappa_{i}
projects receives a probability of at least
\eta_{i}
.

Theorem 6.10.59
.

A RSCR
\varphi:\mathcal{D}^{n}\to\Delta P
is unanimous, strategy-proof, anonymous, and
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-strong-IEG if and only if it is a random median rule
\varphi=\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}\varphi_{w}
such that for all
r\in[1,n]
, all
s\in[0,n-r]
, all
i\in N
, all
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\kappa_{i}}
, and all
\{b_{r},\ldots,b_{r+s}\}\subseteq I
with
b_{r}\trianglelefteq\cdots\trianglelefteq b_{r+s}
, at least one of the following holds:

(C1)

there exists
c\in\{b_{r},\ldots,b_{r+s}\}
such that

\sum_{\{w\;\mid\;\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-t}\trianglelefteq b_{t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-t+1}\mbox{ for some }b_{t}=c\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{i},

(C2)

there exists
c\in I\setminus\{b_{r},\ldots,b_{r+s}\}
such that

\sum_{\{w\;\mid\;\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-u}=c\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{i},

where
u=r-1
if
c\triangleleft b_{r}
,
u=r+s
if
b_{r+s}\triangleleft c
, and else
u
is such that
b_{u}\triangleleft c\triangleleft b_{u+1}
.

Proof 6.10.60
.

(Only if:)
Let
\varphi=\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}\varphi_{w}
be a
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-strong-IEG random median rule. We show that it satisfies (C1) and (C2). Take
r\in[1,n]
,
s\in[0,n-r]
,
i\in N
,
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\kappa_{i}}
, and
\{b_{r},\ldots,b_{r+s}\}\subseteq I
with
b_{r}\trianglelefteq\cdots\trianglelefteq b_{r+s}
. Construct a preference profile
\mathcal{P}_{N}
such that (i)
p_{1}
is the top-ranked project of exactly
r-1
voters, (ii)
U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})=I
, (iii)
b_{r},\ldots,b_{r+s}
are the top-ranked projects of any
s+1
voters including
i
, and (iv)
p_{m}
is the top-ranked project of the remaining voters. Since
\varphi
is
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-strong-IEG, there exists a project
p\in I
such that
\varphi_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\geq\eta_{i}
. This, together with
\varphi=\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}\varphi_{w}
, implies,

\sum_{\{w\mid\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{i}.

(6.4)

Suppose
p\in\{b_{r},\ldots,b_{r+s}\}
. This means there exist
s_{1},s_{2}
with
0\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq s
such that
b_{r+s_{1}}=\cdots=b_{r+s_{2}}=p
. Let
\hat{\varphi}
be a median rule such that
\hat{\varphi}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p
. We claim there exists
t\in[s_{1},s_{2}]
such that
\beta^{\hat{\varphi}}_{n-r-t}\trianglelefteq p\trianglelefteq\beta^{\hat{\varphi}}_{n-r-t+1}
. Suppose not. Then, either
\beta^{\hat{\varphi}}_{n-r-s_{1}+1}\triangleleft p
or
p\triangleleft\beta^{\hat{\varphi}}_{n-r-s_{2}}
. Assume
\beta^{\hat{\varphi}}_{n-r-s_{1}+1}\triangleleft p
. This means
p_{1}\triangleleft p
. Moreover, at least
r+s_{1}-1
voters have top-ranked projects before
p
. Combining these observations with the definition of a median rule, we have
\hat{\varphi}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\triangleleft p
, which is a contradiction. Similarly, if
p\triangleleft\beta^{\hat{\varphi}}_{n-r-s_{2}}
, we can show that
p\triangleleft\hat{\varphi}(\mathcal{P}_{N})
. Therefore, there exists
t\in[s_{1},s_{2}]
such that
\beta^{\hat{\varphi}}_{n-r-t}\trianglelefteq a\trianglelefteq\beta^{\hat{\varphi}}_{n-r-t+1}
. This, together with
Equation

6.4
, implies

\sum_{\{w\;\mid\;\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-t}\trianglelefteq b_{t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-t+1}\mbox{ for some }b_{t}=a\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{i}.

Hence, (C1) holds with
c=p
.

Now suppose
p\in I\setminus\{b_{r},\ldots,b_{r+s}\}
. This implies either
p\in[\min I,b_{r})
, or
p\in(b_{r+s},\max I]
, or there exists
b_{r+t}\in I
such that
b_{r+t}\triangleleft p\triangleleft b_{r+t+1}
. Suppose
p\in[\min I,b_{r})
. Since
r>1
implies there are exactly
r-1
voters with top-ranked projects before
I
, for any median rule
\varphi
with
\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p
, we have
\beta^{\varphi}_{n-r+1}=p
. This, together with
Equation

6.4
, implies

\sum_{\{w\;\mid\;\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-r+1}=p\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{i},

Thus, (C2) holds for
u=r-1
and
c=p
. Similarly, if
p\in(b_{r+s},\max I]
, then (C2) holds for
u=r+s
and
c=p
. Finally, if there exists
b_{r+t}\in I
such that
b_{r+t}\triangleleft p\triangleleft b_{r+t+1}
, then
\varphi_{w}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=p
implies
\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-r-t}=p
. Therefore, (C2) holds for
u=r+t
and
c=p
.

(If:)
Let
\varphi=\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}\varphi_{w}
be a random median rule such that for all
r\in[1,n]
, all
s\in[0,n-r]
, all
i\in N
, all
I\in\mathcal{I}_{\kappa_{i}}
, and all
\{b_{r},\ldots,b_{r+s}\}\subseteq I
with
b_{r}\trianglelefteq\cdots\trianglelefteq b_{r+s}
, at least one of (C1) and (C2) holds. We show that
\varphi
is
(\kappa_{N},\eta_{N})
-strong-IEG. Take a preference profile
\mathcal{P}_{N}
and an arbitrary voter
i
. We have to show that

\exists p\in U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})\mbox{ such that }\varphi_{p}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\geq\eta_{i}.

(6.5)

Let
r=|j\in N:\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\triangleleft\min{U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})}|+1
,
T=\{j\in N:\operatorname{\succ_{j}\!}\left(1\right)\in U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})\}
, and
s=|T|-1
. Let
I=U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})
. Let
b_{r},\ldots,b_{r+s}
be the top-ranked projects of voters in
T
(sorted in increasing order, with repetition). Now, for these values, at least one of (C1) and (C2) holds.
Suppose (C1) holds. This means that there exists
c\in\{b_{r},\ldots,b_{r+s}\}
such that

\sum_{\{w\;\mid\;\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-t}\trianglelefteq b_{t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-t+1}\mbox{ for some }b_{t}=c\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{i}.

By the definition of a median rule, for all
\hat{\varphi}\in\{\varphi_{w}\mid w\in W\mbox{ and }\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-t}\trianglelefteq b_{t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-t+1}\mbox{ for some }b_{t}=c\}
, we have
\hat{\varphi}(\mathcal{P}_{N})=c
. Therefore, by (C1),
\varphi_{c}(\mathcal{P}_{N})\geq\eta_{i}
. Hence,
Equation

6.5
holds. Now suppose (C2) holds. This means there exists
c\in U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})\setminus\{b_{r},\ldots,b_{r+s}\}
such that

\sum_{\{w\;\mid\;\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-u}=c\}}\lambda_{w}\geq\eta_{i},

where
u=r-1
if
c\triangleleft b_{r}
,
u=r+s
if
b_{r+s}\triangleleft c
, and else
u
is such that
b_{u}\triangleleft c\triangleleft b_{u+1}
. First assume
u=r-1
. Since
\min U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})\trianglelefteq c\triangleleft b_{r}
and exactly
r-1
voters have their top-ranked project before
\min U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})
, for any
\varphi\in\{\varphi_{w}\mid w\in W\mbox{ and }\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-p+1}=c\}
,
\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})=c
. Hence,
Equation

6.5
holds with
p=c
. Now assume
u=r+s
where
b_{r+s}\triangleleft c
. Since
b_{r+s}\triangleleft c\trianglelefteq\max U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})
and exactly
n-r-s
voters have their top-ranked projects after
\max U(\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(\kappa_{i}\right),\succ_{i})
, we have for all
\varphi\in\{\varphi_{w}\mid w\in W\mbox{ and }\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-p-q}=c\}
,
\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})=c
. Hence,
Equation

6.5
holds with
p=c
. Finally assume,
u
is such that
b_{u}\triangleleft c\triangleleft b_{u+1}
. Since the number of voters with their top-ranked projects before
c
is exactly
u
, for all
\varphi\in\{\varphi_{w}\mid w\in W\mbox{ and }\beta^{\varphi_{w}}_{n-q}=c\}
,
\varphi(\mathcal{P}_{N})=c
. Hence,
Equation

6.5
holds with
p=c
.

6.11
Compliant Representation Scenarios Satisfying Additional Desiderata

Let us recall that a representation scenario
\psi_{G}
is said to be compliant with representative ranges
\kappa_{G}
, if every representative function
\psi_{q}
is top-ranged and the outcome of
\psi_{q}
is always
\kappa_{q}
-sized interval (
Definition

6.5.17
). In this section, we discuss some representation scenarios that are compliant with
\kappa_{G}
and also satisfy some additional desirable properties.

Given a parameter
k
and the preferences of voters over the projects, the problem of multi-winner voting is to aggregate these preferences and choose exactly
k
projects. This is very well studied in computer science and economics literature
[
40
]
. Each representative function
\psi_{q}
can be viewed as a multi-winner voting rule with parameter
\kappa_{q}
. Motivated by this, we impose some well studied properties of multi-winner voting rules on the representative functions in the representation scenario, in addition to ensuring that it is complaint with
\kappa_{G}
. The first property we look at is anonymity, which ensures that the permutation of preferences of voters in a group does not change the representatives chosen for that group.

Definition 6.11.61
.

A representative function
\psi_{q}:\mathcal{D}^{|N_{q}|}\to{2^{P}}
is said to be anonymous if for any permutation
\pi
of
N_{q}
and
\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}\in\mathcal{D}^{|N_{q}|}
, we have
\psi_{q}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})=\psi_{q}(\mathcal{P}_{\pi(N_{q})})
where
\mathcal{P}_{\pi(N)}=(\succ_{\pi(1)},\ldots,\succ_{\pi(n)})
. A representation scenario is said to be
anonymous
if
\psi_{q}
is anonymous for every
q\in G
.

The next property we define requires that for any group
N_{q}
and a profile
\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}
, there exists a voter whose top ranked project is selected by
\psi_{q}
.

Definition 6.11.62
.

A representative function
\psi_{q}:\mathcal{D}^{|N_{q}|}\to{2^{P}}
is said to be top-containing if for any
\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}\in\mathcal{D}^{|N_{q}|}
, there exists
i\in N_{q}
such that
\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)\in\psi_{q}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})
. A representation scenario is said to be
top-containing
if
\psi_{q}
is top-containing for every
q\in G
.

The next property we look at is candidate monotonicity
[
36
]
, which requires that for any group
N_{q}
, if a project selected by
\psi_{q}
is shifted forward in the preference of some voter in
N_{q}
, that project should continue to be selected by
\psi_{q}
.

Definition 6.11.63
.

A representative function
\psi_{q}:\mathcal{D}^{|N_{q}|}\to{2^{P}}
is said to satisfy candidate monotonicity if for any
\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{D}^{|N_{q}|}
,
p\in\psi_{q}(P)
, and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{|N_{q}|}
obtained by shifting
p
forward in some preference in
\mathcal{P}
, we have
p\in\psi_{q}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
. A representation scenario is said to satisfy
candidate monotonicity
if
\psi_{q}
satisfies candidate monotonicity for every
q\in G
.

Finally, we introduce the concept of pareto-efficiency, a well-known property that ensures no voter can be improved without causing a detriment to another voter. To formally define pareto-efficiency, we need to consider various methods through which a voter can compare two intervals of projects. We use the term ‘x-comparison’ to denote the specific way in which a voter compares intervals based on their preferences. The formal definition of pareto-efficiency is then established in relation to this x-comparison.

Definition 6.11.64
.

A representative function
\psi_{q}:\mathcal{D}^{|N_{q}|}\to{2^{P}}
is said to be pareto-efficient w.r.t. x-comparison if for every
i\in N_{q}
and every
\kappa_{q}
sized interval
I
of
P
, it holds that whenever
\psi_{q}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})
is preferred over
I
by voter
i
w.r.t. x-comparison, we have some voter
j\in N_{q}
who prefers
I
over
\psi_{q}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})
w.r.t. x-comparison. A representation scenario is said to be
pareto-efficient
if
\psi_{q}
is pareto-efficient for every
q\in G
.

We examine two types of x-comparisons: b-comparison (also called best comparison) and lb-comparison (also called lexicographic best comparison). In the case of b-comparison, a voter favors an interval
I
over another interval
I^{\prime}
if the highest-ranked project in
I
is at least as desirable as the highest-ranked project in
I^{\prime}
. If the highest-ranked projects in both intervals are the same, the voter considers both
I
and
I^{\prime}
to be equally preferred. On the other hand, lb-comparison operates similarly to b-comparison, except that two intervals cannot be considered equally preferred by a voter. Therefore, in the event of a tie between two intervals, the next highest-ranked projects in the intervals are compared.

Lemma 6.11.65
.

A representation scenario
\psi_{G}
is compliant with
\kappa_{G}
and pareto-efficient w.r.t. b-comparison or lb-comparison if and only if for every
q\in G
and
\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}\in\mathcal{D}^{|N_{q}|}
,
\psi_{q}
selects a
\kappa_{q}
-sized interval
I
such that
I\subseteq[\tau_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}),\tau_{|N_{q}|}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})]
(or
[\tau_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}),\tau_{|N_{q}|}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})]\subseteq I
).

Proof 6.11.66
.

(Only if:)
First, we will prove that for pareto-efficiency to hold one of the two conditions must necessarily be satisfied. For the sake of contradiction, assume that
\psi_{q}
selects an interval
I
at
\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}
such that
I
and
[\tau_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}),\tau_{|N_{q}|}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})]
are not contained in one another. This implies, exactly one of
\min\{I\}\triangleleft\tau_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})
and
\tau_{|N_{q}|}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})\triangleleft\max\{I\}
holds. We will prove that pareto-efficiency does not hold. First, suppose
\min\{I\}\triangleleft\tau_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})
and
\max\{I\}\trianglelefteq\tau_{|N_{q}|}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})
. If
\tau_{|N_{q}|}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})=\max\{I\}
,
[\tau_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}),\tau_{|N_{q}|}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})]\subseteq I
giving a contradiction. If
\max\{I\}\triangleleft\tau_{|N_{q}|}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})
, consider the interval
I^{\prime}
obtained by shifting
I
by one position towards
\tau_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})
. Clearly,
I
is preferred over
I^{\prime}
by any voter
i
w.r.t. both b-comparison and lb-comparison. Thus, pareto-efficiency does not hold. It can be argued similarly for the case with
\tau_{|N_{q}|}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})\triangleleft\max\{I\}
(we obtain
I^{\prime}
by shifting
I
by one position towards
\tau_{|N_{q}|}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})
).

(If:)
We now prove the sufficiency part. That is, we prove that if one of the two conditions is satisfied, then the representation scenario is pareto-efficient and compliant. Compliance with
\kappa_{G}
follows directly since
I
is
\kappa_{q}
sized interval. We will prove that pareto-efficiency is satisfied w.r.t. b-comparison and lb-comparison. If
I\subseteq[\tau_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}),\tau_{|N_{q}|}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})]
, shifting
I
towards
\tau_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})
will make it less preferable for voter having maximum top-ranked project. Shifting
I
towards
\tau_{|N_{q}|}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})
will make it less preferable for voter having minimum top-ranked project. Thus, it satisfies pareto-efficiency. Now, suppose
[\tau_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}),\tau_{|N_{q}|}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})]\subseteq I
. This implies that the top-ranked projects of all the voters in
N_{q}
are selected as representatives, thereby making
I
optimal. Thus, pareto-efficiency is satisfied.

Theorem 6.11.67
.

The following representation scenarios are compliant with
\kappa_{G}
, anonymous, top-containing, candidate monotone, and pareto-efficient w.r.t. b-comparison and lb-comparison: for every
q\in G
,

(R1)

\psi_{q}
takes a parameter
r\in[1,|N_{q}|]
and selects
\kappa_{q}
consecutive projects starting from
\tau_{r}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}})
.

(R2)

\psi_{q}
selects a
\kappa_{q}
sized interval that has maximum number of projects in
\{\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right):i\in N_{q}\}
(ties can be broken w.r.t. increasing order of the starting points of the intervals).

(R3)

\psi_{q}
selects a
\kappa_{q}
sized interval that maximizes the number of voters in
N_{q}
whose top ranked project is in the interval.

(R4)

\psi_{q}
selects
\kappa_{q}
consecutive projects starting from a project
p
that maximizes the number of voters in
N_{q}
who rank it at top (i.e.,
\argmaxaa\limits_{p\in P}{|\{i\in N_{q}:\operatorname{\succ_{i}\!}\left(1\right)=p\}|}
).

Proof 6.11.68
.

Anonymity and top-containingness follow from the definitions of the rules. Pareto-efficiency follows from
Lemma

6.11.65
. We now prove candidate monotonicity.

First, consider rule (R1) with parameter
r
. Suppose the outcome interval is
I
at
\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}
. Now a project
p\in I
is shifted forwards in the preference of a voter
i
to obtain
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{N_{q}}
. If
p
is
{(r-1)}^{\text{st}}
top-ranked project in
\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}
and
r^{\text{th}}
top-ranked project in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{N_{q}}
, outcome interval at
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{N_{q}}
starts from
p
and thus
p
continues to be selected. Else, the outcome at
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{N_{q}}
continues to be
I
and
p
hence continues to be selected.

Consider rule (R2). Let the outcome interval at
\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}
be
I
. A project
p\in I
is shifted forwards in the preference of a voter
i
to obtain
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{N_{q}}
. If at
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{N_{q}}
,
p\notin\{\operatorname{\succ\!}\left(1\right):i\in N_{q}\}
, then the outcomes at
\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{N_{q}}
remain the same. Similarly, if
p\in\{\operatorname{\succ\!}\left(1\right):i\in N_{q}\}
,
I
continues to win.

Consider rule (R3). Let the outcome interval at
\mathcal{P}_{N_{q}}
be
I
. Now a project
p\in I
is shifted forwards in the preference of a voter
i
to obtain
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{N_{q}}
. The number of voters who rank
p
at the top either increases by
1
or remains the same. For the sake of contradiction, assume that at
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{N_{q}}
, the number of voters having their top-ranked projects in
I
decreases. This implies some project in
I
has been shifted to second position from first, which in turn implies that the number of voters having their top-ranked project as
p
increases by
1
. Thus, the number of voters having their top-ranked project in
I
increases or remains the same.

The proof for (R4) is similar to that of (R1).

Note that all the above four representation scenarios give the output
\psi_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{1}})=\{p_{1}\}
and
\psi_{2}(\mathcal{P}_{N_{2}})=\{p_{2},p_{3}\}
in
Example

6.6.30
. Please note that these are only some of the representation scenarios compliant with
\kappa_{G}
and there can be many more such examples.

Note 6.11.69
.

When the groups are singleton (i.e., each group has exactly one voter), the representation scenario described in
Section

6.8
which chooses top
\kappa_{i}
projects of each voter
i
as her representatives is compliant with
\kappa_{N}
, anonymous, top-containing, pareto-efficient, and also candidate monotone (representation scenarios in
(R1)
and
(R4)
become equivalent to this since all groups are singleton).

6.12
Examples of Fair Rules

In this section, we present a few simple looking and easily understandable RSCRs that satisfy
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-strong-GEG under some specific conditions. All the RSCRs we mention in this section are expressed as convex combinations of DSCRs. Throughout this section, we denote
\min{\!\{\kappa_{G}\}}
by
\boldsymbol{\kappa_{min}}
and
\max{\!\{\eta_{G}\}}
by
\boldsymbol{\eta_{max}}
.

Case I:
\boldsymbol{\sum\limits_{q\in G}{\eta_{q}}\leq 1}
and
\boldsymbol{\kappa_{min}\geq\frac{m+1}{2}}

If the given condition is satisfied, for any
q\in G
,
\kappa_{q}\geq\frac{m+1}{2}
. Thus,
m-\kappa_{q}+1\leq\kappa_{q}
and
p_{m-\kappa_{q}+1}\trianglelefteq p_{\kappa_{q}}
. Now, we construct a RSCR that is unanimous, strategy-proof, group-wise anonymous, and satisfies
(\kappa_{G},\psi_{G},\eta_{G})
-strong-GEG. For each
q\in G
, we construct a group min-max rule
\phi_{q}
whose parameters are as follows:
\beta^{q}_{\underline{\gamma}}=a_{m}
,
\beta^{q}_{\overline{\gamma}}=a_{1}
, and for every other
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
\beta^{q}_{\gamma}\in[p_{m-\kappa_{q}+1},p_{\kappa_{q}}]
. During the construction, it also needs to be ensured that whenever
\gamma\gg\gamma^{\prime}
,
\beta^{q}_{\gamma}\trianglelefteq\beta^{q}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
. One trivial way to do this is to set all the parameters except
\beta^{q}_{\underline{\gamma}}
and
\beta^{q}_{\overline{\gamma}}
to the same project which is in
[p_{m-\kappa_{q}+1},p_{\kappa_{q}}]
. Clearly, there are many other trivial ways to achieve it (start setting a few parameters to
p_{\kappa_{q}}
and progressively move towards
p_{m-\kappa_{q}+1}
as the
\gamma
gets dominant).

Consider the RSCR
\sum_{q\in G}{\eta_{q}\phi_{q}}
. Take any
q,\gamma^{0},\gamma^{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\kappa_{q}},
and
p_{x}
as used in Theorem 5.6. Let
I=\{p_{x},\ldots,p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}\}
. Since
|I|=\kappa_{q}
,
\min\{I\}\trianglelefteq p_{m-\kappa_{q}+1}
and
p_{\kappa_{q}}\trianglelefteq\max\{I\}
. Since
m-\kappa_{q}+1\leq\kappa_{q}
and
p_{m-\kappa_{q}+1}\trianglelefteq p_{\kappa_{q}}
. Therefore,
[p_{m-\kappa_{q}+1},p_{\kappa_{q}}]\subseteq I
. By the construction of
\phi_{q}
, for any
\gamma
other than
\overline{\gamma}
and
\underline{\gamma}
,
\beta^{q}_{\gamma}\in[p_{m-\kappa_{q}+1},p_{\kappa_{q}}]
. Number of projects in
[p_{m-\kappa_{q}+1},p_{\kappa_{q}}]
is strictly lesser than
\kappa_{q}
. Therefore, among
\gamma^{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\kappa_{q}}
, at least two consecutive values should have the same parameter from
[p_{m-\kappa_{q}+1},p_{\kappa_{q}}]
. Let the value of this parameter be
p_{j}
. Clearly
j\geq x
since
x\leq m-\kappa_{q}+1
. Therefore, the condition in
Theorem

6.7.40
is satisfied for
t=j-x
.

Case II:
\boldsymbol{\lfloor\frac{m}{\kappa_{min}}\rfloor\cdot\eta_{max}\leq 1}

Let
r
be the reminder when
m
is divided by
\kappa_{min}
. That is,
r=m-\kappa_{min}\lfloor\frac{m}{\kappa_{min}}\rfloor
. We select any
d\in[\kappa_{min}-r+1,\kappa_{min}]
. For each
i\in[0,\lfloor\frac{m}{\kappa_{min}}\rfloor-1]
, we construct a group min-max rule
\phi_{i}
whose parameters are as follows:
\beta^{i}_{\underline{\gamma}}=p_{m}
,
\beta^{i}_{\overline{\gamma}}=p_{1}
, and for every other
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
\beta^{i}_{\gamma}=p_{d+i\kappa_{min}}
.

Consider the RSCR
\sum_{i}{\eta_{max}\phi_{i}}
. Take any
q,\gamma^{0},\gamma^{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\kappa_{q}},
and
p_{x}
as used in Theorem 5.6. Let
i=\lceil\frac{x}{\kappa_{min}}\rceil
.
Since
|\{p_{x},\ldots,p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}\}|=\kappa_{q}\geq\kappa_{min}
, by definition,
x\leq d+i\kappa_{min}\leq x+\kappa_{q}-1
. Let
d+i\kappa_{min}=x+t
where
t\in[0,\kappa_{q}-1]
. Clearly, the condition in
Theorem

6.7.40
is satisfied for this
t
.

Case III:
\boldsymbol{\eta_{max}\leq\frac{1}{n}}
,
\kappa_{min}<\frac{m+1}{2}
,
and
\boldsymbol{\psi_{G}}
is top-containing

Recall
Definition

6.11.62
. Let us define a function
s:\Gamma\to[0,n]
as
s(\gamma)=\sum_{q\in G}{\gamma_{q}}
. Clearly,
s(\gamma)=n
only when
\gamma=\overline{\gamma}
, and
s(\gamma)=0
only when
\gamma=\underline{\gamma}
. For each
i\in[1,n]
, we construct a group min-max rule
\phi_{i}
whose parameters are as follows:
\beta^{i}_{\underline{\gamma}}=p_{m}
,
\beta^{i}_{\overline{\gamma}}=p_{1}
, and for every other
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
\beta^{i}_{\gamma}\leq p_{\kappa_{min}}
if
s(\gamma)\geq i
and
\beta^{i}_{\gamma}\geq p_{m-\kappa_{min}+1}
otherwise. During this construction, it can be easily ensured that whenever
\gamma\gg\gamma^{\prime}
,
\beta^{q}_{\gamma}\trianglelefteq\beta^{q}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
(similar to Case I).

Consider the RSCR
\sum_{i}{\frac{1}{n}\phi_{i}}
. Take any
q,\gamma^{0},\gamma^{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\kappa_{q}},
and
p_{x}
as used in Theorem 5.6. Since
\kappa_{min}<\frac{m+1}{2}
,
p_{\kappa_{min}}\triangleleft p_{m-\kappa_{min}+1}
. We know that
[p_{x},p_{x+\kappa_{q}-1}]\cap[p_{\kappa_{min}},p_{m-\kappa_{min}+1}]\neq\emptyset
. Since
\psi_{q}
is top-containing,
\gamma^{\kappa_{q}}\neq\gamma^{0}
. Let
T=s(\gamma^{\kappa_{q}})
. Consider the DSCF
\varphi_{T}
. By construction,
\beta^{\varphi_{T}}_{\gamma^{\kappa_{q}}}\trianglelefteq p_{\kappa_{min}}
and
p_{m-\kappa_{min}+1}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{T}}_{\gamma^{0}}
. Since
\gamma^{\kappa_{q}}\gg\ldots\gg\gamma^{1}\gg\gamma^{0}
, we can conclude that there exits
t\in[0,\kappa_{q}-1]
such that
\beta^{\varphi_{T}}_{\gamma^{t+1}}\trianglelefteq p_{x+t}\trianglelefteq\beta^{\varphi_{T}}_{\gamma^{t}}
. Therefore, the condition in
Theorem

6.7.40
is satisfied for this
t
.

6.13
Conclusion and Discussion

This chapter studies the case where costs of the projects are totally flexible and each project can be allocated any amount. We normalize the budget to be
1
, thereby making our participatory budgeting problem equivalent to random social choice problem. A random social choice rule aggregates the preferences of voters and outputs a probability distribution over the projects as an outcome. Probability corresponding to each project may be interpreted as the fraction of budget allocated to the project. We propose fairness notions for both individuals and groups and characterize all random social choice rules that satisfy these properties in addition to other desiderata pursued in the literature.

We consider the model where there exists a natural partition of voters into groups based on attributes such as gender, race, economic status, and location. The goal is to be fair to each of these groups. We propose the notion of group-wise anonymity to ensure fairness within each group and the notions of weak-GEG and strong-GEG to ensure fairness across the groups. The proposed group-fairness notions are generalizations of existing individual-fairness notions and moreover provide non-trivial outcomes for strictly ordinal preferences, unlike the existing group-fairness notions. We characterize all the unanimous and strategy-proof social choice rules, both deterministic and random, that satisfy the three proposed notions. For the random rules, we give direct characterizations as well as extreme point characterizations (in which we express random rules as convex combinations of deterministic rules). We also give simpler characterizations for the special case where each group has only one voter and finally illustrate a few examples of families of fair rules under certain conditions.

It will be interesting to see how the rules satisfying weak-GEG and strong-GEG are structured in the domains beyond single-peakedness. Another promising question is to identify some tractable group-fair rules for cases that are not covered in
Section

6.12
.

Chapter 7
Summary and Future Directions

In this thesis, we studied participatory budgeting (PB), which is a voting paradigm to distribute a divisible resource called budget, among several projects, by aggregating the preferences of voters over the projects.

Preferences of voters could be elicited in two ways: (i) dichotomous preferences, where every voter approves/likes a subset of projects (ii) ordinal preferences, where every voter reports a strict/weak ranking of the projects. The amount that needs to be allocated to each project is referred to as its cost, and there are various approaches to imposing constraints on these costs: (i) the restricted cost model, which assigns a fixed cost value to each project, and (ii) the flexible cost model, which allows for multiple permissible cost values for each project.
