The contributions of this thesis are presented in two parts. In
Part

I
, we studied participatory budgeting under dichotomous preferences.
Chapter

3
in this part studies the restricted costs model, while the
Chapter

4
studies the flexible costs model. In
Part

II
of this thesis, we studied participatory budgeting under ordinal preferences.
Chapter

5
in this part studies the restricted costs model, while the
Chapter

6
studies the flexible costs model. In summary, this thesis introduced a multitude of fresh PB rules for the four considered PB models, along with a range of novel utility notions, axiomatic properties, and fairness notions.

7.1
Summary of the Contributions

7.1.1
Part

I
: Dichotomous Preferences

7.1.1.1
Chapter

3
: Restricted Costs: Egalitarian Participatory Budgeting

We introduced the egalitarian rule, Maxmin Participatory Budgeting (
\mathsf{MPB}
), that maximizes the egalitarian welfare and conducted a thorough computational and axiomatic analysis of the same.

On the computational front, we demonstrated that
\mathsf{MPB}
is strongly NP-hard and identified special cases where the problem becomes tractable. As a part of this, we introduced two novel parameters to study fixed parameter tractability of
\mathsf{MPB}
. We also proposed a LP-rounding based approximation algorithm and empirically proved that it gives optimal outcome in real-world. We proved that all these results carry over to minmax objective with minimal changes. We bounded the loss in approximation guarantee incurred due to strategy-proofness. On the axiomatic front, we analysed
\mathsf{MPB}
with respect to existing axioms and also the novel fairness axiom, maximal participation, introduced by us.

7.1.1.2
Chapter

4
: Flexible Costs: Welfare Maximization when Projects have Multiple Degrees of Sophistication

We studied PB under dichotomous preferences when the project costs are partially flexible. We introduced a generalization of dichotomous preferences, called ranged dichotomous preferences, where each voter approves a range of costs for each project. We generalized two utility notions defined for PB under restricted costs to our model. We also proposed two other utility notions unique to our model. We analyzed all the corresponding utilitarian welfare maximizing rules computationally and axiomatically.

Our computational part strengthened all the existing positive results, and also introduced several new parameterized tractability results (FPT, parameterized FPTAS) by introducing and studying novel parameters. On the axiomatic front, we introduced several axioms for our model with ranged approval votes and investigated their satisfiability by our utilitarian PB rules. We concluded that two of our rules,
R_{|S|}
and
R_{\|\;\|}
, exhibit remarkable axiomatic performance.

7.1.2
Part

II
: Ordinal Preferences

7.1.2.1
Chapter

5
:Restricted Costs: Welfare Maximization and Fairness under Incomplete Weakly Ordinal Preferences

We studied PB under incomplete weakly ordinal preferences and investigated utilitarian welfare maximization and fairness. For the welfare maximization, we extended the existing rules in the literature on dichotomous preferences and strictly ordinal preferences to propose a new family of rules called dichotomous translation rules and a rule called the PB-CC rule. We proved that our extensions perform as good as, and even better than, their parent rules. For the fairness, we identified some major drawbacks suffered by the existing fairness notions and proposed two families of rules, average rank guarantee rules and share guarantee rules, that fill this gap. We studied the computational complexity of these families of rules.

7.1.2.2
Chapter

6
: Flexible Costs: Characterization of Group-Fair and Individual-Fair Rules under Single-Peaked Preferences

We studied PB under strictly ordinal single-peaked preferences for the case where costs of the projects are totally flexible and each project can be allocated any amount. We normalize the budget to be
1
, thereby making our participatory budgeting problem equivalent to random social choice problem. A random social choice rule aggregates the preferences of voters and outputs a probability distribution over the projects as an outcome. Probability corresponding to each project may be interpreted as the fraction of budget allocated to the project. We assume that the voters are partitioned into groups and defined three novel group-fairness notions: (i) group-wise anonymity (ii) weak group entitlement guarantee (weak-GEG) and (iii) strong group entitlement guarantee (strong-GEG). We characterized all random social choice rules that satisfy these properties in addition to other desiderata pursued in the literature. We also studied a special case where every voter is considered to be a group in its own right, and characterized all the individually-fair rules.

7.2
Open Research Directions

7.2.1
Dichotomous Preferences and Restricted Costs

Utilitarian welfare and fairness objectives are well studied in the literature. This thesis studies the maxmin and minmax objectives for egalitarian welfare maximization. Going further, studying leximin objective is an interesting direction. Our focus also extends to addressing intractability through the use of FPT and the design of approximation algorithms. A promising avenue lies in identifying special structures within instances that render our objectives polynomial time tractable.

7.2.2
Dichotomous Preferences and Flexible Costs

This thesis initiated the investigation into utilitarian welfare maximization in the partially flexible costs model, marking the initial stride in this direction. Moving forward, exploring rules that optimize egalitarian welfare within this model holds significant potential. Investigating the impact of strategic agents is also a promising direction. Additionally, formulating novel fairness axioms that are relevant to this model and identifying participatory budgeting rules that satisfy these axioms present captivating avenues for further exploration.

7.2.3
Ordinal Preferences and Restricted Costs

This thesis introduced various families of PB rules under incomplete weakly ordinal preferences with the goals of maximizing utilitarian welfare and achieving fairness. Regarding utilitarian welfare, exploring new utility notions or approaching utility as a multi-objective problem hold promise as future directions. In terms of fairness, investigating tractable special cases with structured preferences would be interesting since the proposed ARSG rules were shown to be computationally hard. Additionally, studying manipulability of the proposed rules and egalitarian welfare optimization in the context of PB under incomplete weakly ordinal preferences are other avenues for future research.

7.2.4
Ordinal Preferences and Flexible Costs

This thesis provided characterizations of group-fair and individual-fair rules in the single-peaked domain. Exploring the behavior of these rules beyond the single-peaked domain poses an interesting avenue for future research. Another intriguing question arises when considering the possibility of relaxing the constraints of unanimity or strategy-proofness in characterizing fair rules. Finding tractable fair rules for scenarios not covered in this thesis would be highly valuable. Additionally, the pursuit of characterizing rules that maximize utilitarian welfare or egalitarian welfare presents an open and promising direction for further investigation.

Appendix

.3
Restricted Costs: Egalitarian Participatory Budgeting

.3.1
Minmax Objective

We define the disutility of a voter to be
b-\operatorname{u_{i}\!}\left(S\right)
. This notion effectively handles the scenarios where the voter, being a taxpayer and contributor to the budget, pays a fixed amount irrespective of the cost of its approval vote. The voter expects superior projects (which it believes are worth the tax paid) to be proposed and also to be selected. Unsatisfactory proposals or non-funded approved projects make a voter unhappy. In such a scenario, it is reasonable to assume that every dollar of the budget not used for approved projects causes disutility to the voter since it amounts to a waste.

The egalitarian goal now is to minimize the disutility of the voter with maximum disutility. From the objective function, it is clear that minimizing maximum disutility is equivalent to maximizing minimum utility. However, the approximation results do not transfer. To derive the approximation guarantee of our algorithm
Ordered-Relax
, we first formulate the ILP.

\displaystyle\min\;

\displaystyle q

subject to

\displaystyle q\geq b-\sum\limits_{p\in A_{i}}{\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)x_{p}}\quad\forall i\in N

(1)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{p\in P}{\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)x_{p}}\leq b

\displaystyle x_{p}\in\{0,1\}\quad\forall p\in P

(2)

\displaystyle q\geq 0

We consider the LP-relaxation for minimax objective by relaxing
Equation

2
to
0\leq x_{p}\leq 1
. Now, again consider the same
Ordered-Relax
algorithm: Solve the relaxed LP to get
(q^{*},x^{*})
. Let
S=\emptyset
be the initial outcome. Add the project with the highest value of
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)x_{p}^{*}
to
S
, followed by the one with the second highest value and so on till the next project does not fit.

Theorem .3.1
.

The algorithm
Ordered-Relax
achieves an approximation guarantee of
\left(\;2-\frac{1}{h_{o}}\right)
for
\mathsf{HCBP}
instances for the objective of minimizing the maximum disutility.

Proof .3.2
.

We obtain the below step following the steps similarly as in the proof of
Theorem

3.4.20
:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{p\in A_{i}}{\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)x_{p}^{*}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{b+\frac{|S|-|Y_{i}|}{|A_{i}|-|Y_{i}|}\;\operatorname{u_{i}\!}\left(S\right)}{\frac{|S|-|Y_{i}|}{|A_{i}|-|Y_{i}|}+1}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{b-\operatorname{u_{i}\!}\left(S\right)}{1+\left(\frac{|A_{i}|-|Y_{i}|}{|S|-|Y_{i}|}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq b-\sum\limits_{p\in A_{i}}{\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)x_{p}^{*}}

(3)

Let
j=\arg\max_{i}{(b-{\operatorname{u_{i}\!}\left(S\right)})}
and the maximum disutility achieved by
Ordered-Relax
be
ALG=b-{\operatorname{u_{j}\!}\left(S\right)}
. Let
\emph{OPT}\;
be the value of the maximum disutility in the optimal solution of
\mathsf{MPB}
objective. Since the optimal solution also belongs to the feasible region of the relaxed LP, we know that
q^{*}\leq\emph{OPT}
. From
Equation

1
, we know that
q^{*}\geq b-\sum\limits_{p\in A_{j}}{\operatorname{c\!}\left(p\right)x_{p}^{*}}
. Combining these, we have,

\displaystyle\emph{OPT}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{b-\operatorname{u_{i}\!}\left(S\right)}{1+\left(\frac{|A_{i}|-|Y_{i}|}{|S|-|Y_{i}|}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle ALG

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\left(\frac{|A_{i}|-|Y_{i}|}{|S|-|Y_{i}|}\right)\right)\emph{OPT}

\displaystyle\leq\left(2-\frac{|S|-|A_{j}|}{|S|-|Y_{j}|}\right)\emph{OPT}

Since
Y_{j}\subseteq S
,
|S|-|Y_{j}|\leq|S|\leq h_{o}
. Since the instance satisfies
\mathsf{HCBP}
,
|S|\geq l_{o}>h_{\mathcal{A}}\geq|A_{j}|
. Therefore
|S|>|A_{j}|
, which implies
|S|-|A_{j}|\geq 1
. Therefore,
\frac{|S|-|A_{j}|}{|S|-|Y_{j}|}\geq\frac{1}{h_{o}}
and
ALG\leq\left(2-\frac{1}{h_{o}}\right)\cdot\emph{OPT}

Note .3.3
.

It is worth highlighting that all the other results in
Chapter

3
, except
Theorem

3.4.28
, hold also for minmax objective, without changes. Axiomatic results and hardness results follow by their definition.
Section

3.4.2
also follows from the same proofs since the number of constraints or variables or the coefficients in the ILP remain same for both maxmin and minmax objectives.

.4
Restricted Costs: Welfare Maximization and Fairness under
Incomplete Weakly Ordinal Preferences

.4.1
Axiomatic Analysis of Dichotomous Translation Rules with Cost-Worthy Translation Scheme

Next, we look at the axiomatic analysis. Proofs for the axioms in
Section

5.3.3.3
follow without any changes.

Theorem .4.4
.

When
f(S)=|S|
, the rule
\langle CT,f\rangle
satisfies all the properties mentioned in
Table

5.1
except limit monotonicity. It satisfies limit monotonicity if and only if
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)<\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+2
.

Proof .4.5
.

1.

Splitting Monotonicity:
The proof is exactly the same as (a)-(i) in
Theorem

5.3.21
, except for the argument that for any
x_{1}\in X
, if
x\in A_{i}
in
\mathcal{I}
, then
x_{1}\in A_{i}
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. This follows for
CT
scheme since all the projects in
X
cost lesser than
x
. Thus, splitting monotonicity is satisfied.

2.

Discount Monotonicity:
This follows since the set of approved projects of each voter in both
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
remain the same. Thus, discount monotonicity is satisfied.

3.

Limit Monotonicity:
First, let us consider the case where
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)<\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+2
. Without loss of generality, assume that the first few entries in the worth vector are
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+1
and the remaining are
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)
. Consider the rule
\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle
. Suppose
f(S)=|S|
. Take an instance
\mathcal{I}
with budget
b
, and a project
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle,\mathcal{I}\right)
. Construct
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
by increasing the budget to
b+1
. Since
\mathcal{P}
and costs are unchanged, the score of any
S
is the same in both
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Consider any set
S^{\prime}\in\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
and any set
S_{x}\in\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
such that
x\in S_{x}
. For the sake of contradiction, assume
S_{x}\notin\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
. So, there exists
a\in S^{\prime}
and
b\in S_{x}
such that
score(a)>score(b)
. However, since
S_{x}\in\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
,
(S_{x}\setminus\{b\})\cup\{a\}
must not be feasible in
\mathcal{I}
(else, it would have strictly more utility than
S_{x}
). Hence,
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S_{x}\right)+\operatorname{c\!}\left(a\right)-\operatorname{c\!}\left(b\right)>b
. Since
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S_{x}\right)\leq b
, this implies,
\operatorname{c\!}\left(b\right)<\operatorname{c\!}\left(a\right)
. The score of any project whose cost is at most
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)
is
n
. The score of any project whose cost is greater than
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+1
is
0
. The score of any project whose cost is exactly
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+1
will belong to
[0,n]
. Since
score(a)>score(b)
, one of these holds: (i)
\operatorname{c\!}\left(a\right)\leq\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)
and
\operatorname{c\!}\left(b\right)\geq\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+1
(ii)
\operatorname{c\!}\left(a\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+1
and
\operatorname{c\!}\left(b\right)\geq\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+1
. That is,
\operatorname{c\!}\left(a\right)\leq\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+1
and
\operatorname{c\!}\left(b\right)\geq\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+1
. This contradicts
\operatorname{c\!}\left(b\right)<\operatorname{c\!}\left(a\right)
. Thus, limit monotonicity is satisfied.

Now, let us look at the case where
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)\geq\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+2
. Construct an instance
\mathcal{I}
as follows: budget
b=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)+\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(2\right)-1
, set of projects
P=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\}
, two voters have rankings
p_{1}\succ\ldots\succ p_{m}
and
p_{1}\succ p_{2}\succ p_{m}\succ p_{4}\ldots\succ p_{m-1}\succ p_{3}
respectively. Set
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{i}\right)
as
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(i\right)
if
i\in\{1,2\}
and as
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(i\right)+1
otherwise. Note that no project in
P
costs exactly
b+1
. This is because, for any project
p_{i}
such that
i\notin\{1,2\}
,
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{i}\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(i\right)+1
. Assume that this is equal to
b+1
, i.e.,
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)+\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(2\right)-\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(i\right)=1
. Since
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(2\right)\geq\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(i\right)\geq 0
, this implies
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)=1
. This contradicts
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)\geq\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+2
. Also, note that
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
have a score of
2
each while
p_{m}
might have a score of
1
. All other projects have a score of
0
.

Consider the set
\{p_{2},p_{m}\}
. We know that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(\{p_{2},p_{m}\}\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(2\right)+\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+1
. Since
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)\leq\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)-2
,
\operatorname{c\!}\left(\{p_{2},p_{m}\}\right)\leq b
. Since
\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
is infeasible, score of
\{p_{2},p_{m}\}
is optimal. Therefore,
\{p_{2},p_{m}\}\in\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
and
p_{m}\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle,\mathcal{I}\right)
. Now, construct an instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
by increasing
b
to
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)+\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(2\right)
. Now,
\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
is feasible and has the optimal maximum score of
4
. Hence,
p_{m}\notin\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
and limit monotonicity is not satisfied.

4.

Inclusion Maximality:
This follows from the fact that
|S|
is subset monotone. In other words, utility from a set
S
is at least as much as the utility from a subset of
S
. Thus, inclusion maximality is satisfied.

5.

Candidate Monotonicity:
Depending on
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(j\right)
,
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(j-1\right)
,
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)
, and
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x^{\prime}\right)
,
x
will be approved by the same voters and possibly by one more voter. Likewise,
x
will be approved by the same number of voters or possibly by exactly one voter less than that number. The scores of all other projects will remain the same in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Since
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle,\mathcal{I}\right)
, there exists
S_{x}\in\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
such that
x\in S_{x}
. By definition, the utility of any set is the sum of scores of all projects in that set. Hence, some set containing
x
will continue to win. Therefore,
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
. Thus, candidate monotonicity is satisfied.

6.

Non-Crossing Monotoncity:
The proof is exactly same as that of
Theorem

5.3.38
.

7.

Pro-Affordability:
Proof is exactly same as that of (a)-(ii) in the proof of
Theorem

5.3.49
.

This completes the proof.

Theorem .4.6
.

When
f(S)=c(S)
, the rule
\langle CT,f\rangle
satisfies all the properties mentioned in
Table

5.1
except discount monotonicity, limit monotonicity, and pro-affordability. It satisfies limit monotonicity and pro-affordability if and only if
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)\leq 1
.

Proof .4.7
.

1.

Splitting Monotonicity:
The proof is exactly the same as (a)-(ii) in
Theorem

5.3.21
, except for the argument that for any
x_{1}\in X
, if
x\in A_{i}
in
\mathcal{I}
, then
x_{1}\in A_{i}
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. This follows for
CT
scheme since all the projects in
X
cost lesser than
x
. Thus, splitting monotonicity is satisfied.

2.

Discount Monotonicity:
Consider an instance
\mathcal{I}
with budget
b=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)
, projects
P=\{p_{1},p_{2},d_{1},\ldots,d_{m-2}\}
such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{1}\right)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{2}\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)
and for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m-2\}

\operatorname{c\!}\left(d_{i}\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(i+2\right)+1
. Let there be two voters with preferences
p_{1}\succ_{1}p_{2}\succ_{1}d_{1}\succ_{1}\ldots\succ_{1}d_{m-2}
and
p_{2}\succ_{2}p_{1}\succ_{1}d_{1}\succ_{1}\ldots\succ_{1}d_{m-2}
respectively. Then,
\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle,\mathcal{I}\right)=\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
. However, if
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{1}\right)
is reduced by
1
,
\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)=\{\{p_{2}\}\}
and
p_{1}\notin\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
. Thus, discount monotonicity is not satisfied.

3.

Limit Monotonicity:
First, we look at the case where
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)\leq 1
. The translation scheme selects only unit cost projects into each
A_{i}
and increasing
b
won’t change it.

Finally, we look at the case where
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)>1
. Consider an instance
\mathcal{I}
with
b=2\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)-2
. Say there are three projects
\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
respectively costing
\{\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right),\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)-1,1\}
. Say there are
m-3
projects
d_{1},\ldots,d_{m-3}
such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(d_{i}\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(i+1\right)+1
. Say there is only one voter and her ranking is
\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}\succ d_{1}\succ\ldots\succ d_{m-3}
. Then,
\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)=\{\{p_{1},p_{3}\}\}
. If the budget is increased by
1
to get
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,
\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)=\{\{p_{1},p_{2}\}\}
. Hence,
p_{3}\notin\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
. Thus, limit monotonicity is not satisfied.

4.

Inclusion Maximality:
This follows from the fact that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(S\right)
is subset monotone. In other words, utility from a set
S
is at least as much as the utility from any subset of
S
. Thus, inclusion maximality is satisfied.

5.

Candidate Monotonicity:
Depending on
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(j\right)
,
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(j-1\right)
,
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x\right)
, and
\operatorname{c\!}\left(x^{\prime}\right)
,
x
will be approved by the same voters and possibly by one more voter. Likewise,
x
will be approved by the same number of voters or possibly by exactly one voter less than that number. The scores of all other projects will remain the same in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Since
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle,\mathcal{I}\right)
, there exists
S_{x}\in\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
such that
x\in S_{x}
. By definition, the utility of any set is the sum of scores of all projects in that set. Hence, some set containing
x
will continue to win. Therefore,
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
. Thus, candidate monotonicity is satisfied.

6.

Non-Crossing Monotoncity:
The proof is exactly same as that of
Theorem

5.3.38
.

7.

Pro-Affordability:
The case of
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)\leq 1
is trivial since all the approved projects for each voter cost at most
1
and there cannot be any project whose cost is lesser than
1
.

Finally, consider the case where
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)>1
. Take any arbitrary voter
i
. If
\operatorname{r_{i}\!}\left(x^{\prime}\right)<\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S_{x}\right)\leq\operatorname{r_{i}\!}\left(x\right)
, then
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S^{\prime}\right)<\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S_{x}\right)
. Else,
\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S^{\prime}\right)=\operatorname{y_{i}\!}\left(S_{x}\right)
. Hence, utility of
S^{\prime}
is at least that of
S_{x}
. Since
S_{x}\in\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
,
S^{\prime}\in\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
.

This completes the proof.

Theorem .4.8
.

When
f(S)=\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
, the rule
\langle CT,f\rangle
satisfies all the properties mentioned in
Table

5.1
except limit monotonicity and non-crossing monotonicity. It satisfies limit monotonicity if and only if
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)<\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+2
and non-crossing monotonicity if and only if
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)
.

Proof .4.9
.

1.

Splitting Monotonicity:
The proof is exactly the same as (a)-(iii) in
Theorem

5.3.21
, except for the argument that for any
x_{1}\in X
, if
x\in A_{i}
in
\mathcal{I}
, then
x_{1}\in A_{i}
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. This follows for
CT
scheme since all the projects in
X
cost lesser than
x
. Thus, splitting monotonicity is satisfied.

2.

Discount Monotonicity:
This follows since the set of approved projects of each voter in both
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
remain the same. Thus, discount monotonicity is satisfied.

3.

Limit Monotonicity:
First, let us consider the case where
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)<\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+2
. Without loss of generality, assume that the first few entries in the worth vector are
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+1
and the remaining are
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)
. Consider the rule
\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle
. Suppose
f(S)=\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
. If there is any project costing at most
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)
, it covers all the voters and the claim follows. If
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
selects only projects costing
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+1
into each
A_{i}
, increasing
b
won’t change the outcome. Thus, limit monotonicity is satisfied.

Now, let us look at the case where
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)\geq\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)+2
. Suppose we have an instance
\mathcal{I}
with
b=2\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)-1
. Let
\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
be projects respectively costing
\{\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)-1,\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right),\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)\}
. Let
\{d_{1},\ldots,d_{m-3}\}
be projects such that
\operatorname{c\!}\left(d_{i}\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(i+1\right)+1
. Let there be six voters such that the ranking of one voter is
p_{1}\succ d_{1}\succ\ldots\succ d_{m-3}\succ p_{2}\succ p_{3}
, ranking of two voters is
p_{2}\succ d_{1}\succ\ldots\succ d_{m-3}\succ p_{3}\succ p_{1}
, and ranking of remaining three voters is
p_{3}\succ d_{1}\succ\ldots\succ d_{m-3}\succ p_{2}\succ p_{1}
. Clearly,
\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)=\{\{p_{1},p_{3}\}\}
. If we increased budget by
1
to get
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
, then
\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)=\{\{p_{2},p_{3}\}\}
. Thus,
p_{1}\notin\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
and limit monotonicity is not satisfied.

4.

Inclusion Maximality:
This follows from the fact that
\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
is subset monotone. In other words, utility from a set
S
is at least as much as the utility from any subset of
S
. Thus, inclusion maximality is satisfied.

5.

Candidate Monotoncity:
Utility of a set
S
is the number of voters who have some project of
S\in A_{i}
. Utility of any
S_{x}\in\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}\right)
increases by
1
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
(when
x
is the only project in
S_{x}
in
A_{i}
) or remains the same, otherwise. Similarly, the utility of any set without
x
stays the same or decreases by
1
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Therefore,
x\in\operatorname{\Phi\!}\left(\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle,\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
. Thus, candidate monotonicity is satisfied.

6.

Non-Crossing Monotonicity:
First, we look at the case where
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)
. Let
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)=Q
. All the voters approve all and only the projects whose cost is at most
Q
. Hence, the outcome is independent of the ranks of the projects in the preferences and thus, non-crossing monotonicity is satisfied.

Now, consider the case where
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)>\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(m\right)
. This implies that there exists some
t\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
such that
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(t\right)>\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(t+1\right)
. Consider an instance
\mathcal{I}
with budget
b=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)+\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(t\right)
and a set of projects
P=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{5},d_{1},\ldots,d_{m-5}\}
whose costs are as follows:
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{1}\right)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{4}\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)
;
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{2}\right)=\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{3}\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(t\right)
;
\operatorname{c\!}\left(p_{5}\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(1\right)+\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(t\right)
; for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,t-2\}

\operatorname{c\!}\left(d_{i}\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(i+1\right)+1
; for every
i\in\{t-1,\ldots,m-5\}
,
\operatorname{c\!}\left(d_{i}\right)=\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(i+3\right)+1
. Suppose we have three voters whose preferences are (1)
p_{1}\succ d_{1}\succ\ldots\succ d_{t-2}\succ p_{2}\succ p_{3}\succ d_{t-1}\succ\ldots\succ d_{m-5}\succ p_{5}\succ p_{4}
(2)
p_{4}\succ d_{1}\succ\ldots\succ d_{t-2}\succ p_{2}\succ p_{3}\succ d_{t-1}\succ\ldots\succ d_{m-5}\succ p_{5}\succ p_{1}
and (3)
p_{5}\succ d_{1}\succ\ldots\succ d_{t-2}\succ p_{3}\succ p_{2}\succ d_{t-1}\succ\ldots\succ d_{m-5}\succ p_{4}\succ p_{1}
. Since
\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(t\right)>\operatorname{\alpha\!}\left(t+1\right)
, Algorithm
2
gives
A_{1}=\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
,
A_{2}=\{p_{4},p_{2}\}
,
A_{3}
is either
\{p_{5},p_{3}\}
or
\{p_{3}\}
. Hence, the outcome of
\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle
with
f(S)=\mathds{1}(|S|>0)
is
\{\{p_{1},p_{2}\},\{p_{1},p_{3}\},\{p_{4},p_{2}\},\{p_{4},p_{3}\}\}
since each of these sets has an utility of exactly
2
. Now, let
S=\{p_{1},p_{3}\}
. See that
p_{2}\notin S
. Exchange
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
in the first preference to obtain new instance
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. In
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
,
\{p_{3},p_{4}\}
has a strictly better utility than
S
and hence
S\notin\operatorname{\langle\mathcal{C}_{\alpha},f\rangle\!}\left(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\right)
. Thus, non-crossing monotonicity is not satisfied.

7.

Pro-Affordability:
Proof is exactly same as that of (a)-(ii) in the proof of
Theorem

5.3.49
.

This completes the proof.

.5
Flexible Costs: Characterization of Group-Fair and Individual-Fair Rules under Single-Peaked Preferences

.5.1
Proof of
Lemma

6.7.37

Take a cut
C
. Note that if
C
contains either
x
or
y
then
c(C)\geq 1
. So, assume
C
does not contain
x
and
y
. We show that there must exist
\gamma_{i_{1}},\ldots,\gamma_{i_{m}}
such that
\gamma_{i_{j}}\gg\gamma_{i_{j+1}}
for all
j\in 1,\ldots,m-1
, and
(\gamma_{i_{j}},j)\in C
. First note that for each
j\leq m
, there exists some
(\gamma_{i_{j}},j)\in C
as otherwise we can construct a deterministic rule
f
where
f_{rj}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma_{i}})=1
for all
\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma
. Hence,
C
can be written in the form

C=\{(\gamma_{1,1},1),\ldots,(\gamma_{1,k_{1}},1),(\gamma_{2,1},2),\ldots,(\gamma_{2,k_{2}},2),\ldots,(\gamma_{m,1},m),\ldots,(\gamma_{m,k_{m}},m)\}.

Let us define the deterministic rule
f
as follows. Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
. If there are
(\gamma_{i_{m-1}},m-1),\ldots,(\gamma_{i_{1}},1)\in C
such that
\gamma_{i_{1}}\gg\cdots\gg\gamma_{i_{m-1}}\gg\gamma
then
f(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma})=p_{m}
. Otherwise, if there are
(\gamma_{i_{m-2}},m-1),\ldots,(\gamma_{i_{1}},1)\in C
such that
\gamma_{i_{1}}\gg\cdots\gg\gamma_{i_{m-2}}\gg\gamma
then
f(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma})=p_{m-1}
. Continuing in this manner, if there are
(\gamma_{i_{1}},1)\in C
such that
\gamma_{i_{1}}\gg\gamma
then
f(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma})=p_{2}
. Finally, for all the remaining cases, let us define
f(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma})=p_{1}
.

It follows from the definition that
f
is strategy-proof. Therefore, the path in the network induced by
f
must intersect
C
. Let the vertex where
f
intersects the cut be
(\gamma,k)
, that is,
f(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma})=p_{k}
. Suppose
k<m
. Then by definition, there are
(\gamma_{i_{k-1}},k-1),\ldots,(\gamma_{i_{1}},1)\in C
such that
\gamma_{i_{1}}\gg\cdots\gg\gamma_{i_{k-1}}\gg\gamma
. Since
(\gamma,k)\in C
, this implies
\gamma_{i_{1}}\gg\cdots\gg\gamma_{i_{k-1}}\gg\gamma_{i_{k}}=\gamma
, which in turn means
f(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma})\neq a_{k}
. Therefore, it follows that
k=m
, and hence
\gamma_{i_{1}}\gg\cdots\gg\gamma_{i_{m-1}}\gg\gamma_{i_{m}}=\gamma
.

Consider
\gamma_{i_{1}},\cdots,\gamma_{i_{m-1}},\gamma_{i_{m}}
as defined in the preceding paragraph. By strategy-proofness,
\varphi_{p_{1}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma}_{i_{1}})\geq\varphi_{p_{1}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma}_{i_{2}})
. Therefore,

c(\gamma_{i_{1}},1)+c(\gamma_{i_{2}},2)\geq\varphi_{p_{1}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma}_{i_{2}})+\varphi_{p_{2}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma}_{i_{2}}).

By induction, it follows that

c(\gamma_{i_{1}},1)+\ldots+c(\gamma_{i_{k+1}},k+1)\geq\varphi_{p_{1}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma}_{i_{k+1}})+\ldots+\varphi_{p_{k+1}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma}_{i_{k+1}}).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle c(C)

\displaystyle\geq c(\gamma_{i_{1}},1)+\ldots+c(\gamma_{i_{m}},m)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{p_{1}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma}_{i_{m}})+\ldots+\varphi_{p_{m}}(\mathcal{P}^{\gamma}_{i_{m}})=1.

This completes the proof.
